  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Both punishment and reward systems, when present, can increase short-term cooperation (especially when sanctions are moderate rather than extreme), but their removal leads to a significant decline in trust, and in some cases, a decline in cooperation. Strong sanctions do not increase cooperation above control, and their removal reduces cooperation. Weaker sanctions increase cooperation while present, but their removal does not immediately reduce cooperation, though trust is still undermined. The negative effects on trust are similar for both punishment and reward. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so findings must be interpreted through the lens of contribution/cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the presence and removal of punishment (and reward) systems can have complex effects on cooperation, which is a behavioral proxy for efficiency but not a direct measure. Strong punishment does not increase cooperation above control, and its removal reduces cooperation. Weaker punishment increases cooperation while present, but its removal does not immediately reduce cooperation. The undermining of trust by sanctions is robust, regardless of sanction strength. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on the strength of the sanction and the baseline level of cooperation, but direct efficiency outcomes are not reported. Use caution when extrapolating to efficiency, as only contribution rates are measured.

- source: Krieg, J., & Samek, A. (2017). When charities compete: A laboratory experiment with simultaneous public goods. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that monetary incentives (bonus) increase contributions to the treated public good and, in early rounds, to the untreated public good, resulting in a higher total sum of contributions (i.e., an expanded 'charitable pie'). Recognition and non-monetary sanctions do not significantly increase contributions in the treated game, and recognition leads to a decrease in contributions to the untreated game in later rounds, consistent with expenditure substitution. Sanctions are actively used but do not increase contributions. The findings suggest that monetary incentives are more effective than social incentives in increasing total contributions when multiple public goods are present. However, the study does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff, so implications for efficiency must be inferred from contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effect of non-monetary sanctions (costless approval/disapproval) in a simultaneous public goods environment. In this design, punishment is costless and does not affect payoffs, and it does not increase contributions or the sum of contributions. Thus, in similar environments (simultaneous PGGs, costless non-monetary punishment, no chat, n=4, 20 rounds, MPCR=0.4), enabling this form of punishment is unlikely to increase efficiency or group payoff. The paper does not test costly punishment, so its results are most relevant for costless, non-monetary sanctions. The findings also suggest that the presence of alternative opportunities for prosocial behavior (multiple simultaneous PGGs) may reduce the effectiveness of social incentives. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that costless, non-monetary punishment will not improve efficiency in simultaneous PGGs with these parameters.

- source: Gürerk, Irlenbusch, B., & Rockenbach, B. (2014). On cooperation in open communities. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that when subjects can self-select into a punishment community (VF-PUN), average contributions are much higher than in a reward community (VF-REW) or in exogenously assigned punishment communities (EX-PUN, FIX-PUN). The effect is attributed to self-selection rather than slow growth of the community. The presence of a punishment option is critical for high cooperation, and adding reward does not further improve outcomes. However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff; all main results are in terms of contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that self-selection into punishment-enabled public goods games leads to higher cooperation (contribution) rates than exogenous assignment or reward-based institutions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher contributions likely imply higher efficiency, but actual efficiency or group payoff is not reported. The design details (player count, rounds, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, so the paper is useful for mapping design to expected cooperation, but not for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Skatova, A., & Ferguson, E. (2013). Individual differences in behavioural inhibition explain free riding in public good games when punishment is expected but not implemented. *Behavioral and Brain Functions*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both the implementation and the expectation of punishment (even when not actually implemented) increase individual contributions in a one-shot PGG compared to standard PGGs without punishment. Implemented punishment leads to higher contributions than non-implemented punishment. Individual differences in BIS-anxiety (a measure of behavioral inhibition) predict higher contributions and less free riding when punishment is expected but not implemented. FFFS (flight, fight, freeze system) does not predict contributions. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution levels, free riding), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both implemented and expected (but not implemented) centralized punishment increase contributions in a one-shot PGG, with implemented punishment having a stronger effect. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes due to punishment. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in contribution rates under different punishment regimes and for understanding the role of individual differences in behavioral inhibition. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling punishment (even if not always implemented) increases cooperation, but the magnitude of efficiency gains cannot be directly inferred from the reported outcomes.

- source: Villatoro, D., Andrighetto, G., Brandts, J., Nardin, L. G., Sabater-Mir, J., & Conte, R. (2014). The Norm-Signaling Effects of Group Punishment: Combining Agent-Based Simulation and Laboratory Experiments. *Social Science Computer Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when punishment is inflicted by the entire group, human subjects are more likely to cooperate (i.e., contribute to the public good) than when punishment is uncoordinated, even though the material cost to defectors is held constant. The authors attribute this to the norm-signaling function of group punishment, which is hypothesized to activate both instrumental and normative motivations for compliance. Agent-based simulations support the idea that norm salience, as modeled in the EMIL-I-A architecture, can explain the observed human behavior. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (contribution rates).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of punishment (specifically, whether it is coordinated by the group or uncoordinated) can significantly affect cooperation rates in public goods games, even when the material incentives are held constant. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper suggests that group punishment may lead to higher efficiency than uncoordinated punishment, but this is inferred from contribution rates rather than directly measured. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to calibrate quantitative predictions of efficiency gains from punishment. The findings are most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of group punishment structure and norm-signaling on cooperation, which may be relevant for predicting efficiency when combined with other sources.

- source: Bortolotti, S., Casari, M., & Pancotto, F. (2015). NORMS OF PUNISHMENT: EXPERIMENTS WITH STUDENTS AND THE GENERAL POPULATION. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The opportunity for peer punishment increases cooperation among students but not among the general population. In the student sample, average contributions rise substantially when punishment is available (from 6.8 to 12.8 out of 20), while in the representative sample, contributions are slightly lower with punishment (from 9.1 to 8.9). The general population punishes as much or more than students, but punishment is more often directed at cooperators (antisocial punishment), and recipients are less responsive to punishment. As a result, punishment fails to increase cooperation or efficiency in the general population. Earnings per period decrease with punishment in both groups, but more so in the representative sample. The study highlights that the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency is highly dependent on the subject pool, with punishment only improving cooperation (and likely efficiency) among students.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated PGGs depends critically on the subject pool. In a standard 4-player, 8-round PGG with MPCR=0.5 and a punishment technology of 1 token cost to reduce 4 tokens from another, punishment increases cooperation (and likely efficiency) among students but not among a representative general population. The main moderator is the prevalence of antisocial punishment and the responsiveness to punishment. For prediction, if the game is run with a student sample, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency; with a general population sample, punishment may not improve and could even reduce efficiency due to antisocial punishment and lack of response. The paper does not report efficiency directly, but the pattern of contributions and earnings suggests these conclusions.

- source: Zhou, X. Y., Alysandratos, T., & Naef, M. (2023). Rice Farming and the Origins of Cooperative Behaviour. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that (1) participants from wetland rice regions contribute more in both the no-punishment and punishment conditions of a repeated PGG, and (2) participants from rice regions assign more punishment points to free riders than those from non-rice regions. The difference in contribution is larger in the punishment condition. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined. The results are robust to a wide range of controls and IV strategies, and are interpreted as evidence of persistent cultural norms of cooperation and punishment rooted in historical rice farming.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a punishment option increases contribution rates in a repeated PGG, and that the magnitude of this effect is moderated by cultural background (rice vs. non-rice regions). However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are useful for predicting contribution behavior under different cultural backgrounds, but not for direct efficiency prediction as required by the downstream task.

- source: Nelissen, R. M. A., & Mulder, L. B. (2013). What makes a sanction stick? The effects of financial and social sanctions on norm compliance. *Social Influence*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both financial and social sanctions increase cooperation (measured as average contribution) in a repeated public goods game compared to a no-sanction control. Financial sanctions produce higher contributions than social sanctions while the sanctioning opportunity is present. However, after the removal of sanctions, contributions drop much more in the financial sanction condition than in the social sanction or control conditions. Social sanctions thus create more persistent cooperation norms. The study does not report group payoff or efficiency, but the contribution results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency effects in similar game designs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both financial and social punishment mechanisms increase cooperation in a standard PGG, and that the effect is larger for financial punishment while the mechanism is active. However, the persistence of cooperation after punishment is removed is greater for social sanctions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment will be positive for both financial and social punishment, but the durability of the effect may depend on the type of punishment. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff reporting means that predictions must be inferred from contribution rates, but the mapping is straightforward in standard PGGs. The paper's design and results are highly informative for settings with 4 players, continuous contributions, no communication, and known MPCR.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2020). Heterogeneous groups cooperate in public good problems despite normative disagreements about individual contribution levels. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, in a linear public goods game with peer punishment and heterogeneous returns, experimentally induced normative disagreement within groups does not reduce cooperation (measured by average contributions) or increase punishment. Instead, the average normative view within a group predicts the group's contribution level. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but notes that joint payoffs are maximized at full contribution, and that observed contribution levels are lower than this maximum, consistent with underprovision. The presence of punishment maintains cooperation at moderate levels, but does not eliminate the effect of group-mean norms. The study does not include a no-punishment control, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the effect of normative disagreement in a linear PGG with peer punishment, but does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, nor does it include a no-punishment control. For prediction tasks, it shows that, in a 3-player, 10-round, continuous-contribution PGG with punishment (cost 1, impact 3), with full information about others' contributions and payoffs but anonymous punishment, group-mean normative views are a strong predictor of contribution levels. The presence of punishment maintains moderate cooperation, but the paper does not allow direct estimation of the efficiency gain from enabling punishment versus disabling it. The results suggest that, under these parameters, punishment supports cooperation even in the presence of normative disagreement, but the efficiency effect relative to no-punishment is not measured.

- source: Molleman, L., Kölle, F., Starmer, C., & Gächter, S. (2019). People prefer coordinated punishment in cooperative interactions. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that in a one-shot, three-player binary public goods game with a costly punishment stage, a substantial fraction of participants prefer to punish free riders only if another group member also punishes (coordinated punishment). This preference is especially prevalent among those who do not punish unconditionally. The presence of coordinated punishment is robust across two large samples and is not correlated with conditional cooperation preferences. The study provides detailed behavioral evidence on how punishment decisions are conditioned on others' actions, but does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the behavioral mechanisms of punishment in public goods games, especially the prevalence and motivations for coordinated punishment. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, nor does it compare efficiency between punishment-enabled and punishment-disabled conditions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the structure of punishment (e.g., whether it is coordinated) may matter, but does not provide quantitative or comparative efficiency data. The game design dimensions are well specified, which may help in mapping to other studies that do report efficiency outcomes.

- source: Mischkowski, D., Glöckner, A., & Lewisch, P. (2018). From spontaneous cooperation to spontaneous punishment - Distinguishing the underlying motives driving spontaneous behavior in first and second order public good games. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in both one-shot and iterated public goods games with a punishment option, individuals who decide to punish quickly invest more in punishment, especially if they contributed more than their peers and feel highly upset. The spontaneous punishment effect is not moderated by dispositional pro-sociality (SVO), but by situational factors (above-average contribution and negative affect). The study does not analyze or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, so it does not provide evidence on whether punishment increases or decreases efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed evidence on the psychological and situational determinants of punishment behavior in public goods games, but does not report or analyze efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Therefore, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. However, it does confirm that punishment is used more by above-average contributors and those experiencing negative affect, and that the cost-to-impact ratio of punishment is 1:4. The game design is a standard PGG with continuous contributions, four players, two rounds (Study 1) or one round (Study 2), no chat, and full information about others' contributions. The absence of efficiency or payoff analysis limits the paper's direct utility for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Albrecht, F., Kube, S., & Traxler, C. (2018). Cooperation and norm enforcement - The individual-level perspective. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds substantial heterogeneity in both cooperation and punishment patterns. About half of subjects are pro-social punishers, and about 40% are non-punishers. There is only a moderate correlation between cooperation and punishment types: many conditional cooperators do not punish, and some free-riders do punish pro-socially. In repeated public goods games with punishment, groups with more pro-social punishers achieve higher and more stable contribution levels, even after controlling for the number of conditional cooperators. Regression analyses show that the number of pro-social punishers in a group is a stronger predictor of group contribution levels than the number of conditional cooperators. However, the paper does not report group payoffs, efficiency, or welfare, so the effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in linear public goods games with punishment, the presence of pro-social punishers in a group increases contribution levels. This suggests that enabling punishment can increase cooperation, but the paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoffs. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is informative about behavioral mechanisms (contributions and punishment) but not about the net payoff effects of punishment. The game design dimensions are well specified, and the experimental setup matches the canonical PGG with punishment, so the findings are highly relevant for understanding how group composition and punishment opportunities affect cooperation rates, but not directly efficiency.

- source: Engel, C., Kube, S., & Kurschilgen, M. (2021). Managing expectations: How selective information affects cooperation and punishment in social dilemma games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Selective pre-play information about others' cooperativeness can strongly affect initial beliefs, contribution behavior, and the effectiveness of punishment in repeated public goods games with punishment and counter-punishment. Unfavorable information (showing low cooperation in other groups) leads to lower initial and sustained contributions, and reduces the ability of punishment to increase cooperation. Favorable information has little effect in already cooperative environments (ceiling effect), but in less cooperative environments it can prevent further decline in cooperation and enhance the positive effect of pro-social punishment. The effect of information does not depend on whether it is public or private, but is strongest when the information is surprising to participants. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the strong effects on contribution rates imply likely effects on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing cooperation (and thus likely efficiency) is moderated by participants' expectations, which can be manipulated by selective pre-play information. In environments where baseline cooperation is high, punishment is effective unless unfavorable information is provided, which undermines both cooperation and the effectiveness of punishment. In less cooperative environments, favorable information can help maintain cooperation and enhance the effect of pro-social punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the baseline expectations and informational context (not just structural game parameters) are important moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, predictions must be inferred from contribution data.

- source: Kocher, M., Martinsson, P., & Visser, M. (2012). Social background, cooperative behavior, and norm enforcement. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper reports on one-shot public goods games with and without punishment, conducted in four South African high schools representing different socio-economic and cultural backgrounds. The introduction of punishment increased contributions significantly only in the white community, and only modestly or insignificantly in the others. Punishment was more frequent and severe in the African communities, leading to lower average earnings in the punishment condition for those groups. The study finds that social background variables (such as trust and household violence) explain much of the variation in cooperation and punishment. Overall, the efficiency impact of punishment is mixed: in some groups, punishment increases contributions but also reduces earnings due to the cost of punishment, sometimes resulting in no net efficiency gain.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency in one-shot, three-player public goods games with MPCR=0.5 and a punishment technology of 1:3 (cost:impact) is highly context-dependent. In groups with low baseline contributions, punishment may increase contributions and potentially efficiency, but in groups with already high contributions, punishment may not increase cooperation and can reduce efficiency due to punishment costs. The presence of anti-social punishment (punishing high contributors) can further reduce efficiency. Control efficiency (without punishment) is a strong moderator: if baseline contributions are high, punishment may not improve and can even worsen efficiency. Social background and group norms are important moderators, but are not directly encoded in the game design dimensions. The findings caution against assuming that punishment will reliably increase efficiency, especially in one-shot or low-trust settings.

- source: Gallier, C. (2020). Democracy and compliance in public goods games. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a non-deterrent contribution rule in a public goods game increases contributions, regardless of whether it is imposed exogenously or chosen democratically. The aggregate increase in contributions is primarily due to the presence of the rule itself, not the democratic process. Democratic participation (voting) does not have a statistically significant effect on contributions at the aggregate level after accounting for the direct effect of the rule, self-selection, and information effects. At the individual level, participants who are overruled in the group vote tend to reduce their contributions, but this negative effect is counteracted by a small democracy premium if the rule is democratically chosen. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a linear public goods game with a mild, non-deterrent automatic sanction, the introduction of the rule (punishment) increases contributions, but the effect is not due to democratic participation per se. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of introducing a weak punishment mechanism on contribution rates, but not directly on efficiency or group payoff, as these are not reported. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, continuous contribution, etc.) are clearly specified and can inform parameterization of prediction models. However, since efficiency is not reported, the paper's quantitative results cannot be directly used for efficiency prediction, but the qualitative pattern (punishment increases cooperation even when non-deterrent) is robust.

- source: Schroeder, K. B., Nettle, D., & McElreath, R. (2015). Interactions between personality and institutions in cooperative behaviour in humans. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that the opportunity for decentralized punishment in a repeated public goods game increases contributions to the public good compared to a no-punishment baseline. This effect is especially pronounced for individuals high in extraversion, who tend to free-ride in the absence of punishment but increase their contributions when punishment is possible. Agreeableness is associated with higher initial contributions regardless of institution, while neuroticism is associated with lower contributions in both conditions. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the increase in contributions with punishment suggests a likely increase in efficiency, subject to the cost of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a repeated public goods game increases contributions, particularly among certain personality types (notably extraverts). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be quantified from this paper alone. The game design dimensions are well specified, and the punishment mechanism is standard (costly, decentralized, 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio). The findings support the general prediction that punishment increases cooperation, but do not allow direct prediction of efficiency outcomes for the downstream task.

- source: Engel, C., & Kurschilgen, M. (2013). The Coevolution of Behavior and Normative Expectations: An Experiment. *American Law and Economics Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that (1) both norm elicitation and legal framing increase cooperation in a repeated public goods game, but do not differ significantly in the absence of punishment; (2) introducing a costly peer punishment option (1:1 cost-to-impact) increases cooperation and reduces free-riding, but the effect is strongest when the norm is embedded in a legal frame; (3) in the presence of punishment, the legal frame prevents the crowding out of intrinsic motivation and leads to higher contributions and more stable norm perceptions; (4) average earnings are higher in the Law-S (legal frame with sanctions) treatment than in other sanctioning treatments, indicating that punishment can improve group welfare when combined with legal framing. However, the paper does not directly analyze efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but average earnings per treatment are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with 4 players, 30 rounds, and a 1:1 punishment cost-to-impact ratio, enabling punishment increases group earnings and cooperation, especially when the norm is legally framed. The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive and most pronounced when combined with a legal frame. The control (no-punishment) efficiency is lower than in the punishment-enabled treatments, particularly Law-S. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, but the magnitude depends on the presence of legal/normative framing. The results are most directly applicable to games with continuous contributions, no chat, full information about others' contributions, and a 1:1 punishment cost-to-impact ratio.

- source: Weber, T. O., Weisel, O., & Gächter, S. (2018). Dispositional free riders do not free ride on punishment. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that the availability of punishment leads dispositional free riders (DFR) to increase their contributions to the public good to levels similar to dispositional conditional cooperators (DCC). Both DFR and DCC are equally likely to punish non-cooperators, and their punishment expenditures and motives are similar, regardless of whether punishment reduces payoff inequality (3:1 ratio) or not (1:1 ratio). The presence of punishment thus increases cooperation among DFR, but the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, focusing instead on behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a one-shot public goods game increases contributions among individuals who would otherwise free ride, and that both free riders and conditional cooperators are willing to punish non-cooperators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does, however, support the expectation that punishment increases cooperation even among those predisposed to free ride, which is likely to translate into higher efficiency in similar game designs. The lack of direct efficiency data is a limitation for quantitative prediction.

- source: Visser, M., & Burns, J. (2015). Inequality, social sanctions and cooperation within South African fishing communities. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment opportunities increase contribution rates in both equal and unequal endowment groups, with a larger effect in unequal groups. Low endowment players contribute a higher share of their endowment than high endowment players. Punishment is used less in unequal groups, but is more effective at raising contributions. Demand for punishment does not differ significantly between high and low endowment players, despite higher relative costs for low endowment players. Punishment is mainly targeted at free-riders, with antisocial punishment more common in equal groups. Socio-economic status and social attitudes (e.g., distrust in formal institutions) influence punishment behavior. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that peer punishment increases contribution rates in linear public goods games, especially in groups with unequal endowments. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, and the findings about contribution rates and punishment behavior are informative for predicting behavioral responses to punishment. For efficiency prediction, the paper suggests that punishment is likely to increase efficiency in similar settings, especially in unequal groups, but the magnitude cannot be inferred. The absence of payoff/efficiency data is a key limitation for direct prediction.

- source: Vollan, B., Landmann, A., Zhou, Y. X., Hu, B. L., & Herrmann-Pillath, C. (2017). Cooperation and authoritarian values: An experimental study in China. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a one-shot, linear public goods game with a mild, non-deterrent punishment for under-contribution, exogenously imposed punishment rules (authoritarian/top-down) significantly increase average contributions compared to no law or endogenously chosen (democratic) punishment rules. This effect is strongest among participants (especially workers) with high deference to authority, and weaker or absent among those with low deference to authority (especially students). The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but since the game is linear and contributions are the main outcome, higher contributions imply higher efficiency. There is no evidence of a democracy premium in this Chinese sample; instead, authoritarian implementation is more effective at increasing cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a linear PGG with a mild, non-deterrent punishment, enabling punishment via exogenous (authoritarian) imposition increases average contributions (and thus efficiency) compared to no punishment. The effect is context-dependent: in this Chinese sample, exogenous punishment is more effective than democratic (endogenous) punishment, especially among those with high deference to authority. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on cultural context and the method of rule implementation (exogenous vs. endogenous). However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so predictions must be inferred from contribution rates.

- source: Bicskei, M., Lankau, M., & Bizer, K. (2016). Negative reciprocity and its relation to anger-like emotions in identity-homogeneous and -heterogeneous groups. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when peer punishment is available in a one-shot PGG, the likelihood and intensity of punishment for low contributions is significantly lower in identity-homogeneous (in-group) groups than in identity-heterogeneous (out-group) or random groups. Anger-like emotions (anger, contempt, irritation) are a strong driver of punishment, but their effect is moderated by group identity: they lead to more punishment in out-groups than in-groups. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but argues that higher punishment in out-groups, being costly, likely reduces social welfare relative to in-groups, where punishment is less frequent and less intense.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on behavior in PGGs is moderated by group identity: punishment is less frequent and less intense in in-groups, and anger-like emotions drive punishment more in out-groups. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that in settings with identity-heterogeneous groups, punishment is more likely to be used, which (given the costliness of punishment) may reduce efficiency relative to in-groups. For prediction tasks, this paper is most informative about the behavioral mechanisms and moderators of punishment, but not about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Engel, C., Beckenkamp, M., Glöckner, A., Irlenbusch, B., Hennig-Schmidt, H., Kube, S., Kurschilgen, M., Morell, A., Nicklisch, A., Normann, H. T., & Towfigh, E. (2014). First impressions are more important than early intervention: Qualifying broken windows theory in the lab. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated public goods games with and without punishment, the authors find that first impressions (i.e., the initial level of cooperation in the group) have a strong and lasting effect on subsequent contributions. The opportunity to punish freeriders (costly punishment) stabilizes cooperation and slows the decay of contributions over time, compared to games without punishment. However, the effect of early punishment (punishing in the first round) is small and fades over time, especially when punished players can retaliate. The main driver of sustained cooperation is good first impressions, with punishment playing a secondary, stabilizing role. The paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoffs, but higher contributions in the punishment condition imply higher efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in standard linear public goods games, enabling punishment increases and stabilizes contributions compared to no-punishment baselines, especially when initial group behavior is cooperative. While the paper does not report efficiency directly, the close link between contribution rate and efficiency in this design means the results are highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes. The effect of punishment is positive but depends on initial conditions; early punishment per se is less important than the overall opportunity for punishment. The findings are most applicable to 4-player, 10-round, continuous-contribution PGGs with standard punishment technology and no communication.

- source: Wu, J. H., Balliet, D., Peperkoorn, L. S., Romano, A., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2020). Cooperation in Groups of Different Sizes: The Effects of Punishment and Reputation-Based Partner Choice. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both punishment and reputation-based partner choice significantly increase the rate of full cooperation in one-shot public goods games, regardless of group size (4, 20, or 40). The effect of punishment on cooperation is positive and robust, but there is no evidence that its effectiveness depends on group size. The main outcome is the binary cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not report on efficiency, welfare, or total group earnings, so its findings are adjacent to the prediction task, which requires payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment increases cooperation rates in one-shot public goods games across a range of group sizes (4, 20, 40), with a fixed marginal group return (MGR = 1.6). However, because the outcome is cooperation rate (all-or-nothing contribution), not efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency. The design dimensions are well specified (player count, all-or-nothing, no chat, fixed MGR, known group size, etc.), so the paper is useful for understanding how punishment affects cooperation rates under these conditions, but not for directly predicting efficiency as defined by group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum.

- source: Bell, A., Zhang, W., & Nou, K. (2016). Pesticide use and cooperative management of natural enemy habitat in a framed field experiment. *Agricultural Systems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment uses a one-shot public goods game with three treatments: (1) Base (no punishment), (2) UCPun (conditional decision maker can be punished by an unconditional decision maker), and (3) CPun (conditional decision maker can punish an unconditional decision maker). The threat of punishment (UCPun) increases the slope of conditional cooperation but reduces the probability of being a conditional cooperator (more free riders). The power to punish (CPun) does not reduce conditional cooperation (no substitution effect on cooperation), but does lead to more sensitive punishment behavior. Responsibility effects (increased cooperation when given punishment power) are stronger than substitution effects. The overall effect of punishment on cooperation is positive, but the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment mechanisms affect conditional cooperation in a one-shot public goods game with four players, MPCR 0.4, and costless punishment. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that enabling punishment increases the responsiveness of cooperators to others' contributions and may increase overall cooperation, but also increases free riding. For prediction tasks, the results imply that punishment is likely to increase cooperation-related behaviors, but the net effect on efficiency is ambiguous due to the rise in free riders. The absence of direct efficiency analysis limits the ability to quantitatively predict treatment efficiency from control efficiency, but the behavioral mechanisms identified are informative for modeling.

- source: Cheung, S. L. (2014). New insights into conditional cooperation and punishment from a strategy method experiment. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that both conditional cooperation and punishment in a public goods game are sensitive to the full distribution of others' contributions, not just the average. When punishment is available, more subjects contribute and a substantial fraction are willing to punish, with punishment increasing as the target's contribution falls below the punisher's and as the third player's contribution rises. However, the study does not directly report or analyze group efficiency, welfare, or total earnings as a function of punishment availability. The main findings are about the behavioral patterns of contribution and punishment, and the heterogeneity of types (conditional cooperators, free-riders, others).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment affects contribution and punishment behavior in a one-shot, three-player public goods game with a strategy method. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does support the qualitative expectation that punishment increases contributions and that the effect depends on the distribution of others' contributions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is informative about mechanisms and behavioral responses, but not about the magnitude of efficiency changes.

- source: Zhou, Y. X., Chen, S. W., Chen, Y. F., & Vollane, B. (2022). Does parental migration impede the development of the cooperative preferences in their left-behind children? Evidence from a large-scale field experiment in China. *China Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that (1) children's cooperation (measured by contribution in a public goods game) increases with age; (2) paternal migration significantly reduces children's cooperation, while maternal or both-parent migration does not; (3) both exogenous and endogenous punishment mechanisms significantly increase cooperation, with exogenous punishment being more effective; (4) exogenous punishment can offset the negative effect of paternal migration on cooperation. The main outcome is contribution level, not efficiency or group payoff, so the findings are most directly about behavioral cooperation rather than payoff-based efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing punishment mechanisms (especially exogenous punishment) increases cooperation (contributions) in one-shot public goods games with children, and can offset negative effects of paternal absence. However, because the outcome is contribution level rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings must be mapped to efficiency outcomes with caution. The game design dimensions are well specified (e.g., player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech), and the effect of punishment is robustly positive on contributions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides indirect but informative evidence that punishment is likely to increase efficiency in similar settings, especially when baseline cooperation is low due to social factors.

- source: Kamei, K. (2014). Conditional punishment. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment finds that individuals' punishment of non-cooperators increases with the amount of punishment assigned by other group members. Most subjects are 'free-riders' who do not punish, but about 23% are conditional punishers who increase punishment as others do. There is also a distinction between pro-social and anti-social conditional punishers. The study provides evidence that conditional punishment types are heterogeneous and that these types can predict unconditional punishment behavior. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed behavioral evidence on the heterogeneity and conditionality of punishment in a standard PGG with punishment, but does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and types of punishment behavior that may underlie efficiency effects in similar games, but cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, suggesting that the presence of conditional punishers may amplify punishment effects when others also punish, but the net effect on efficiency is not measured.

- source: Spadaro, G., Graf, C., Jin, S. X., Arai, S., Inoue, Y., Lieberman, E., Rinderu, M. I., Yuan, M. L., Van Lissa, C. J., & Balliet, D. (2022). Cross-Cultural Variation in Cooperation: A Meta-Analysis. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat
  findings: The meta-analysis finds that the presence of sanctions (punishment and/or reward) in social dilemma experiments is associated with significantly higher cooperation rates. Other situational features, such as communication and lower conflict of interest, also increase cooperation. However, the outcome measured is cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. No significant cross-societal or cross-cultural differences in cooperation are found after controlling for study characteristics. The effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff is not directly analyzed or reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment or reward mechanisms increases cooperation rates in PGG and PD experiments, across a wide range of societies and experimental designs. However, because the outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The paper does not address whether increased cooperation from punishment leads to higher or lower efficiency, as it does not analyze payoff-based outcomes. For the prediction task, the paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment increases cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency effects.

- source: Capraro, V. (2024). The Dual-Process Approach to Human Sociality: Meta-Analytic Evidence for a Theory of Internalized Heuristics for Self-Preservation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that promoting intuitive processing (via time pressure, ego depletion, conceptual primes, etc.) increases negative reciprocity (i.e., punishment of norm violations) in economic games, including public goods games with punishment phases and ultimatum games. This is supported by a meta-analysis showing a small but significant increase in punishment/rejection rates when intuition is promoted. However, the review does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes. The effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is not addressed; the focus is on the cognitive basis of the decision to punish, not on the downstream consequences for group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that intuitive processing increases the likelihood of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments, but it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this review is not directly informative, as it does not report or synthesize efficiency or welfare outcomes. It is useful for understanding moderators of punishment behavior (e.g., cognitive manipulation, individual differences), but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. Use this paper to inform models of punishment frequency or behavioral response, not efficiency.

- source: de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., Kiyonari, T., Molenmaker, W. E., & van Dijk, E. (2019). Do people prefer leaders who enforce norms? Reputational effects of reward and punishment decisions in noisy social dilemmas. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across three experimental studies, the paper finds that both rewarding and punishing norm enforcement by leaders in public goods games (and an organizational scenario) generally improve third-party observers' perceptions of leader competence and morality, especially under conditions of perfect monitoring (no noise). Under behavioral noise (imperfect monitoring), the reputational benefits of punishment are reduced or eliminated, while rewarding continues to yield positive reputational effects. These effects extend to leader preference (i.e., observers are more likely to prefer leaders who reward or punish, especially reward, over those who do not enforce norms). However, the studies do not measure or report on actual group efficiency, payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public goods games. Its findings are limited to third-party observer perceptions of leader reputation and preference, not to actual behavioral or payoff outcomes among group members. While the design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, punishment cost) are described, the outcomes are not efficiency-related. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. It may, however, inform hypotheses about the reputational incentives for leaders to use punishment or reward, which could indirectly affect norm enforcement behavior in other studies.

- source: Dugar, S. (2013). NON-MONETARY INCENTIVES AND OPPORTUNISTIC BEHAVIOR: EVIDENCE FROM A LABORATORY PUBLIC GOOD GAME. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with groups of four, the introduction of non-monetary, costless disapproval points (social sanctions) after each round leads to significantly higher group contributions and group earnings (efficiency) compared to a baseline with no such mechanism. Approval points (social rewards) alone have a small, weakly significant effect on efficiency. The combination of approval and disapproval points produces the highest efficiency, with group earnings and contributions both significantly higher than in any other treatment. The effect is robust over 15 rounds, though some decay is observed. The study demonstrates that even costless, payoff-irrelevant social sanctions can substantially improve group welfare in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a costless, non-monetary punishment mechanism (disapproval points) in a standard linear public goods game increases efficiency (group earnings) relative to a baseline with no such mechanism. The effect is larger than that of a costless reward mechanism (approval points), and the combination of both is most effective. Because the punishment is costless and does not reduce group payoffs, all efficiency gains are realized. For prediction tasks, this suggests that introducing a non-monetary, costless punishment mechanism in a repeated, anonymous, four-player, 15-round linear PGG with MPCR=0.4 will increase efficiency substantially over the control. The effect is likely to be even larger if both approval and disapproval are available. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with costless, anonymous, non-monetary sanctions, and may not generalize to costly or monetary punishment.

- source: Fatas, E., Morales, A. J., & Ubeda, P. (2010). Blind justice: An experimental analysis of random punishment in team production. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study implements a public goods game with a novel random punishment mechanism: after each round, each group member is excluded from the group benefit with probability 1-R, where R is the normalized group contribution. This 'blind' punishment does not target individual defectors. The experiment compares three conditions: standard VCM (no punishment), random punishment without redistribution (RP), and random punishment with redistribution (RPR). Results show that both RP and RPR significantly increase contributions compared to VCM. However, only RPR increases group earnings (efficiency), as RP's punishment cost outweighs the contribution gains. The effect of random punishment is similar whether introduced after a baseline or from the start. Behavioral analysis shows that high contributors reduce their contributions if punished, but overall, conditional cooperation persists and the net effect is positive for contributions. The mechanism does not fully prevent the decline in contributions over time.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that random, non-individually-targeted punishment can increase contributions in a standard public goods game, but its effect on efficiency depends on whether the penalty is redistributed. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism that is not individually targeted but is probabilistically linked to group performance will likely increase contributions, but may not increase efficiency unless the punishment cost is recycled within the group. The effect is robust to order and does not depend on communication or knowledge of individual contributions. The marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is 0.5, and the group size is 4. The findings suggest that, for similar designs, enabling random punishment will increase contributions and may increase efficiency if the punishment cost is not lost to the group.

- source: Dannenberg, A., Haita-Falah, C., & Zitzelsberger, S. (2020). Voting on the threat of exclusion in a public goods experiment. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with groups of 5, the option to exclude (ostracize) members is introduced either costlessly (B10) or with a 20% endowment cost to all (B8). Groups can vote to implement the exclusion institution (endogenous) or have it imposed (exogenous). The exclusion institution leads to significantly higher contributions in all treatments. When exclusion is costless (B10), average payoffs (efficiency) are higher with exclusion than without. When exclusion is costly (B8), average payoffs are lower with exclusion than without, despite higher contributions. The exclusion mechanism is used almost exclusively to remove the lowest contributors, and the threat of exclusion increases cooperation among those who remain. The effect of exclusion on efficiency is positive when costless, but negative or neutral when costly. Endogenous and exogenous implementation yield similar results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment (here, exclusion/ostracism) on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the costliness of the punishment institution. When exclusion is costless, enabling punishment increases both contributions and efficiency. When exclusion is costly (reducing endowments), enabling punishment increases contributions but does not improve efficiency and may reduce it. The marginal per-capita return (MPCR), group size, and the presence/absence of communication are all specified, allowing for direct mapping to prediction tasks. The results are robust to endogenous vs exogenous institution choice. For prediction, the key moderator is whether the punishment institution imposes a cost on all group members; if so, efficiency gains are not realized even if cooperation rises.

- source: Peeters, R., & Vorsatz, M. (2013). IMMATERIAL REWARDS AND SANCTIONS IN A VOLUNTARY CONTRIBUTION EXPERIMENT. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing costless, immaterial feedback (smilies/frownies) does not significantly increase average group contributions or payoffs in a voluntary contribution public goods game. Under partner matching, feedback treatments show higher variance in group contributions, with some groups sustaining high contributions, but the average effect is not significant. Under stranger matching, feedback treatments do not outperform control and may even reduce contributions. The effect of feedback on future contributions is context-dependent: under partner matching, sending/receiving emoticons can reduce future contributions; under stranger matching, receiving a frowny can increase future contributions for some participants. Overall, immaterial feedback does not reliably improve efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that costless, immaterial punishment or reward mechanisms (such as emoticons) do not significantly improve efficiency or group payoff in standard public goods games, regardless of matching protocol. For prediction tasks, the presence of immaterial, non-costly, non-monetary punishment should not be expected to increase efficiency relative to control. The effect of such feedback is highly variable and context-dependent, with no robust positive effect on group payoff or efficiency. The findings suggest that, unless punishment or reward is material (i.e., has real payoff consequences), enabling such mechanisms is unlikely to improve efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Galbiati, R., & Vertova, P. (2014). How laws affect behavior: Obligations, incentives and cooperative behavior. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Obligations (minimum required contributions) significantly increase average contributions to the public good, even in the absence of incentives. Non-binding incentives (probabilistic, low-stakes rewards/punishments) alone do not affect contributions, but when combined with obligations, they substantially increase contributions beyond the effect of obligations alone. The effect of obligations and incentives is complementary. The mechanisms are traced to changes in beliefs about others' contributions and in individuals' willingness to cooperate. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the design implies that higher contributions increase group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a one-shot linear public good game with 6 players and no communication, introducing a minimum contribution requirement (obligation) increases average contributions, and adding even weak, non-binding incentives (probabilistic punishment/reward) further increases contributions. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the structure of the game means that higher contributions correspond to higher efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence of obligations and (even weak) punishment/reward mechanisms can be expected to increase efficiency relative to a baseline with neither, especially when both are present. However, the absence of direct efficiency data means that quantitative predictions must be inferred from the reported contribution rates.

- source: Sell, J., & Wilson, R. K. (1999). The maintenance of cooperation: Expectations of future interaction and the trigger of group punishment. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that requiring a grim trigger punishment rule (where defection locks a player into permanent defection) substantially increases cooperation rates in a repeated public goods game. The effect of the discount parameter (expected number of rounds) on cooperation is only significant when the grim trigger is imposed, and only between the highest and lower values. Without a mandated punishment phase, increasing the discount parameter does not increase cooperation. The main dependent variable is the proportion of cooperative choices, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that group-level punishment threats can coordinate expectations and sustain cooperation, but the value of future interaction alone is insufficient unless paired with credible punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the imposition of a group-level, long punishment phase (grim trigger) increases cooperation rates in repeated public goods games, especially in the early rounds. However, the outcome measured is cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling a strong group punishment mechanism (even if not individual-targeted) will increase cooperation and likely improve efficiency, especially when players expect many rounds. The effect of expected future interaction (discount parameter) is only realized when punishment is credible and severe. For prediction tasks, the presence of a mandated, long punishment phase should be treated as a strong positive moderator of efficiency, but the absence of direct efficiency data limits quantitative inference.

- source: Gangadharan, L., Nikiforakis, N., & Villeval, M. C. (2017). Normative conflict and the limits of self-governance in heterogeneous populations. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Allowing communication and rewards in a public goods game leads to near-maximal efficiency in homogeneous groups, but only partial efficiency in heterogeneous groups where returns from the public good differ. In homogeneous groups, communication increases group earnings to 94% of the maximum, while in heterogeneous groups, the increase is only 80% of the maximum. The main reason is that most heterogeneous groups prioritize equality or compromise between equality and efficiency, rather than maximizing group earnings. Rewards are not systematically used to offset inequality. The loss in efficiency in heterogeneous groups is due both to lower contributions and to under-use of rewards. The study demonstrates that normative conflict can limit the effectiveness of self-governance mechanisms in promoting efficiency in heterogeneous populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency gains from enabling communication and rewards in public goods games depend critically on group homogeneity. In homogeneous groups, such mechanisms can achieve near-maximal efficiency. In heterogeneous groups, even with communication and rewards, efficiency gains are much smaller due to normative conflict between equality and efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of heterogeneity in returns (mpcr differences) is a strong moderator that limits the efficiency benefit of enabling communication and rewards. The absence of punishment means the results are most directly applicable to reward-based or communication-based interventions, but the findings on normative conflict are likely relevant for predicting the limits of punishment as well, especially in heterogeneous settings.

- source: Nosenzo, D., & Tufano, F. (2017). The effect of voluntary participation on cooperation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment compares three main treatments: Baseline (forced participation), Unconditional (voluntary participation before knowing partner's contribution), and Conditional (voluntary participation after seeing partner's contribution). In Baseline and Unconditional, average contributions and efficiency are nearly identical (about 22% of endowment contributed; efficiency ~22%). In Conditional, average contributions and efficiency are much higher (47% of endowment; efficiency up to 81.9% among those who agree to play, and 46.6% overall). The positive effect of voluntary participation on efficiency is only observed when participation can be used as a threat against free-riding. There is little evidence that unconditional voluntary participation leads to assortative matching of cooperators, even when the outside option is increased. The main mechanism for increased efficiency is the threat of non-participation disciplining free-riders, not selection. However, frequent withdrawals can reduce realized efficiency gains.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-person one-shot PGG, enabling voluntary participation only increases efficiency if players can condition their participation on knowledge of the partner's intended contribution (i.e., if non-participation is a credible threat against free-riding). Unconditional voluntary participation (without information) does not improve efficiency, even with a higher outside option. For prediction, the presence of a conditional participation mechanism (a form of endogenous partner exclusion or exit threat) is a strong moderator of punishment-like effects on efficiency, even in the absence of explicit costly punishment. The results suggest that, in similar game designs, enabling conditional exit can substitute for explicit punishment in raising efficiency, but unconditional exit does not. Control efficiency (forced participation) is a good predictor of efficiency in unconditional exit games, but not in conditional exit games, where efficiency is much higher.

- source: Riedel, N., & Schildberg-Hörisch, H. (2013). Asymmetric obligations. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that both high and low minimum contribution obligations (even when only weakly enforced) increase contributions in the initial periods compared to no obligation, but this effect is not sustained in the long run. Asymmetric obligations are as effective as symmetric ones: for a given own obligation, the partner's obligation does not affect behavior. The effect of obligations is interpreted as arising from an emotional cost of disobeying, not from expectations about others' behavior. No direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that weakly enforced minimum contribution obligations can temporarily increase contributions in public goods games, regardless of whether obligations are symmetric or asymmetric. However, since the study does not include a standard punishment institution (where players can assign costly punishment to others), and the 'punishment' here is a very weak, probabilistic, externally imposed fine for not meeting a minimum, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The findings suggest that expressive law (weak obligations) can have a short-term effect on cooperation, but do not inform about the efficiency effects of endogenous peer punishment. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, etc.) are well specified, but the absence of a standard punishment treatment limits direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency under peer punishment.

- source: Hauser, O. P., Hendriks, A., Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2016). Think global, act local: Preserving the global commons. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
