  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that when participants can observe their neighbors' contributions to the public good and can condition their pairwise cooperation (in a PD) on this information, contributions to the public good are sustained at high levels, even in very large groups (up to 1,000 participants). In the control condition, where such information is not available, contributions decline over time. The mechanism is not direct punishment but rather the withholding of cooperation in a separate pairwise game, which acts as a social sanction. The effect is robust to group size and does not require participants to know the overall level of public good provision. The main outcome is sustained contribution, not measured efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms allowing for targeted social sanctions (withholding cooperation in a pairwise PD) based on observed public good contributions can sustain high contribution rates in large groups. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to punishment (withholding cooperation as a sanction) but does not involve costly punishment or explicit payoff reduction. The findings suggest that enabling targeted, observable social sanctions—even if not costly—can have a strong positive effect on contribution rates, which may translate to higher efficiency in similar designs, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Boulu-Reshef, B., Holt, C. A., Rodgers, M. S., & Thomas-Hunt, M. C. (2020). The impact of leader communication on free-riding: An incentivized experiment with empowering and directive styles. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that empowering leader messages reduce free-riding (increase contributions) more than directive messages when there is no two-way leader-follower communication. When two-way communication is allowed, both leadership styles lead to higher contributions, and the difference between styles disappears. The presence of two-way communication is especially beneficial for directive leadership. The main dependent variable is contribution rate; earnings are reported but not the focus. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present, and efficiency is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides evidence that communication (especially two-way leader-follower communication) can increase contributions, which may be relevant for understanding the context in which punishment is or is not necessary. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, communication, etc.) are well specified, but the absence of punishment means the findings are only indirectly relevant for the prediction task.

- source: Shirado, H., & Christakis, N. A. (2020). Network Engineering Using Autonomous Agents Increases Cooperation in Human Groups. *Iscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing bots that facilitate the breaking of ties with defectors ('disengaged' intervention) in dynamic network public goods games leads to a substantial increase in cooperation rates among human participants, compared to controls (no bots, bots that always cooperate, or tit-for-tat bots). The effect is robust even when only a single bot is present and when humans are aware of the bots' presence. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperative players per round. There is some supplementary evidence that, under the disengaged intervention, cooperators earn more than defectors, but group-level efficiency or total group payoff is not the main focus. The study does not involve punishment or sanctions in the standard sense, but the network exclusion of defectors is interpreted as a form of decentralized ostracism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Instead, it provides evidence about the effect of network engineering (ostracism via tie-breaking) on cooperation rates. The findings suggest that enabling players to break ties with defectors (or having bots facilitate this) can increase cooperation and, to some extent, the earnings of cooperators. However, because efficiency (group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) is not directly measured or reported, and because punishment is not present, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency under punishment is limited. It may be useful as adjacent evidence for the effects of exclusion or ostracism mechanisms.

- source: Greiff, M., & Paetzel, F. (2016). Second-order beliefs in reputation systems with endogenous evaluations - an experimental study. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a repeated two-player public good game, providing information about both one's own and the partner's evaluation (reputation) increases contributions compared to control and to the treatment where only the partner's evaluation is shown. When only the partner's evaluation is available, contributions do not increase significantly relative to control. The effect is explained by the role of second-order beliefs in conditional cooperation. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but higher contributions in the BOTH-INFO treatment imply higher group payoffs. There is no punishment or costly sanctioning; evaluations are costless, non-binding signals.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of reputation-based information (not costly punishment) on contribution rates in repeated two-player public good games. It suggests that simply providing information about others' evaluations is insufficient to increase cooperation, but providing both own and partner's evaluation can increase contributions. However, because the study does not include a costly punishment mechanism, its direct relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment is limited. The findings are most relevant for settings where social image or reputation feedback (not material punishment) is the main intervention. For prediction tasks involving costly punishment, this paper provides only adjacent evidence.

- source: Reigstad, A. G., Stromland, E. A., & Tinghög, G. (2017). Extending the Cooperative Phenotype: Assessing the Stability of Cooperation across Countries. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that individuals' cooperative behavior is positively correlated across different economic games (Public Goods Game, Dictator Game, Prisoner's Dilemma) and is stable over time and across countries (Norway, Sweden, Austria, United States). Punishment behavior (measured in a Dictator Game with Punishment) is not correlated with prosociality in other games. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate punishment in a PGG context, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing that punishment behavior and cooperation are uncorrelated at the individual level, suggesting that the presence of punishment may not be strongly linked to prosociality. However, it does not inform the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms in PGG-like environments.

- source: Jolly, E., & Chang, L. J. (2021). Gossip drives vicarious learning and facilitates social connection. *Current Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Allowing private communication (gossip) in a repeated public goods game increases average group contributions and slows the decline of cooperation over rounds, compared to no-communication conditions. Gossip is especially important when direct observation of others' behavior is limited, as it enables vicarious learning and helps participants adjust their own contributions based on information received from others. Gossip also increases social bonding and alignment of impressions between conversation partners. The study does not include any formal punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling communication (gossip) in public goods games increases contribution rates and sustains cooperation, especially under incomplete information. However, it does not address the effects of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that social information transmission (gossip) can substitute for formal sanctions in sustaining cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates for efficiency outcomes or for games with punishment enabled. The design dimensions most directly addressed are player count, number of rounds, chat/communication, and information visibility. The absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes limits its direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: Messer, K. D., & Zarghamee, H. (2007). New hope for the voluntary contributions mechanism: The effects of context. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that context—specifically, the combination of cheap talk, voting, and a status quo of giving—can dramatically increase and sustain contributions and efficiency in a voluntary contributions mechanism (VCM) with a low MPCR. In the baseline (no context), efficiency starts at 82.3% and decays to 72.6% by round 10. In the full-context treatment, efficiency is 99.0% initially and 98.1% in the final round, with no significant decay. Cheap talk and status quo of giving each increase initial contributions, but only their interaction (and with voting) sustains high contributions. Voting alone has no effect. The results show that context can substitute for incentive-compatible mechanisms in achieving high efficiency, but the paper does not test or discuss punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is highly informative about the effects of communication, voting, and default framing, but not about punishment. For the downstream prediction task—predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency—this paper is not directly relevant. However, it does provide a strong benchmark for how non-punitive contextual factors can achieve high efficiency, which may be useful for comparative purposes or for understanding the limits of punishment's added value in similar settings.

- source: Kocher, M. G., Tan, F. F., & Yu, J. (2018). PROVIDING GLOBAL PUBLIC GOODS: ELECTORAL DELEGATION AND COOPERATION. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares a standard linear public goods game (Baseline) to a Delegation treatment where each group elects a delegate who determines the contributions of all group members. Delegation leads to higher average contributions and efficiency than the Baseline, but both fall short of the social optimum. Over time, contributions and efficiency decline in both treatments, but less so in Delegation. Delegates rarely exploit their group members, typically assigning equal contributions. However, intergroup conditionality and lack of coordination across groups lead to a downward spiral in contributions and efficiency, a phenomenon the authors term 'P-inefficiency.' The study provides direct evidence on how delegation affects efficiency in public goods provision, but does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the effect of electoral delegation (but not punishment) on efficiency in a linear public goods game. It is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments with and without delegation, but not for environments with punishment or sanctioning. The design dimensions (player count, group size, rounds, MPCR, information, etc.) are well specified, but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is useful as a control or baseline for understanding efficiency in the absence of punishment.

- source: Schmidt, K. M., & Ockenfels, A. (2021). Focusing climate negotiations on a uniform common commitment can promote cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that negotiation design is crucial for promoting cooperation and efficiency in public goods games. Uniform common commitments (UCC) result in group investments averaging 83% of the social optimum with enforcement, compared to 45% (individual commitments, IC) and 48% (complex common commitments, CCC). Even without enforcement, UCC achieves 52% of the optimum, while IC and CCC achieve only 34%. The UCC design is robust to asymmetries and to the absence of enforcement. The paper does not study punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that negotiation design—specifically, requiring a uniform minimum contribution—can dramatically increase group efficiency in public goods games, even in the absence of enforcement. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It is highly relevant for understanding the impact of commitment and negotiation structure, but not for the prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Keuschnigg, M., & Schikora, J. (2014). The dark side of leadership: An experiment on religious heterogeneity and cooperation in India. *Journal of Socio-economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that religious heterogeneity does not reduce efficiency or contributions in a standard simultaneous PGG. However, introducing a leader (sequential move, 'leading by example') reduces both contributions and efficiency, especially in religiously mixed groups. In the control (no leader) treatment, net profit is about 58-60% of endowment in both pure and mixed groups. In the leadership treatment, net profit drops to 52.5% in pure groups and 39.4% in mixed groups. The negative effect of leadership is driven by a lack of reciprocity in mixed groups: followers do not respond to leader contributions, and leaders anticipate this, leading to lower contributions overall. Thus, the introduction of a hierarchical structure (leadership) in heterogeneous groups can be detrimental to efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning, introducing a sequential move structure (leadership) in a linear PGG can reduce efficiency, especially in culturally heterogeneous (religiously mixed) groups. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling a leadership/first-mover mechanism (without punishment) is likely to decrease efficiency relative to the simultaneous-move baseline, particularly when group heterogeneity is high. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the only institutional change is the introduction of a leader, not punishment or reward. The results do not inform about punishment effects, as no punishment or sanctioning is present.

- source: Bouma, J., & Ansink, E. (2013). The role of legitimacy perceptions in self-restricted resource use: A framed field experiment. *Forest Policy and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that label framing (contextualizing the game as protected area management) does not significantly reduce extractions (increase efficiency) on average. However, for participants who trust enforcement officers or feel their community can influence park management, framing leads to lower extractions (higher efficiency). The effect is not significant for the sample as a whole, but is significant for these subgroups. The explanatory power of legitimacy indicators is low, and the sample size is small. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or reward mechanisms are present. However, it does show that contextual framing and legitimacy perceptions can moderate extraction (and thus efficiency) in PGG-like environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that institutional context and perceived legitimacy may interact with other design features to affect efficiency, but it does not inform the effect of adding punishment. It is not directly useful for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, but may be informative for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment, especially in field or framed settings.

- source: Ren, T. Y., & Zheng, J. J. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics in the spatial public goods game with tolerance-based expulsion and cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in a standard linear public goods game (VCM) with four players and 20 rounds per part, groups that start with unequal endowments (two rich, two poor) and then experience redistribution to equality do not increase their group contributions or efficiency after redistribution. Group contributions in the post-redistribution phase are indistinguishable from those in groups that remain unequal, and both are significantly lower than in always-equal groups. The experience of initial inequality creates a persistent negative spillover, locking groups into low cooperation and efficiency even after equality is restored. The mechanism is traced to the former poor mimicking the low-contribution behavior of the former rich after redistribution. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, simply reducing inequality via redistribution does not increase efficiency in public goods games if groups have previously experienced inequality. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, for games with similar design (no punishment, no reward, linear PGG, 4 players, 20+20 rounds, MPCR=0.4, no chat, no identification, no summary of individual contributions), the efficiency in the treatment with redistribution will not exceed that of the control with persistent inequality, and both will be lower than in always-equal groups. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in PGGs without punishment or reward, but do not inform about the effect of punishment.

- source: Cadigan, J., Wayland, P. T., Schmitt, P., & Swope, K. (2011). An experimental dynamic public goods game with carryover. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing dynamic carryover (either via endowment or MPCR) can increase first-stage contributions in public goods games, but this does not reliably translate into higher efficiency. Efficiency, defined as the share of maximal cooperative gains captured, is not significantly different from the baseline in most treatments, except when MPCR is increased. The main determinants of contributions and efficiency are endowment and MPCR, consistent with prior VCM literature. There is no evidence on punishment or sanctioning, as these mechanisms are not present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on how dynamic features like carryover in endowment or MPCR affect efficiency and contributions in public goods games without punishment. It shows that increasing contributions via dynamic incentives does not necessarily increase efficiency, especially if the maximum possible surplus also increases. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency is primarily determined by MPCR and endowment, and that dynamic features alone do not guarantee higher efficiency. The paper does not inform about the effects of punishment, so it should not be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment in similar games.

- source: van Soest, D., Stoop, J., & Vyrastekova, J. (2016). Toward a delineation of the circumstances in which cooperation can be sustained in environmental and resource problems. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game (VCM), average net group contributions are positive and significant, leading to substantial group payoffs and efficiency. In contrast, when the action space is extended to allow negative contributions (claims/taking from the public fund), average net group contributions are not significantly different from zero, and group payoffs are much lower. This holds even for subjects who initially display prosocial behavior. The ability to undo others' contributions destroys cooperation and efficiency, and the effect is not explained by framing or subject pool differences. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the action space—specifically, whether players can undo others' contributions—has a dramatic effect on efficiency in public goods games. In standard VCMs (with only non-negative contributions), efficiency is much higher than in Claim Games (where negative contributions are allowed). However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, establish a strong baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency is high in standard VCMs and very low in Claim Games. For prediction tasks, this paper helps set expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency under different action space designs, but does not inform the effect of punishment.

- source: Pan, X. S., & Houser, D. (2011). Competition for Trophies Triggers Male Generosity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in a standard linear public goods game, the introduction of a unique and displayable reward (a 'trophy' mug) leads to significantly higher contributions, especially among males, compared to both a baseline (no reward) and a control reward (ice-cream) that is not unique or displayable. The effect is driven by male competitiveness for the trophy. Female behavior is less affected by the trophy. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as outcomes. The main outcomes are behavioral: contribution rates, approval points, and star rewards.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding the effects of non-pecuniary, status-based rewards (trophies) on cooperation, especially among males, but does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The game design dimensions are well described, but the absence of punishment and lack of efficiency or payoff analysis means this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Isler, O., Maule, J., & Starmer, C. (2018). Is intuition really cooperative? Improved tests support the social heuristics hypothesis. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that time-pressure (intuitive decision-making) increases contributions to the public good compared to forced-delay (reflective decision-making), especially under certain instructional conditions. The effect is robust to compliance and understanding confounds. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment condition or analyze payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on contribution rates under different cognitive conditions may be contextually informative for understanding cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency with or without punishment. The design dimensions reported (e.g., player count, MPCR, no punishment, no reward, no chat) may help contextualize the baseline, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes limits its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., El Mouden, C., & West, S. A. (2017). Social learning and the demise of costly cooperation in humans. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that providing social information about other groups' behaviors or payoffs does not increase cooperation in repeated public goods games; in most cases, it leads to a significant decline in cooperation. The decline is strongest when individuals can observe the behavior and payoffs of the most successful individuals, supporting the idea of payoff-biased or prestige-biased social learning. There is no evidence for conformity bias (copying common behaviors) or group-prestige bias (copying successful groups). The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design. Its findings are relevant for understanding how social learning and information about others' behavior/payoff affect cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as not providing evidence about punishment effects, but may be informative about baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment.

- source: Lamba, S. (2014). Social learning in cooperative dilemmas. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that individuals do not show a clear tendency to conform to the modal contribution or to copy the highest earner's contribution in public goods games. There is significant individual and village-level variation in social learning strategies, and these are influenced by individual and contextual factors. Overall, contributions decreased in the second round, and there is no evidence that social learning increases cooperation. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present in the experimental design. The findings are relevant for understanding baseline behavior in the absence of punishment, especially regarding the role of social learning, but do not inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may help characterize control conditions (no punishment, no reward, no communication), but does not provide treatment effect estimates or moderators for punishment.

- source: Horita, Y., Takezawa, M., Inukai, K., Kita, T., & Masuda, N. (2017). Reinforcement learning accounts for moody conditional cooperation behavior: experimental results. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that moody conditional cooperation (MCC) patterns are present in both repeated multiplayer PDG and PGG, though the specific form differs from previous literature. In the PGG, conditional cooperation is observed in both fixed and mixed group treatments. Reinforcement learning models (Bush-Mosteller and Roth-Erev) fit the observed behavioral data as well as or better than CC and MCC models, especially when direct reciprocity is absent. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It is useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics and the explanatory power of behavioral models in repeated PGGs with and without stable group composition. For the prediction task, this paper may inform expectations about baseline (control) cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, but provides no evidence about the effect of punishment or moderators of punishment efficacy.

- source: Brañas-Garza, P., Molis, E., & Neyse, L. (2021). Exposure to inequality may cause under-provision of public goods: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that making subjects aware of inequality in returns between groups (by informing them about other groups' mpcr) leads to a significant reduction in contributions among low-mpcr groups and a non-significant increase among high-mpcr groups, but only when the inequality is large (mpcr difference of 0.5). When the inequality is small (mpcr difference of 0.25), the effect of social information is negligible. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcome is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that transparency about inequality can undermine cooperation in public goods provision, especially for those at a disadvantage.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in any treatment. However, it does provide informative evidence about how information about inequality (social comparison) can affect contribution rates, which may indirectly affect efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight that contextual factors like perceived inequality and information can strongly moderate contribution behavior. The game design dimensions are well specified (player count, mpcr, number of rounds, information structure), but the absence of punishment means the findings cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Martinangeli, A. F. M. (2021). Do what (you think) the rich will do: Inequality and belief heterogeneity in public good provision. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games with unequal endowments, both rich and poor participants expect the rich to contribute more. Individual contributions in the repeated game are more strongly correlated with beliefs about the rich than about the poor. Group cohesion interventions (allowing chat in a team-building task) do not significantly increase overall contributions, though there is some evidence of increased contributions among poor participants. There is no significant difference in the proportion of endowment contributed between equal and unequal endowment conditions. The paper does not analyze efficiency or total group payoff, and there are no punishment or reward mechanisms in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or reward mechanisms are present. However, it provides informative context about how endowment heterogeneity and belief formation affect contribution behavior. For prediction tasks involving efficiency, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of beliefs about specific group members (especially the rich) in driving cooperation, and in showing that group cohesion interventions may have limited impact in the absence of feedback or punishment.

- source: Chaudhuri, A., Paichayontvijit, T., & Smith, A. (2017). Belief heterogeneity and contributions decay among conditional cooperators in public goods games. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that belief heterogeneity among conditional cooperators explains persistent differences in contribution levels and the observed decay in contributions over time. In treatments without feedback, contributions remain stable and reflect initial beliefs. When feedback is provided, optimists reduce their contributions more than pessimists increase theirs, leading to overall decay. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the mechanisms behind contribution decay but do not directly inform the effects of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments. However, it does provide detailed evidence on how information feedback and belief heterogeneity affect contribution dynamics. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding baseline contribution decay in the absence of punishment.

- source: My, K. B., & Chalvignac, B. (2010). Voluntary participation and cooperation in a collective-good game. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing voluntary participation (exit option) in a repeated public-good game does not increase average contributions compared to the baseline with compulsory participation, unless the exit option is highly attractive. In the VP-16 treatment (exit payoff = 16), the decay in contributions over time is counteracted, and in some groups, contributions even increase in later rounds. However, average contributions are not significantly higher than in the baseline. The less attractive exit option (VP-11) does not have a positive effect. No punishment or sanctioning is present in any treatment. The main outcome is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-good games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It does, however, provide informative context about the baseline (no-punishment) efficiency and contribution dynamics in a standard linear PGG with and without voluntary participation. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only useful for understanding the control (no-punishment) condition, not the treatment (punishment-enabled) condition. It does not inform the effect of punishment or moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Kölle, F., & Quercia, S. (2021). The influence of empirical and normative expectations on cooperation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a standard linear public goods game, most people are conditional cooperators, increasing their contributions as others do. When there is strategic uncertainty (simultaneous play), normative expectations (what is socially appropriate) and empirical expectations (what others will do) diverge, and actual contributions are lower than the social optimum. When players can condition on others' contributions (sequential/strategy method), empirical and normative expectations are more closely aligned, and contributions are higher and more predictable. The paper provides direct evidence for the existence of a social norm of conditional cooperation, but does not study punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding baseline cooperation and the role of expectations in the absence of punishment, but cannot inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The game design dimensions are well specified for a standard PGG, but the absence of any punishment or sanctioning mechanism means this paper cannot be used to predict treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Gianotti, L. R. R., Dahinden, F. M., Baumgartner, T., & Knoch, D. (2019). Understanding Individual Differences in Domain-General Prosociality: A Resting EEG Study. *Brain Topography*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that higher resting beta2 and beta3 current density in the right temporo-parietal junction (TPJ) is positively associated with higher domain-general prosociality, as measured by contributions in three one-shot PGGs (with multipliers 1.2, 2, and 3.2) and giving in a dictator game. The neural trait is stable and specific to the TPJ and beta bands. No relationship is found between prosociality and the honesty-humility personality scale. The study does not include any punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings are limited to individual differences in prosociality (contribution/giving) and their neural correlates. Thus, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment or related game design dimensions.

- source: Gong, X., & Sanfey, A. G. (2017). Social rank and social cooperation: Impact of social comparison processes on cooperative decision-making. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that social comparison, operationalized as being assigned a high or low rank in a prior task, significantly affects subsequent contribution decisions in a standard public goods game. High rank leads to increased contributions, low rank to decreased contributions, compared to a baseline with no ranking. These effects are robust to whether the PGG group is the same as the ranking group, and are more pronounced in individuals with high social comparison orientation. The experiment does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are limited to how social comparison affects contribution behavior. While the game design (standard PGG) is relevant, the absence of punishment means it cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The paper may be useful for understanding baseline contribution behavior or the impact of social comparison, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Lotz, S. (2015). Spontaneous Giving under Structural Inequality: Intuition Promotes Cooperation in Asymmetric Social Dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that priming participants to use intuition (vs. reflection) increases their contributions to the public good in a one-shot, four-player PGG with asymmetric MPCRs (structural inequality). The effect of intuition is robust to controls for age, gender, comprehension, and paragraph length. There is no significant main effect of MPCR (high vs. low) on cooperation, nor an interaction between intuition and MPCR. The study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant as a baseline for understanding cooperation rates in asymmetric, one-shot PGGs without punishment or reward. The findings may inform the expected control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar asymmetric games, but cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on contribution rates (not payoffs) limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Metcalfe, R., & Powdthavee, N. (2015). Can priming cooperation increase public good contributions?. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that priming participants with cooperative concepts (via a word search) leads to significantly higher contributions in a one-shot public goods game compared to a neutrally primed control group. The effect is robust to controls for gender, nationality, and field of study, and does not appear to be mediated by beliefs about others' contributions. The main outcome is contribution level; average earnings are reported but not analyzed in detail. No punishment, reward, or sanction mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. Its findings are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency with or without punishment. However, it does provide evidence that subtle environmental interventions (like priming) can increase contributions in a standard linear PGG, which may be contextually relevant for understanding other non-incentive-based interventions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not informative about punishment effects.

- source: Martinsson, P., Villegas-Palacio, C., & Wollbrant, C. (2015). Cooperation and social classes: evidence from Colombia. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that both high and medium-low social class groups contribute about 40% of their endowment in a one-shot public goods game, with no significant difference in unconditional contributions or beliefs about others' contributions. The distribution of contributor types (conditional cooperators, free riders, etc.) is also similar between groups. However, medium-low social class members are more likely to contribute when others contribute nothing (i.e., higher baseline conditional cooperation). The study does not include punishment, reward, or repeated interaction, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide a clear description of a standard one-shot linear public goods game with four players, continuous contributions, and MPCR of 0.4, which may be useful as a control or baseline for comparison with studies that do include punishment. The findings on contribution behavior by social class may inform expectations about baseline cooperation in similar environments, but cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Barr, A., Packard, T., & Serra, D. (2014). Participatory accountability and collective action: Experimental evidence from Albania. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is a robust positive correlation between cooperative behavior in a one-shot, binary public goods game and participation in both school-level and national accountability systems. At both the individual and district level, higher rates of cooperation in the game are associated with higher rates of participation in parent representative elections and higher voter turnout. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in the public goods game, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or discuss punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are limited to the relationship between cooperative tendencies and real-world participation in collective action. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods game environments.

- source: Nishi, A., Christakis, N. A., & Rand, D. G. (2017). Cooperation, decision time, and culture: Online experiments with American and Indian participants. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that American participants are more likely to cooperate than Indian participants in a repeated public goods game, both in the first round and in subsequent rounds, regardless of the social environment. Decision time for cooperation versus defection varies by culture: Americans decide faster when cooperating (except in non-cooperative environments), while Indians decide faster when defecting. The experiment does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any related payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or efficiency outcomes are present. It does, however, provide contextual evidence about baseline cooperation rates in repeated public goods games with different cultural groups, which may be useful for understanding baseline control conditions in similar designs. However, it cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment or any moderators of that effect.

- source: Dekel, S., Fischer, S., & Zultan, R. (2017). Potential Pareto Public Goods. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a linear public goods game with two majority players (who benefit from the public good) and one minority player (who is harmed by the public good), punishment institutions do not increase contributions or efficiency. Punishment is used ineffectively, with minority players sometimes punishing contributors, and majority players reluctant to punish free riders. As a result, efficiency is significantly lower in the punishment treatments compared to the baseline. Communication does not improve outcomes in the punishment condition. In contrast, when a reward institution is available and communication is allowed, majority players compensate the harmed minority, which increases majority contributions and efficiency. The presence of a harmed minority undermines the usual positive effects of punishment and communication on cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in public goods games with a harmed minority, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency and may even reduce it, relative to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of communication. The game design features—three players, 30 rounds, continuous contributions, known group composition, and a high punishment impact (3:1 ratio)—do not rescue the effectiveness of punishment in this context. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in similar settings (especially with harmed minorities), enabling punishment is likely to have a neutral or negative effect on efficiency, even if control efficiency is moderate. The paper also shows that reward institutions, especially with communication, can increase efficiency by enabling compensation.

- source: Mulder, L. B., van Dijk, E., De Cremer, D., & Wilke, H. A. M. (2006). When sanctions fail to increase cooperation in social dilemmas: Considering the presence of an alternative option to defect. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two lab experiments, the authors find that introducing a sanctioning system (punishment for defection) increases collective outcomes (efficiency) in a standard two-option social dilemma (cooperate or defect). However, when a third option to defect (alternative defection, more harmful to the group) is available, sanctions do not increase efficiency and can even decrease it, particularly when participants have low expectations of others' cooperation. The negative effect is mediated by the failure of sanctions to increase trust or expectations of cooperation in the presence of the third option. Thus, the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency is contingent on the decision structure and social expectations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games is not uniform: it is positive in standard two-choice settings but can be null or negative when alternative, more harmful defection options are available and when group members have low expectations of cooperation. For prediction, this means that the presence of alternative defection options (i.e., more than two choices, with at least one being more harmful to the group) and low trust/expectations are critical moderators. If the control game (no punishment) has low efficiency and the game allows for alternative defection, enabling punishment may not improve and could even worsen efficiency, especially if group trust is low. In standard two-choice games, punishment is likely to increase efficiency.

- source: Aakre, S., Helland, L., & Hovi, J. (2016). When Does Informal Enforcement Work?. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that enabling punishment of both nonparticipants and noncompliant participants (i.e., enforcing both participation and compliance) leads to a substantial increase in total group contributions (efficiency) when punishment is introduced. In contrast, if only one type of free riding can be punished (either only insiders or only outsiders), introducing punishment does not increase efficiency. In the forced participation (FP) control, punishment increases efficiency as in standard PGGs. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is conditional: it is positive only when both participation and compliance can be enforced, and negligible or even negative otherwise.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on whether the game design allows for both participation and compliance enforcement. If the design allows punishment of both nonparticipants and noncompliant participants (or, in standard PGGs, if participation is forced), enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency. If only one type of free riding can be punished, enabling punishment will not increase efficiency. For prediction, the presence or absence of both enforcement channels is a critical moderator. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not sufficient to predict treatment efficiency unless the enforcement regime is specified.

- source: Eisenkopf, G., & Bächtiger, A. (2013). Mediation and Conflict Prevention. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a three-round common-pool resource game with two players, the addition of a third-party mediator who can both communicate and credibly threaten punishment leads to a substantial increase in group efficiency (combined payoff), with most groups achieving the efficient outcome. Communication alone (either direct or via mediator) increases efficiency relative to no mediation, but can also result in greater inequality if one player exploits the other's cooperation. Punishment alone, when costly and not paired with communication, does not improve efficiency. The effectiveness of the punishment-plus-communication treatment is robust to changes in the mediator's interests (impartial, biased, or no-stakes). The study provides direct evidence that communication and punishment are complementary tools for conflict prevention and efficiency in common-pool settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a repeated common-pool resource game with two players, enabling both communication (even indirect, via a mediator) and credible punishment substantially increases efficiency compared to control conditions (no mediation or only one tool enabled). The effect is robust to mediator bias and to the mediator's economic stake. For prediction tasks, the presence of both punishment and communication should be expected to yield much higher efficiency than control, while punishment alone (especially if costly) may not improve efficiency. The game is not a standard linear PGG but is a close variant with continuous extraction and a well-defined efficient outcome, making the findings highly relevant for similar environments.

- source: Cason, T. N., & Gangadharan, L. (2013). Empowering neighbors versus imposing regulations: An experimental analysis of pollution reduction schemes. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab experiment simulating a non-point source pollution problem with heterogeneous agents, four treatments were compared: baseline (no intervention), group tax only, punishment only, and group tax plus punishment. The group tax mechanism (formal regulation) led to large and statistically significant increases in efficiency (from ~55% in baseline to ~87% with tax, and ~90% with tax plus punishment). Peer punishment alone increased efficiency only slightly and not significantly. When combined, punishment reduced variance and helped keep pollution below the target more often, but the efficiency gain was offset by the deadweight loss from punishment costs. Punishment was used less frequently and less intensely than in standard public goods games, limiting its effectiveness. The results suggest that formal regulatory mechanisms are more effective than informal peer punishment in increasing efficiency, but peer punishment can play a complementary role.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a public-goods-like environment with a negative externality (pollution), enabling peer punishment alone yields only modest and inconsistent efficiency gains, while a formal group penalty (tax) mechanism yields large and robust efficiency improvements. Adding punishment to the group tax further reduces damages and increases the likelihood of meeting the target, but does not significantly increase efficiency due to the costliness of punishment. For prediction, the presence of a formal group penalty is the key driver of efficiency, and the marginal effect of enabling peer punishment is small or negligible unless punishment is much more frequent or severe than observed here. The game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment tech, group size, rounds, information) are well-documented, making this study highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in similar settings.

- source: De Geest, L. R., Stranlund, J. K., & Spraggon, J. M. (2017). Deterring poaching of a common pool resource. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing insiders to observe and punish outsiders (poachers) has a non-monotonic effect on efficiency. With zero monitoring (no punishment possible), outsiders show some restraint and insiders achieve moderate efficiency. With partial monitoring (randomly able to punish one outsider), poaching increases over time, sanctions are low and random, and efficiency for insiders drops to Nash equilibrium levels or below. With full monitoring (all outsiders observable and punishable), poaching is lower and more stable, insiders coordinate better, and efficiency is highest, though still far from the social optimum. Sanctions are generally insufficient to fully deter poaching. The results suggest that imperfect punishment opportunities can backfire, reducing efficiency, while full monitoring and punishment can improve but not maximize efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment in common-pool-resource-like games depends critically on the monitoring structure. If punishment is only partially possible (imperfect monitoring), efficiency can decrease relative to no punishment at all, due to increased poaching and poor coordination of sanctions. If punishment is fully enabled (full monitoring), efficiency increases relative to both the control and partial monitoring, but does not reach the social optimum. For prediction, the presence and effectiveness of punishment, the monitoring structure, and the ability to coordinate sanctions are crucial moderators. Control efficiency (no punishment) is not always a lower bound: enabling punishment can reduce efficiency if monitoring is imperfect. Game design dimensions such as punishment cost, punishment tech, group size, communication, and information structure (who sees what) are all relevant. The results caution against assuming that adding punishment always increases efficiency.

- source: Ozono, H., Jin, N., Watabe, M., & Shimizu, K. (2016). Solving the second-order free rider problem in a public goods game: An experiment using a leader support system. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when leaders are given the ability to punish followers using resources provided by follower support (support-present condition), both the frequency and effectiveness of punishment increase compared to a no-support condition. This leads to a higher probability of groups achieving high cooperation (as measured by contribution rates) and higher average profits for both leaders and followers, especially under 'linkage' punishment leaders who punish both non-cooperators and non-supporters. The no-support condition, where leaders have externally provided punishment resources, results in weaker punishment and lower group cooperation. The findings suggest that the structure of punishment funding and the type of leader punishment strategy are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling leaders to punish using resources provided by group members (rather than external resources) increases the efficiency of punishment in public goods games, as measured by group and individual profits and the frequency of high-cooperation groups. The effect is especially pronounced when leaders punish both first- and second-order free riders. For prediction tasks, the presence of a leader support system, the type of punishment strategy, and the source of punishment capital are key moderators. The control (no-support) condition provides a baseline for efficiency, and the support-present condition demonstrates a substantial positive shift in efficiency when punishment is enabled and properly incentivized.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2013). If Cooperation Is Likely Punish Mildly: Insights from Economic Experiments Based on the Snowdrift Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This study uses repeated group snowdrift games (a close variant of public goods games) with and without punishment to test the effects of mild versus severe punishment on cooperation and average payoff. In groups of five, with 25 rounds per session, the authors manipulate the cost-to-benefit ratio (r = 0.2 or 0.8) and the punishment fine (p = 2.0 or 4.0, with constant cost to punisher). When cooperation is likely (r = 0.2), both mild and severe punishment increase cooperation, but severe punishment does not further increase cooperation and reduces average payoff compared to mild punishment. When cooperation is unlikely (r = 0.8), only severe punishment substantially increases cooperation and average payoff; mild punishment is less effective and can even reduce average payoff compared to no punishment. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent: mild punishment is optimal when cooperation is already likely, while severe punishment is only justified in highly defection-prone environments.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the underlying difficulty of cooperation (cost-to-benefit ratio, analogous to MPCR in PGGs). If the control game (no punishment) already achieves high efficiency, adding severe punishment will not improve and may reduce efficiency; mild punishment is sufficient. If the control game has low efficiency (cooperation is rare), only severe punishment can increase efficiency. The cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as the baseline cooperation rate, are critical moderators. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency in snowdrift- or PGG-like games with peer punishment.

- source: Nair, A., Blome, C., Choi, T. Y., & Lee, G. (2018). Re-visiting collaborative behavior in supply networks structural embeddedness and the influence of contextual changes and sanctions. *Journal of Purchasing and Supply Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated triadic supply network game with real managers, the introduction of sanctions (punishment) reduces both group allocation and average payoffs when the incentive structure favors cooperation (i.e., low temptation to defect). When the incentive structure is changed to increase the temptation to defect, sanctions increase group allocation compared to the no-sanction condition, but average payoffs remain lower than in the cooperative baseline. Sanctions are only effective at increasing group allocation when the underlying incentive structure is unfavorable to cooperation. The study does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but average payoffs are provided for each condition. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus negative in cooperative contexts and ambiguous (possibly less negative) in defect-prone contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like network games depends critically on the underlying incentive structure. When the baseline (control) efficiency is high (cooperative context), enabling punishment reduces efficiency. When the baseline efficiency is low (defection is tempting), enabling punishment can increase group allocation but does not fully restore efficiency/payoff to cooperative levels. For prediction, this suggests that the marginal effect of punishment on efficiency is negative when control efficiency is high, and less negative or possibly weakly positive when control efficiency is low, but the overall efficiency with punishment rarely exceeds the cooperative baseline. The game design dimensions most relevant here are the payoff structure (temptation to defect), the presence and cost/effectiveness of punishment, and the information structure.

- source: Guillen, P., Schwieren, C., & Staffiero, G. (2007). Why feed the Leviathan?. *Public Choice*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that a centralized sanctioning mechanism (CSM) in a step-level public good game leads to a dramatic increase in the rate at which groups successfully provide the public good (i.e., reach the threshold). In the baseline (no CSM), groups almost never reach the threshold after a few rounds. With CSM, the threshold is reached in nearly 90% of rounds. After CSM is removed, groups with prior CSM experience continue to reach the threshold at much higher rates than baseline groups (AR: 65%, VR: 48%, BL: 0% in rounds 8-14). The effect is attributed to an 'educational' or coordination-facilitating role of CSM. Voting on CSM removal does not improve post-removal outcomes compared to automatic removal. Failures to reach the threshold during the CSM phase predict lower post-CSM success. The CSM is costly (3 units per player per round), so while it increases the likelihood of public good provision, it also reduces per-round payoffs when active. The paper does not directly report efficiency ratios, but the increased provision rate implies higher group welfare relative to baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a centralized punishment mechanism in a threshold (step-level) public good game increases the rate of successful public good provision, both during and after the punishment phase. The effect persists even after punishment is removed, supporting the idea of an 'educational' or coordination effect. The CSM is costly, so the net efficiency gain depends on the balance between increased public good provision and the cost of the mechanism. For prediction, the presence of a centralized punishment mechanism should be expected to substantially increase efficiency (proxied by provision rate) compared to a no-punishment baseline, especially in step-level games with coordination problems. The effect is robust to whether removal is automatic or by vote. However, the paper does not provide direct efficiency ratios, so predictions must use provision rate as a proxy.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & van Dijk, E. (2016). The impact of personal responsibility on the (un)willingness to punish non-cooperation and reward cooperation. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that people are more reluctant to punish non-cooperators when they feel personally responsible (i.e., when deciding individually), but this reluctance is attenuated when responsibility is shared in a group. This effect is specific to punishment and does not apply to rewarding cooperation. The effect is mediated by feelings of personal responsibility and entitlement, not by external accountability. The experiments use a common resource dilemma with sanctioning opportunities, but do not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs. Thus, the findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral moderators of punishment willingness, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the willingness to punish non-cooperation is higher when punishment decisions are made jointly by groups rather than by individuals, due to reduced personal responsibility and increased entitlement. However, since the study does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or common resource games. The results are useful for predicting when punishment will be more or less likely to be used, but not for predicting the efficiency consequences of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the structure of the punishment decision (individual vs. group) may moderate the behavioral use of punishment, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & van Dijk, E. (2014). On the willingness to costly reward cooperation and punish non-cooperation: The moderating role of type of social dilemma. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
