  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that enabling players to assign nonmonetary disapproval (sanction) points to others in a repeated minimum effort game leads to convergence on the efficient (payoff-dominant) equilibrium, with group earnings nearly doubling compared to the baseline. In contrast, enabling approval (reward) points does not improve efficiency and results in outcomes similar to or worse than the baseline. The effect of disapproval is not due to communication per se, but to the psychological impact of negative feedback. Sanctions are used more persistently and effectively than rewards, and their behavioral impact on raising effort is stronger. The findings are robust to initial group behavior and are supported by both aggregate and regression analyses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling nonmonetary, costless, and payoff-neutral sanctions (disapproval) in a repeated coordination game can dramatically increase group efficiency, even when the sanctions have no direct monetary effect. The effect is not observed for nonmonetary rewards (approval). For prediction tasks, the presence of a nonmonetary sanction mechanism (even if costless and payoff-neutral) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control with no such mechanism, while a nonmonetary reward mechanism may not. The game is a minimum effort game (not a standard PGG), so transfer to PGGs should be cautious but is likely informative for adjacent coordination settings.

- source: Kamei, K. (2020). Group size effect and over-punishment in the case of third party enforcement of social norms. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Third party punishment in a prisoner's dilemma is effective at deterring defection as group size increases, because aggregate punishment increases even though individual punishment per punisher decreases only mildly. However, with large groups (e.g., 10 third party punishers), over-punishment leads to severe efficiency losses: defectors' payoffs become negative, and cooperators' payoffs are also reduced due to perverse punishment. When third parties are allowed to coordinate punishment levels democratically, over-punishment is mitigated, and efficiency is restored. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is positive at moderate group sizes but negative at large group sizes without coordination.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on group size and the presence of coordination mechanisms. For small to moderate group sizes, enabling third party punishment increases efficiency by deterring defection. For large group sizes, punishment without coordination leads to over-punishment and large efficiency losses, reducing group payoffs below the social optimum. If coordination mechanisms (e.g., voting) are present, over-punishment is mitigated and efficiency is restored. Thus, when predicting treatment efficiency from game design, group size and the presence/absence of coordination mechanisms are key moderators. The control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the paper provides expected payoffs for both control and punishment conditions, allowing for estimation of efficiency changes.

- source: Xiao, E., & Kunreuther, H. (2016). Punishment and Cooperation in Stochastic Social Dilemmas. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In two-person repeated prisoner's dilemma games, both deterministic and stochastic (with outcome uncertainty), the restricted punishment mechanism (only cooperators can punish defectors) increases cooperation rates compared to no-punishment and unrestricted punishment. However, the efficiency gains (measured by average earnings) from restricted punishment are significant only in deterministic environments; in stochastic environments, restricted punishment does not significantly increase average earnings compared to no-punishment. Unrestricted punishment leads to frequent retaliation and antisocial punishment, resulting in lower average earnings than the baseline in both environments. The effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and efficiency is reduced by outcome uncertainty, as noncooperators are less likely to be punished when outcomes are stochastic.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on both the structure of the punishment mechanism (restricted vs unrestricted) and the presence of outcome uncertainty (stochastic vs deterministic). In games with two players, repeated for 10 rounds, with perfect information and no communication, restricted punishment can increase efficiency in deterministic settings but not in stochastic ones. Unrestricted punishment can reduce efficiency due to retaliation and antisocial punishment. For prediction, the presence of outcome uncertainty and the type of punishment mechanism are key moderators: restricted punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when outcomes are deterministic and less so when outcomes are stochastic. Control efficiency (no-punishment) is similar in both environments, but the efficiency boost from punishment is context-dependent.

- source: Ahmad, M. G., & Loch, C. (2020). What do the followers want? The core functions of leadership. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in repeated two-player strategic games, allowing players to choose a punishment option (where one can assign a low-value payoff to the other after decisions are made) does not increase group efficiency. Instead, punishment is associated with lower group payoffs due to retaliation cycles and reduced cooperation. In all games, the average group benefit under punishment is lower than under individual decision-making or leadership. Leadership (especially recommending leadership) increases efficiency, while punishment governance leads to the lowest efficiency. The negative effect of punishment on efficiency is robust across games with and without monetary incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player repeated games with public-goods-like social dilemmas, enabling punishment as a governance option (without central leadership) reduces group efficiency compared to control conditions without punishment. The effect is robust across different game structures and incentive types. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment option (with moderate cost and impact, as in this design) should be expected to decrease efficiency relative to control. The findings are directly relevant for settings where punishment is decentralized and not embedded in a leadership structure.

- source: Roux, C., & Thöni, C. (2015). Collusion among many firms: The disciplinary power of targeted punishment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The availability of targeted punishment in Cournot oligopoly experiments leads to significantly higher group profits and lower total output (closer to the collusive benchmark) for all group sizes tested (2, 4, 6, 8). The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive and increases with group size beyond duopoly: in 6- and 8-firm markets, mean profits nearly double with punishment. Punishment is used in a targeted and proportional way, with defectors facing harsher penalties as group size increases. The study provides direct evidence that targeted punishment can sustain high-efficiency collusion even in large groups, contrary to standard theory and previous experimental results without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that enabling targeted punishment in repeated Cournot (oligopoly) games leads to large increases in group efficiency (profit), especially as group size increases. The design is not a standard public goods game but is closely related in structure and in the punishment mechanism. The results suggest that, for games with similar punishment technology (high impact, targeted, known cost), the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be much higher than in the control (no-punishment) condition, and the effect is larger for larger groups. The findings are highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions, especially for settings with targeted, high-impact punishment and no communication.

- source: Moser, S., & Musshoff, O. (2016). Ex-ante Evaluation of Policy Measures: Effects of Reward and Punishment for Fertiliser Reduction in Palm Oil Production. *Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, holding expected income effects constant, the design of incentives (reward vs. punishment, certain vs. uncertain) significantly affects both behavioral response and efficiency. Certain rewards are the most efficient (i.e., minimize profit loss for a given reduction in fertiliser use), while uncertain punishments are the least efficient. Certain punishments and uncertain rewards are intermediate. Punishment is less efficient than reward, and uncertainty reduces efficiency. The findings suggest that policymakers aiming for efficient reduction of negative externalities should prefer certain rewards over punishments or uncertain incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a public-good-like field context, the efficiency impact of punishment (vs. reward) depends strongly on the design: punishment is less efficient than reward, and uncertainty in the incentive (probabilistic punishment or reward) further reduces efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment (especially uncertain punishment) will not increase efficiency as much as enabling certain rewards, even if the expected value is held constant. The results are most applicable to field settings with individual decision-making and externalities, but less so to classic multi-player PGGs with peer punishment. The efficiency outcome is measured as profit difference, directly supporting payoff-based prediction.

- source: Grimalda, G., Pondorfer, A., & Tracer, D. P. (2016). Social image concerns promote cooperation more than altruistic punishment. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a one-shot, two-player prisoner's dilemma, the presence of a local authority (Big Man) as an observer increases cooperation and leads to the highest average payoffs, exceeding baseline. Introducing a costly punishment stage (where players can spend to reduce the other's payoff) does not increase cooperation above the social image condition and results in lower average payoffs than baseline, due to the costs incurred by both punisher and punished. When both social image and punishment are present, efficiency is still lower than with social image alone and remains below baseline. The presence of an external (outgroup) authority does not increase cooperation or payoffs. Thus, punishment is not an efficient mechanism for promoting cooperation in this setting, and social image concerns are more effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a one-shot, two-player PD with costly punishment, enabling punishment reduces efficiency compared to baseline (no punishment). The effect is robust: both punishment alone and punishment combined with social image concerns yield lower payoffs than baseline. The design is not a standard public goods game but is closely related (two-player PD with all-or-nothing contribution). The findings suggest that, for similar game designs (one-shot, small group, all-or-nothing, high punishment cost), enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency, even if it increases cooperation rates. The control (baseline) efficiency is higher than the punishment treatment efficiency. This evidence should be weighted heavily when predicting the effect of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Wilson, A. J., & Wu, H. (2017). At-will relationships: How an option to walk away affects cooperation and efficiency. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Introducing a termination (walk-away) option in repeated PD games increases cooperation and efficiency when the outside option is low, as termination is rarely used and serves as a credible threat. However, as the outside option rises above the in-relationship minmax, subjects begin to use termination more frequently as a punishment, leading to a sharp drop in efficiency. If the outside option increases further, efficiency rises again, as the cost of termination is less severe. Asymmetric division rules for termination payoffs have dramatic effects: if the party who terminates gets the higher payoff, almost all relationships dissolve immediately, resulting in very low efficiency. If the more cooperative party is rewarded, cooperation and efficiency are maximized. The paper provides direct evidence on how the structure and value of punishment options (termination) affect efficiency in repeated social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment (here, the option to terminate a relationship) on efficiency is highly sensitive to the value and distribution of the punishment's payoff. When punishment is costly (low outside option), its mere availability increases efficiency, but if it becomes too attractive (high outside option), it is overused and efficiency drops. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the presence and value of the punishment option, the symmetry/asymmetry of its payoff, and the underlying game structure (here, repeated PD with imperfect monitoring). The findings suggest that the efficiency impact of enabling punishment depends non-monotonically on the cost/benefit of punishment, and that institutional details (who gets what on punishment/termination) can have very large effects. For prediction, the control efficiency (without punishment) and the specific design of the punishment option are critical moderators.

- source: Köster, R., Hadfield-Menell, D., Everett, R., Weidinger, L., Hadfield, G. K., & Leibo, J. Z. (2022). Spurious normativity enhances learning of compliance and enforcement behavior in artificial agents. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: Introducing a punishment regime for norm violations (e.g., eating a poisonous berry) enables agents to learn to avoid harmful actions more quickly, resulting in higher group efficiency (collective return) than in the absence of punishment. Adding a second, arbitrary 'silly' rule (punishment for eating a harmless berry) further increases group efficiency during the learning process, as it provides more opportunities for agents to practice enforcement and compliance, leading to faster and more robust avoidance of genuinely harmful actions. The effect is strongest during the intermediate learning phase, with the silly rule condition yielding the highest group payoffs. The study demonstrates that third-party punishment, even when applied to arbitrary rules, can enhance the emergence of efficient, norm-compliant behavior in artificial agent societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a multiagent environment with norm violations increases group efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust even when punishment is costly and decentralized. The design is not a standard public goods game, but the core mechanism—costly third-party punishment of norm violations—closely parallels PGG punishment treatments. The results suggest that, for similar environments, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency, especially when the environment presents a credit assignment problem (delayed or opaque consequences of harmful actions). The effect is further enhanced if the environment provides more opportunities for agents to practice enforcement (e.g., via additional, even arbitrary, punishable actions). The findings are most directly applicable to settings where learning and credit assignment are challenging, and where punishment is visible and incentivized. The results are less directly transferable to standard PGGs with immediate, transparent payoffs, but the qualitative direction (punishment increases efficiency) is likely to hold.

- source: Bohnet, I., & Baytelman, Y. (2007). Institutions and trust - Implications for preferences, beliefs and behavior. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional constraints, including a post-play punishment option, increase trustors' willingness to send money and trustees' willingness to return money in a trust game scenario, compared to an anonymous one-shot game. This suggests that efficiency (total group payoff) is higher with punishment. However, the presence of punishment and other institutional constraints reduces the role of intrinsic motivation (social orientation) in driving trust, supporting the 'crowding out' hypothesis. For trustworthiness, the effect of punishment is indirect: trustees return more because they expect to receive more, not because their intrinsic motivation changes. The study uses a within-subject design with senior executives and hypothetical payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that adding a punishment option to a trust game increases the amounts sent and returned, which is a proxy for increased efficiency. While the design is not a public goods game, the trust game with punishment is adjacent and the direction of effect is likely to generalize to PGG-like settings: punishment increases efficiency-related outcomes. However, the paper also highlights that the mechanism is not an increase in intrinsic motivation, but rather a shift in beliefs and expectations. For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment in a similar game design will increase efficiency, especially when the baseline (control) efficiency is low. The effect is robust across several institutional manipulations, but the crowding out of intrinsic motivation may have long-term implications not captured by short-run efficiency.

- source: Friebel, G., Kosfeld, M., & Thielmann, G. (2019). Trust the Police? Self-Selection of Motivated Agents into the German Police Force. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Police applicants in Germany are more trustworthy (return more in the trust game) and invest more in costly third-party punishment and reward than comparable high school students. The difference in trust is explained by risk preferences, but the difference in trustworthiness and norm enforcement remains significant after controlling for covariates. The willingness to punish and reward is measured as the average amount invested in these actions as a third party in a trust-game-based scenario. The study provides evidence for positive self-selection of intrinsically motivated, norm-enforcing individuals into the police force. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, and thus does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that individuals who self-select into enforcement roles (police applicants) are more willing to engage in costly punishment and reward to enforce cooperation norms. However, the experimental design is not a public goods game or a repeated social dilemma, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the motivation to punish and enforce norms, but do not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoffs in public-goods-game-like environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as indirect evidence about the types of individuals who may be more likely to use punishment, rather than as evidence about the efficiency consequences of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Zhao, K., Ferguson, E., & Smillie, L. D. (2017). Politeness and Compassion Differentially Predict Adherence to Fairness Norms and Interventions to Norm Violations in Economic Games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that politeness and honesty-humility predict fair allocations in the dictator game, while compassion predicts third-party recompensation (helping the victim) and, to a lesser extent, third-party punishment (punishing the offender). Most third-party punishers are 'prosocial' (they also behave fairly as dictators), and both punishment and recompensation increase with the degree of unfairness observed. However, the study does not analyze or report group efficiency, total welfare, or surplus, and the game structure is not a public goods game but a dictator/third-party punishment/recompensation design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it offers insight into the psychological predictors of punishment and recompensation behaviors in third-party games, it does not report group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze public goods game design dimensions such as MPCR, player count, or rounds. Its findings are only indirectly relevant for understanding the motivations behind punishment and helping behaviors, not their impact on group efficiency.

- source: MOLM, L. D. (1994). IS PUNISHMENT EFFECTIVE - COERCIVE STRATEGIES IN SOCIAL-EXCHANGE. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment is effective in increasing compliance (frequency of rewarding the punisher) when it is used frequently, contingently, and with high magnitude. The combination of high probability and high magnitude of punishment produces the highest compliance. Retaliation by the target does not increase with more frequent or stronger punishment, and negative affect toward the punisher is actually lower when punishment is strong and consistent, due to the resulting mutually rewarding exchanges. The effects are robust to the target's ability to retaliate and to variations in the probability and contingency of punishment. However, the main outcome is not group efficiency or total payoff, but the frequency of compliance in a dyadic exchange. The results challenge classical exchange and bargaining theories that predict punishment provokes retaliation and reduces cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in dyadic reciprocal exchange settings with both reward and punishment options, frequent and contingent punishment increases compliance (rewarding behavior) by the target, without increasing retaliation or negative affect. However, because the primary outcome is not group efficiency or total payoff, and the setting is not a public goods game or direct variant, the findings should be transferred to PGG-like environments with caution. The results suggest that, under conditions of high punishment frequency, contingency, and magnitude, punishment can be effective at increasing the target's prosocial behavior, but the implications for group efficiency in multi-person public goods games remain indirect. The paper is most informative about the behavioral mechanisms of punishment rather than direct efficiency outcomes.

- source: Rauhut, H. (2009). HIGHER PUNISHMENT, LESS CONTROL? EXPERIMENTAL EVIDENCE ON THE INSPECTION GAME. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing the strength of punishment reduces both the rate of inspection (control) and the rate of theft (crime), contrary to the strict game-theoretic prediction that only inspection should decrease. The effect on inspection is strong and robust, while the effect on theft is also significant but not as strong as predicted by standard theory. The behavioral adaptation to changes in punishment is slower than predicted by rational learning models. The main outcomes are theft and inspection rates; efficiency or group welfare is not directly measured, but the design ensures that theft is inefficient (gamma = 0.5). Inspectees lose money over time, while inspectors' earnings are roughly stable. The findings suggest that punishment has mixed effects on strategic behavior in inspection settings, and that human adaptation is slower and less complete than predicted by theory.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the behavioral effects of punishment in an inspection game with inefficient theft, which is structurally adjacent to public goods games. The main outcomes are theft and inspection rates, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. However, since theft is inefficient (gamma < 1), reductions in theft due to higher punishment would increase group efficiency, while reductions in inspection (which is costly) could also increase efficiency if inspection is not needed. The paper shows that higher punishment reduces both theft and inspection, suggesting that the net effect on efficiency would depend on the relative magnitudes of these changes and the cost structure. The design and findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is formal, inspection is costly, and theft is inefficient. The lack of direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes limits the ability to calibrate quantitative predictions, but the qualitative direction of effects (higher punishment reduces both crime and control) is informative for adjacent settings.

- source: Whitson, J. A., Wang, C. S., See, Y. H. M., Baker, W. E., & Murnighan, J. K. (2015). How, when, and why recipients and observers reward good deeds and punish bad deeds. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party observers are more likely to punish bad deeds (e.g., deception) than direct recipients, and that direct recipients are more likely to reward good deeds (e.g., honesty) than observers. These effects are mediated by feelings of obligation: observers feel a stronger obligation to punish, while recipients feel a stronger obligation to reward. The studies use one-shot, controlled scenarios with real monetary stakes, but do not involve repeated interaction, group provision, or public goods. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward assigned), not group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and behavioral drivers of punishment and reward in one-shot, dyadic or triadic settings, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that the presence of observers may increase the likelihood and intensity of punishment for norm violations, but do not inform how this affects group payoffs or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly about who is likely to punish and under what circumstances, not about the payoff consequences of punishment.

- source: Bone, J. E., Wallace, B., Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2016). Power Asymmetries and Punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma with Variable Cooperative Investment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma with variable cooperative investment and a punishment option, punishment did not increase the likelihood that targets would increase their cooperation, regardless of whether the punisher was strong or weak, or whether the game was symmetric or asymmetric. Both weak and strong players retaliated against punishment, and the threat of punishment from strong players may have led to higher baseline cooperation from weak players, but actual punishment had no effect on subsequent cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral responses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in two-player games with variable investment and a punishment option, enabling punishment does not increase cooperation or efficiency, even when punishment is more effective for some players (power asymmetry). However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The behavioral results suggest that, in similar two-player designs, enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency and may provoke retaliation. The findings are most relevant for adjacent game designs (iterated PD with variable investment) and may be less transferable to standard public goods games or multiplayer settings.

- source: Brosig, J., Weimann, J., & Yang, C. L. (2004). Communication, reputation, and punishment in sequential bargaining experiments. *Journal of Institutional and Theoretical Economics-zeitschrift Fur Die Gesamte Staatswissenschaft*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, without communication, the efficiency of punishment and the opportunity cost for the first mover significantly affect the likelihood of punishment and the first mover's choices, respectively. However, almost all second movers exploit the first mover's cooperative move, and equal splits are rare. When preplay communication is allowed, especially face-to-face, there is a dramatic increase in the number of equal splits (efficient outcomes), and the influence of punishment efficiency and opportunity cost largely disappears. E-mail communication also increases equal splits, but less so than face-to-face. The effect of communication cannot be fully explained by reputation. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the behavioral shift toward equal splits with communication suggests higher efficiency in those treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that preplay communication (especially face-to-face) is a powerful moderator that increases the likelihood of efficient, equal-split outcomes in sequential bargaining games with punishment and reward options. In the absence of communication, the efficiency of punishment and the opportunity cost for the first mover are important determinants of behavior, but do not lead to efficient outcomes. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and type of communication should be considered a key dimension, and that enabling communication can substantially increase efficiency, even when punishment is available. However, the results are from a sequential bargaining game, not a standard public goods game, so direct quantitative predictions for PGGs should be made with caution.

- source: Bone, J. E., Wallace, B., Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2015). The Effect of Power Asymmetries on Cooperation and Punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma with punishment, punishment did not increase cooperation rates. Instead, cooperation from a partner was more effective at eliciting cooperation than punishment. Punishment, especially from strong players, sometimes provoked retaliation and further defection. Weak players never retaliated against punishment, but both weak and strong players retaliated against strong punishers. Players were more likely to opt out after being punished or when facing a strong partner. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, and the authors explicitly state their focus is on behavioral rather than payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma games with punishment, enabling punishment does not increase cooperation and may even have detrimental effects on cooperative behavior, especially when power asymmetries exist. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings suggest that, at least in two-player, all-or-nothing, binary-choice games with possible opting out, punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency and may worsen behavioral outcomes. Caution should be used in generalizing to multi-player or continuous-contribution public goods games.

- source: Makovi, K., Sargsyan, A., Li, W. D., Bonnefon, J. F., & Rahwan, T. (2023). Trust within human-machine collectives depends on the perceived consensus about cooperative norms. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a third-party punishment game followed by a trust game, humans earn less trust by sharing with or punishing bots than with humans. Trust-gain is attenuated but not eliminated in mixed human-bot collectives. Perceived consensus about cooperative norms predicts trust-gain, and experimentally making norm consensus salient increases trust-gain in some conditions. The main outcomes are trust and norm perception, not group efficiency or total payoff. The design is adjacent to public goods games, but the outcome focus is on trust and norm-following rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its design is adjacent (third-party punishment game with binary sharing), and the main outcomes are trust-gain and norm perception, not efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that punishment can increase trust in norm-followers, especially when norm consensus is salient, but do not quantify effects on group efficiency. The paper may inform mechanism design around norm salience and trust, but is not directly informative for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., Rijkhoff, S. A. M., & van Dijk, E. (2013). Equality as a benchmark for third-party punishment and reward: The moderating role of uncertainty in social dilemmas. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishers and rewarders use the equality rule as a benchmark for sanctioning decisions only when the size of the common resource is certain. Under certainty, those who take more than an equal share are punished much more, and those who take an equal or smaller share are rewarded more. Under uncertainty, the amount of punishment or reward is linearly related to the amount harvested, with no sharp benchmark. The emotional basis for punishment is negative affect (anger), while for reward it is positive affect. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or common resource games, as it does not report any payoff-based outcomes. However, it provides informative context about how the clarity of the cooperation norm (equality rule) and environmental uncertainty affect the application of punishment and reward. In settings where the cooperation norm is clear and known, punishment is more sharply targeted at norm violators; under uncertainty, punishment is more diffuse. This may indirectly inform predictions about the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation or efficiency, but the paper does not provide direct evidence for such effects.

- source: Ibanez, M., & Martinsson, P. (2013). Curbing coca cultivation in Colombia - A framed field experiment. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that both increased risk of punishment (eradication) and increased legal profits (subsidies) reduce investment in illegal coca cultivation, but the effect is highly heterogeneous. About one third of participants have high moral costs and do not need external incentives, while two fifths are very hard to deter. Only about a quarter are responsive to policy levers. The supply of coca is inelastic to policy changes unless eradication risk is high (>30%). Carrots (subsidies) are more effective in areas with better market integration, while sticks (punishment) are more effective in depressed areas. The study provides cost estimates for both policies and suggests that optimal policy should mix both, tailored to local conditions. However, the main outcomes are behavioral (investment choices), not group efficiency or total welfare as in standard PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence on how exogenous punishment (risk of eradication) and reward (subsidy) policies affect individual investment in a socially harmful activity, with a design adjacent to public goods games. The findings support that punishment can reduce undesirable behavior, but the effect is highly dependent on individual heterogeneity. The results are most useful for predicting how changes in punishment and reward parameters might affect behavioral outcomes (e.g., investment in the public bad), but less directly for predicting group efficiency as defined in PGGs. The cost-effectiveness analysis and elasticity estimates can inform models that link punishment/reward design to behavioral and, indirectly, payoff outcomes, especially in field-like or framed settings with real-world stakes.

- source: Guo, Z. Y., Guo, R., Xu, C. Y., & Wu, Z. (2022). Reflexive or reflective? Group bias in third-party punishment in Chinese and Western cultures. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both Chinese and Western participants show ingroup favoritism in third-party punishment under reflexive (fast, high cognitive load) conditions, punishing ingroup norm violators less than outgroup violators. Under reflective (slow, low cognitive load) conditions, Western participants—especially those with high group identity—reduce ingroup favoritism and may even punish ingroup violators more, while Chinese participants continue to show strong ingroup favoritism regardless of decision mode. Group identity amplifies these effects. The main outcome is the amount of punishment assigned, not efficiency or group payoff. The game is a third-party punishment mini-dictator game, not a standard public goods game, and does not report on group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how group identity, culture, and decision-making mode moderate the behavioral use of costly punishment in a third-party punishment context. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is adjacent to PGGs (mini-dictator/TPP), and the outcome is punishment behavior, not payoff. The findings are useful for understanding when and how punishment is used, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Galbiati, R., Schlag, K. H., & van der Weele, J. J. (2013). Sanctions that signal: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that exogenous sanctions increase effort and beliefs about others' effort, with a stronger effect on effort than beliefs. Endogenous sanctions, where a principal chooses to sanction after observing first-round behavior, are less effective, especially for subjects who initially chose high effort. For low initial effort players, both types of sanctions have similar effects. The signaling effect—where the imposition of a sanction is interpreted as a signal that others are not cooperating—can counteract the incentive effect of sanctions for cooperators. The main analysis is on effort and beliefs, not on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that sanctions can increase effort in a minimum-effort game, but the effect depends on whether sanctions are exogenous or endogenous and on subjects' prior behavior. The signaling effect can reduce the effectiveness of sanctions for cooperators. For prediction, this suggests that the context and perceived informational content of punishment mechanisms may moderate their effect on efficiency, especially in coordination settings. However, because efficiency is not directly measured, the findings should be used as informative but indirect evidence.

- source: Bigoni, M., Camera, G., & Casari, M. (2013). Strategies of cooperation and punishment among students and clerical workers. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing personal punishment increases cooperation rates in indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games for both students and workers. Students show a larger increase in cooperation and are more likely to adopt conditional cooperation strategies (e.g., forgiving or unforgiving trigger strategies) when punishment is available, while workers shift more toward unconditional cooperation. Workers are less likely to use personal punishment than students. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from the data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing personal punishment increases cooperation rates in indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games, with the effect being more pronounced among student subjects. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates and strategy adoption, not efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games (pairwise PDs in groups of four with random matching), so caution is needed in generalizing to standard PGGs.

- source: Li, X. H., Molleman, L., & van Dolder, D. (2021). Do descriptive social norms drive peer punishment? Conditional punishment strategies and their impact on cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that individuals' punishment of defectors is conditional on descriptive social norms: people punish more when cooperation or punishment is more common in a reference group. There is substantial heterogeneity: some punish independently, some increase punishment with higher cooperation (norm enforcement), and some with higher punishment (conformist). The dynamic model demonstrates that these conditional punishment strategies can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of high cooperation rates in populations. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment behavior is conditional on social context, and that such conditional punishment can promote cooperation in repeated social dilemmas. However, because the experiment is a one-shot prisoner's dilemma with punishment and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled in public goods games. The dynamic model suggests that conditional punishment can help sustain high cooperation rates, which may imply higher efficiency in analogous settings, but this is an indirect inference. The paper is most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and heterogeneity in punishment, and for modeling the potential for punishment to support cooperation, but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Fabbri, M., & Carbonara, E. (2017). Social influence on third-party punishment: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that social information about peer punishment decisions leads third-party punishers to revise their own punishment levels toward the peer average. This effect is robust and stronger when conformity requires increasing punishment, even though this reduces the punisher's own payoff. The addition of a normative social influence channel (emoticon feedback) does not significantly change punishment behavior compared to informational social influence alone. The study does not report or analyze efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral conformity in punishment decisions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that social information about peer punishment can substantially alter third-party punishment behavior, increasing conformity to peer norms. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public-goods-like environments. The design is a modified dictator game with third-party punishment, not a public goods game, and the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, conformity), not efficiency. The findings may be relevant for understanding how social information moderates punishment behavior, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions.

- source: Spadaro, G., Molho, C., Van Prooijen, J. W., Romano, A., Mosso, C. O., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2023). Corrupt third parties undermine trust and prosocial behaviour between people. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across five experimental studies, participants exposed to a corrupt (dishonest) third-party punisher (TPP) trusted strangers less and cooperated less in subsequent economic games (trust game, prisoner's dilemma, dictator game) with TPP present. The effect is robust to both second-hand and first-hand exposure to corruption, and to whether the TPP's corruption harms only the experiment or also a collective good. The negative effect of perceived TPP corruption on cooperation is mediated by reduced interpersonal trust. The studies do not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect of TPP corruption on efficiency is not measured. The design always includes a TPP; there is no comparison to a no-punishment baseline, so the effect of enabling punishment per se on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the perceived integrity of a third-party punisher moderates the effectiveness of punishment institutions in promoting trust and cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment (vs. no punishment) on group efficiency or payoff, nor does it report efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that if punishment is administered by corrupt or untrusted third parties, its positive effect on cooperation may be undermined or even reversed, but the paper does not quantify efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper is most informative about the moderating role of institutional trustworthiness, not about the baseline effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.

- source: Zhang, Y., Yu, H. B., Yin, Y., & Zhou, X. L. (2016). Intention Modulates the Effect of Punishment Threat in Norm Enforcement via the Lateral Orbitofrontal Cortex. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that the intention behind punishment threat critically shapes norm compliance: when a partner intentionally retains the right to punish, participants allocate less to the partner (reduced norm compliance), but when the partner intentionally waives the right, participants allocate more (increased norm compliance and positive affect). When the punishment threat is unintentional (computer-decided), these effects are weaker or absent. The rLOFC is shown to mediate these effects, as brain stimulation modulates the behavioral response to intention. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the game is a modified dictator game rather than a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the social context and perceived intention behind punishment can strongly affect norm compliance (proxied by allocation), but does not report efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that in environments where punishment is perceived as self-serving or intentional, punishment may reduce cooperation or norm compliance, while unintentional or third-party punishment may be less detrimental or even neutral. For prediction, this implies that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may depend on whether punishment is perceived as legitimate or self-interested, but the lack of efficiency data and the use of a dictator game limit direct applicability to public goods game efficiency predictions.

- source: Chen, H. Z., Zeng, Z. J., & Ma, J. H. (2020). The source of punishment matters: Third-party punishment restrains observers from selfish behaviors better than does second-party punishment by shaping norm perceptions. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment (TPP) of unfair behavior in a dictator game leads observers to perceive higher descriptive and injunctive norms for sharing, and to share more themselves, compared to second-party punishment (SPP). Mediation analyses show that the effect on sharing is primarily through changes in descriptive norm perception. The effect is robust across two experiments with different manipulations (verbal blame and monetary punishment). However, the outcome is individual sharing behavior, not group efficiency or payoff, and the context is a dictator game, not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the source of punishment (third-party vs. second-party) can affect observers' prosocial behavior by shaping norm perceptions, especially descriptive norms. While the findings are robust for sharing behavior in dictator games, they do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. The results suggest that punishment mechanisms that are perceived as norm-expressive (e.g., third-party punishment) may have stronger effects on prosocial behavior, but the transferability to efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is indirect and should be treated with caution.

- source: D'Orsogna, M. R., Kendall, R., McBride, M., & Short, M. B. (2013). Criminal Defectors Lead to the Emergence of Cooperation in an Experimental, Adversarial Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that the availability of the Informant strategy (criminals who also report/punish other criminals) is necessary for the emergence and stability of high cooperation in an adversarial game. When Informants are allowed, the system reliably transitions to and maintains a high-cooperation state (Utopia), regardless of initial conditions or parameter profile. When Informants are not allowed, the system converges to high defection (Dystopia). The results are robust across different initializations, parameter profiles (costs of reporting), and levels of behavioral inertia. However, the study does not report group payoffs, efficiency, or welfare as outcome variables.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the availability of a punishment mechanism (specifically, the Informant strategy) is crucial for the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in adversarial games. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral strategies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The game is adjacent to PGGs, and the behavioral results suggest that enabling punishment (especially by defectors) can transform a high-defection environment into a high-cooperation one, which would likely increase efficiency if measured. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of this inference.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2021). Moral labels increase cooperation and costly punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment option. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that moral framing (using labels like 'I cooperate' and 'I cheat') increases the likelihood of both moral and hypocritical punishment compared to neutral framing ('A' and 'B'). In Experiment 1, where only participants could punish, moral framing increased punishment but did not affect cooperation. In Experiment 2, where both participants and partners could punish, moral framing increased both punishment and cooperation. Antisocial punishment and general punishment bias were not affected by framing. The results suggest that the effect of moral framing on cooperation is contingent on the presence of punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes as a function of punishment, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and framing of punishment options can increase both punishment behavior and, under certain conditions, cooperation in a Prisoner's Dilemma game. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage, but the findings about behavioral responses to punishment and framing may be informative for predicting when punishment will increase cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) in similar environments. The results suggest that punishment is more likely to increase cooperation when it is symmetric (i.e., both players can punish), and that moral framing amplifies this effect.

- source: Tan, F. F., & Xiao, E. (2018). Third-party punishment: Retribution or deterrence?. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that group third parties are more likely to approve punishment for deterrence purposes (when their decisions are revealed before the PD game), while individual third parties' punishment decisions are not sensitive to whether punishment can deter. Groups are less likely than individuals to approve punishment overall, but the magnitude of punishment (when approved) is similar. Despite the presence of third-party punishment, cooperation rates in the PD game remain low across all treatments, and punishment is not sufficient to change the game into one where cooperation is the dominant strategy for most participants. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that the punishment institution as implemented does not substantially increase cooperation or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the behavioral effects of third-party punishment in a one-shot PD game, especially regarding the motives for punishment and the difference between group and individual third parties. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The findings suggest that punishment (even when costless to the third party) is not always used to maximize cooperation or group welfare, and that group decision-making may lead to more deterrence-driven but less frequent punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of third-party punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency or cooperation, especially if punishment is not strong enough to change incentives. The design is adjacent to PGGs but not a direct variant.

- source: Gummerum, M., López-Pérez, B., Van Dijk, E., & Van Dillen, L. F. (2022). Ire and punishment: Incidental anger and costly punishment in children, adolescents, and adults. *Journal of Experimental Child Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that experimentally induced incidental anger increases costly punishment of unfair offers in the ultimatum game (second-party punishment) for children, adolescents, and adults. In third-party punishment games, incidental anger increases punishment severity for unfair offers in adults and adolescents, but not in children. The effect of anger is specific to unfair (unequal) offers and does not increase punishment of fair (equal) offers. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment rates and severity), not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the motivation for costly punishment in response to unfairness is influenced by emotional state and developmental stage, with self-relevance and norm appraisals playing key roles.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or related games. Its main contribution is to show that emotional state (incidental anger) can increase costly punishment of unfairness, especially when the violation is self-relevant (second-party) or, for adults, when it violates fairness norms (third-party). For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that punishment rates and severity can be modulated by emotional and developmental factors, but it does not inform how these changes in punishment behavior translate into changes in efficiency or group payoff. The game designs used (ultimatum and third-party punishment games) are adjacent to, but not the same as, standard public goods games, and no efficiency or welfare outcomes are reported.

- source: Bone, J. E., & Raihani, N. J. (2015). Human punishment is motivated by both a desire for revenge and a desire for equality. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment is motivated by both a desire for revenge (punishment occurs even when it cannot restore equality) and a desire for equality (when possible, punishment is tailored to equalize payoffs). Punishment is more likely when stealing creates disadvantageous inequality and when punishment is effective. However, the outcomes measured are behavioral (punishment frequency and amount), not efficiency or group payoff. The experiment is a two-player, one-shot, anonymous game with no communication, and does not use a public goods game or its direct variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about how punishment behavior is shaped by the effectiveness of punishment and the presence of inequality, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar multi-player settings. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment will be used and how it will be targeted, especially regarding the importance of punishment effectiveness (fee-to-fine ratio) and inequality, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not report efficiency or welfare, so its value for the prediction task is limited to qualitative insights about punishment motivation and targeting.

- source: Engel, C., Goerg, S. J., & Yu, G. N. (2016). Symmetric vs. Asymmetric Punishment Regimes for Collusive Bribery. *American Law and Economics Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that when punishment is asymmetric (bribers punished less than recipients), bribers are much more likely to report to authorities if recipients accept the bribe but do not grant the favor. This threat is anticipated by recipients, who are then more likely to grant the favor, and bribers are more likely to offer bribes. These effects are robust across countries (Germany and China) and to framing (neutral vs. corruption). The main outcomes are behavioral (bribe offers, reporting, granting favors), not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that asymmetric punishment, while intended to deter corruption, may actually facilitate collusive bribery by making enforcement of corrupt deals easier.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that lowering the cost of punishment (making it cheaper for one party to punish another) increases the use of punishment and, in this context, increases the frequency of cooperation in corrupt deals. However, because the game is not a public goods game or direct variant, and because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The main implication is that punishment cost and effectiveness are critical moderators of behavioral outcomes, but the translation to efficiency is not directly tested here.

- source: FeldmanHall, O., Otto, A. R., & Phelps, E. A. (2018). Learning Moral Values: Another's Desire to Punish Enhances One's Own Punitive Behavior. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across four experiments, participants who learned about another's strong preference for punishment increased their own endorsement of punitive options when responding to unfair offers. This effect was observed both when participants actively implemented another's preferences and when they passively observed them. The effect generalized across online and lab settings, and from a justice game (with compensatory and punitive options) to the ultimatum game (where only costly punishment is available). The learning process is best described by a reinforcement learning model sensitive to the magnitude of unfairness. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare; the focus is on individual behavioral change in punitive choices.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that exposure to others' punitive preferences increases individuals' own willingness to punish in response to unfairness, even when punishment is costly. However, it does not report on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is dyadic and focused on justice restoration, not multi-player public goods provision. The findings are most relevant for predicting changes in punitive behavior, not efficiency.

- source: Meier, S., Pierce, L., Vaccaro, A., & La Cara, B. (2016). Trust and in-group favoritism in a culture of crime. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment mechanisms increase cooperation rates in one-shot prisoner's dilemma games in both high- and low-Mafia neighborhoods, but do not close the gap in trust or cooperation between the two. In high-Mafia schools, punishment intensifies in-group favoritism, leading to higher cooperation with in-group members but not with out-group members. In low-Mafia schools, punishment does not increase in-group favoritism and may even reduce it. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined. The main outcomes are behavioral: trust, cooperation, and in-group bias.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing punishment in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma increases cooperation rates, but the effect on efficiency is not reported. The design is adjacent to a public goods game (binary PD, not multi-person PGG), so results may partially transfer. The effect of punishment is context-dependent: in environments with strong in-group norms (high-Mafia), punishment increases in-group favoritism and does not resolve overall trust/cooperation deficits. For prediction tasks, this suggests that punishment may increase cooperation rates but not necessarily efficiency, especially in settings with strong parochial norms. The absence of efficiency or group payoff data limits direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Sun, K. T., Liu, Y., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2022). Evolution of trust in a hierarchical population with punishing investors. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that exogenously imposed collective punishment in a binary trust game significantly increases trustworthiness (from 38.5% to 82.3%) and somewhat increases trust (from 28.1% to 39.6%). When the adoption of the punishment mechanism is determined by majority vote, most subjects vote against it, and the endogenous adoption does not further increase trust or trustworthiness compared to exogenous imposition. Cognitive ability predicts voting for punishment. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff outcomes, and the main focus is on behavioral measures (trust, trustworthiness, voting).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that collective punishment can increase trustworthiness and trust in a binary trust game, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it is a binary trust game rather than a multi-player PGG. The punishment mechanism is collective and endogenous in some treatments, but the main outcomes are behavioral. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGG-like environments, this paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can improve cooperative behavior, but does not allow direct inference about efficiency effects. The absence of efficiency or payoff data limits its direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Sun, C. B., & Luo, C. (2020). Co-evolution of influence-based preferential selection and limited resource with multi-games on interdependent networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that female water point leaders (WPLs) in rural Namibia are younger and better educated than male WPLs, but are often related to traditional elites. In a trust game with third-party punishment, female WPLs punish less frequently (not statistically significant) and significantly less intensively than male WPLs. However, these differences in punishment behavior do not translate into differences in villager satisfaction with leaders. The experiment does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on gender differences in third-party punishment in a trust game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It shows that female leaders punish less, but this does not affect satisfaction. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The design and findings may be useful for understanding how leader characteristics affect norm enforcement, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Schroeder, K. B., Pepper, G. V., & Nettle, D. (2014). Local norms of cheating and the cultural evolution of crime and punishment: a study of two urban neighborhoods. *Peerj*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a 3PP game, residents of a high-crime, low-trust neighborhood (B) are more likely to steal and less likely to punish theft than those in a low-crime, high-trust neighborhood (A). Perceived cheating (descriptive norm violation) is higher in B and predicts both more theft and less expectation of punishment. Experimentally manipulating perceived norms in B increases expectation of punishment. The cost of punishment (subjective value of £2) and trust both moderate willingness to punish. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but mean payoffs are similar across neighborhoods. The results support the idea that local norms and trust shape antisocial behavior and punishment, with potential feedback loops.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. While it does not use a standard public goods game or directly analyze efficiency, it shows that punishment is less likely to be used in low-trust, high-norm-violation environments, and that expectation of punishment can be increased by manipulating perceived norms. The cost of punishment and trust are important moderators.
