For prediction, this suggests that punishment may be less effective at increasing efficiency in environments with low trust and high perceived norm violation, and that the subjective cost of punishment matters. However, because efficiency is not directly measured, the evidence is indirect and should be used with caution.

- source: Marlowe, F. W., Berbesque, J. C., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Gurven, M., & Tracer, D. (2011). The 'spiteful' origins of human cooperation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that: (1) Small-scale societies are less generous in the Dictator Game and less likely to engage in third-party punishment, but are equally likely to engage in second-party punishment (Ultimatum Game) as large societies. (2) In large societies, third-party punishment is more common and even exceeds second-party punishment, suggesting a greater role for norm enforcement. (3) Generosity and punishment behaviors are correlated across games and societies. The findings challenge the view that strong reciprocity (including third-party punishment) is universal, suggesting that 'spiteful' second-party punishment is more fundamental in small-scale societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not use a PGG or report efficiency-related outcomes. However, it provides informative context about the prevalence and type of punishment (second- vs third-party) across societies, which may be relevant for understanding the social and cultural moderators of punishment effects in related game environments. The results suggest that the willingness to punish (especially second-party) is widespread, but the emergence of third-party punishment and higher generosity is more characteristic of large, complex societies. For prediction tasks, this implies that the social context (e.g., group size, societal complexity) may moderate the type and frequency of punishment, but the direct impact on efficiency in PGG-like games is not addressed.

- source: Schildberg-Hörisch, H., & Strassmair, C. (2012). An Experimental Test of the Deterrence Hypothesis. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that introducing small or intermediate punishment incentives can increase the amount stolen compared to no punishment, contrary to the deterrence hypothesis. Only very strong punishment incentives reduce stealing. The effect is driven by heterogeneity: 'selfish' subjects respond to incentives as predicted, but 'fair-minded' subjects increase stealing when incentives are introduced, possibly due to crowding out of fairness. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, as the design is not a public goods game but a dyadic stealing game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment on norm-violating behavior (stealing) is non-monotonic: small and intermediate punishments can increase violations, while only strong punishments deter. However, because the design is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments is limited. The main lesson is that punishment can backfire under some conditions, especially when intrinsic motivation is present, but the mapping to efficiency outcomes in multi-player PGGs is indirect.

- source: Lo Gerfo, E., Gallucci, A., Morese, R., Vergallito, A., Ottone, S., Ponzano, F., Locatelli, G., Bosco, F., & Lauro, L. J. R. (2019). The role of ventromedial prefrontal cortex and temporo-parietal junction in third-party punishment behavior. *Neuroimage*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that modulating neural activity in the VMPFC increases the tendency to punish unfair behavior (altruistic punishment), while modulating the TPJ increases the tendency to punish fair behavior (antisocial punishment) in a third-party punishment game. The behavioral outcomes are measured as the decision to punish and the amount of punishment, not as group efficiency or total payoff. The study does not report on the effect of punishment on group welfare, efficiency, or aggregate earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its findings are limited to individual punishment behavior in a third-party context, with no analysis of how punishment affects group outcomes or efficiency. The game is a modified Dictator Game, not a standard PGG or repeated PGG, and the outcomes are not payoff-based. Thus, the paper offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except as context for the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying punishment behavior.

- source: Marlowe, F. W. (2009). Hadza Cooperation - Second-Party Punishment, Yes; Third-Party Punishment, No. *Human Nature-an Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The Hadza, unlike participants from larger or more complex societies, rarely engage in costly third-party punishment in experimental games. Offers in all games are low, and Hadza proposers tend to maximize their own income. Second-party punishment (rejection of low offers in the UG) is frequent, but third-party punishment (punishing low offers to others in the TPPG) is infrequent. The paper finds that third-party punishment is more prevalent in larger societies, suggesting that the scale and complexity of society may moderate the use of third-party punishment. Group earnings are low due to frequent rejections and low offers, but efficiency or group payoff is not the main focus.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. While it does not use a public goods game or directly report efficiency, it shows that in a closely related allocation game (TPPG), the availability of third-party punishment does not increase offers or group earnings among the Hadza, because third-party punishment is rarely used. In small-scale societies with low third-party punishment, enabling punishment may not improve efficiency. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on societal context and the willingness to use punishment, which may be low in small, egalitarian groups. The results are less informative for standard PGGs but suggest caution in assuming punishment will increase efficiency in all contexts.

- source: Ouss, A., & Peysakhovich, A. (2015). When Punishment Doesn't Pay: Cold Glow and Decisions to Punish. *Journal of Law & Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across a series of lab experiments, the authors show that individual punishment decisions are driven by 'cold glow' (private satisfaction from punishing) rather than by optimizing social efficiency. When punishment costs are shared by the group, individuals assign higher punishments than when they pay the cost themselves, resulting in over-punishment and excessive social cost. When the probability of apprehension is low, punishers do not increase punishment to compensate, so deterrence is insufficient and social efficiency is not achieved. When multiple punishers can assign punishment, their decisions are not crowded out by others' punishments, leading to excessive total punishment. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, taking behavior), but the implications for efficiency are discussed in terms of deviation from optimal deterrence and social cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on institutional details: (1) If punishment costs are externalized (group pays), enabling punishment can reduce efficiency due to over-punishment and excessive social cost. (2) If the probability of apprehension is low, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency because punishers do not adjust their behavior, leading to under-deterrence. (3) When multiple punishers are present, lack of crowding out can lead to excessive total punishment and lower efficiency. The findings suggest that simply enabling punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency; the effect is moderated by cost structure, probability of apprehension, and the number of punishers. For prediction, the presence of group-funded punishment, low apprehension probability, or multiple punishers should be expected to reduce or even reverse the efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Gambetta, D., & Przepiorka, W. (2019). Sharing Compromising Information as a Cooperative Strategy. *Sociological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that when a punishment mechanism is available (i.e., when norm-enforcement is possible), cooperation among less cooperative types ('hawks') increases, especially if both parties can mutually reveal their compromising information and thus make themselves vulnerable to punishment. In conditions without punishment, hawks rarely cooperate with each other. The effect is strongest when both the revelation of compromising information and the ability to punish are present and mutually chosen. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, defection, information sharing), not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that punishment opportunities can enable cooperation among otherwise uncooperative individuals, but the paper does not quantify the effect on group efficiency or total earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism in a trust-game-like environment can increase cooperation among less cooperative individuals, especially when mutual vulnerability is possible. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings should be used as indirect support for the prediction task. The game is a one-shot trust game variant, not a standard repeated public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured. The punishment mechanism is costly and only available in certain conditions. The results suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, but the magnitude of efficiency gains (if any) is not reported.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Diekmann, A. (2018). Heterogeneous groups overcome the diffusion of responsibility problem in social norm enforcement. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that diffusion of responsibility (lower individual sanctioning rates as group size increases) is present in symmetric groups but is largely overcome in heterogeneous groups where one member has a lower cost of sanctioning. Over repeated rounds, groups tacitly coordinate on the strong member to sanction, leading to higher and more efficient norm enforcement. However, the main outcomes are behavioral (sanctioning rates), not payoff-based efficiency or group welfare. The findings suggest that group heterogeneity can mitigate the bystander effect in norm enforcement, but do not provide direct evidence on how this affects group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative behavioral evidence about how group size and heterogeneity in punishment cost affect the likelihood and efficiency of norm enforcement in a volunteer's dilemma context. However, it does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings suggest that introducing heterogeneity in punishment cost can increase the likelihood and efficiency of norm enforcement, especially in larger groups, but the translation to efficiency or welfare outcomes in standard PGGs is indirect.

- source: Guo, R., Ding, J. S., & Wu, Z. (2020). How intergroup relation moderates group bias in Third-Party Punishment. *Acta Psychologica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that third-party observers punish selfish allocators more than fair ones, and punish outgroup members more harshly than ingroup members, demonstrating group bias in norm enforcement. Competition between groups increases punishment toward outgroup selfish allocators when the cost of punishment is low, but this effect is eliminated when the cost is high. The results support the 'mere-preference hypothesis' of group bias and show that cost-benefit analysis moderates the effect of intergroup relations on punishment behavior. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about how punishment behavior is moderated by intergroup relations and punishment cost in a third-party punishment context, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the findings suggest that punishment behavior (and thus potential effects on efficiency) may be more sensitive to intergroup competition and cost-benefit ratios, especially in settings where group identity and outgroup status are salient. However, since efficiency is not measured, the paper's value is mainly in informing behavioral mechanisms rather than direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Raihani, N. J., Grutter, A. S., & Bshary, R. (2012). Female cleaner fish cooperate more with unfamiliar males. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that female cleaner fish behave more cooperatively (feed more against their preference) when paired with unfamiliar males, who are more aggressive in punishing cheating than familiar males. Preventing male punishment reduces female cooperation. The effect of punishment on female cheating is short-lived, with no significant long-term effect. Male cooperative behavior is not affected by partner familiarity, likely because females do not punish males. The results suggest that in asymmetric interactions, harsher punishment from unfamiliar partners can induce greater cooperation from subordinates, even when familiarity would otherwise be expected to promote cooperation. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral proxies for cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and severity of punishment can increase cooperative behavior in asymmetric dyadic interactions, even when the punisher is unfamiliar. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The findings are most relevant for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment increases cooperation, especially in settings with asymmetric power and punishment ability. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that enabling punishment (especially effective, low-cost punishment by dominants) can increase cooperation rates, but does not provide direct evidence about the impact on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Raihani, N. J., Pinto, A. I., Grutter, A. S., Wismer, S., & Bshary, R. (2012). Male cleaner wrasses adjust punishment of female partners according to the stakes. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that male cleaner wrasses adjust the severity of punishment towards female partners based on the stakes of the cheating event (client value) and the relative size of the female. More severe punishment is delivered when cheating is more costly to the male (high-value client) and when the female is similar in size to the male. In turn, females respond to more severe punishment by behaving more cooperatively (cheating less in subsequent opportunities). The results support the idea that punishment can be flexibly adjusted to circumstances and that such flexibility can influence cooperative behavior. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment can be flexibly and contextually applied in cooperative dilemmas, and that increased punishment can reduce cheating and increase cooperative behavior. However, because the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot directly inform quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms and moderators (e.g., cost of cheating, partner asymmetry) that may influence the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation, but not for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Bshary, A., & Bshary, R. (2010). Self-Serving Punishment of a Common Enemy Creates a Public Good in Reef Fishes. *Current Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that (1) punishment by victim fish (chasing the blenny) reduces the likelihood of being attacked again, (2) in shoaling species, this punishment increases the probability that the blenny switches to another species, creating a public good for the group, and (3) blennies can learn to discriminate between punishers and nonpunishers, which selects against free riding. The findings are based on both field observations and controlled laboratory experiments using artificial plates to simulate fish. The outcomes are behavioral and do not include direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff, but the results are relevant to understanding how punishment can create public goods in repeated natural interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that in repeated, naturalistic interactions, self-serving punishment can create public good benefits for a group, and that the ability of the 'cheater' (blenny) to identify and target nonpunishers can sustain punishment even in the absence of kin structure. However, the study does not use a formal public goods game, does not manipulate standard game design dimensions (e.g., MPCR, player count, explicit payoff structure), and does not report efficiency or group payoff. Thus, while the behavioral mechanisms are informative for understanding the conditions under which punishment can sustain cooperation and public goods, the findings cannot be directly mapped to predictions about efficiency in laboratory public goods games with or without punishment. The main value is in highlighting the importance of repeated interactions, individual identification, and self-serving motives in the emergence of public goods via punishment.

- source: Fitouchi, L., & Singh, M. (2023). Punitive justice serves to restore reciprocal cooperation in three small-scale societies. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across three small-scale societies, punitive justice is primarily restorative and dyadic, not group-norm-enforcing. Third-party punishment is rare, and most interventions are aimed at mediating disputes and restoring relationships. Compensation to victims is common and often proportional to harm, especially among the Mentawai and Nuer. Kinship and interdependence reduce the likelihood or magnitude of punishment/compensation. When costs are not imposed on offenders, cooperation often breaks down and feuding may result. The paper does not use public goods games or measure efficiency, but provides evidence that punishment and compensation are more about restoring dyadic cooperation than increasing group-level efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence about the function of punishment in environments that are adjacent to public goods games: real-world small-scale societies with reciprocal obligations and norm violations. It suggests that, in such contexts, punishment is not primarily about increasing group efficiency or enforcing group-level cooperation, but about restoring dyadic relationships and preventing escalation. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment may depend on whether the context is more about dyadic reciprocity or group-level cooperation. However, because the paper does not use experimental PGGs or report efficiency or payoff outcomes, its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in PGGs is limited.

- source: Lopez, A. C. (2017). The Evolutionary Psychology of War: Offense and Defense in the Adapted Mind. *Evolutionary Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that (1) willingness to participate in collective aggression is predicted by expected group benefit in defense (public good) and by expected personal benefit in offense (private/club good); (2) punitive sentiment toward nonparticipants is more strongly triggered by willingness to participate in defense than in offense, supporting the idea that punishment is used to enforce participation in public goods contexts; (3) reward sentiment is also modulated by conflict type, with willingness to participate predicting reward sentiment in offense, and expected benefit predicting it in defense; (4) sex differences exist, with males generally more willing to participate, but labor sufficiency cues can increase female participation in defense; (5) labor sufficiency/insufficiency manipulations affect both willingness to participate and punitive sentiment, especially in defense. However, all outcomes are self-reported attitudes or intentions in hypothetical scenarios, not actual behavior or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, conceptual support for the idea that punishment is more likely to be used and to have a motivational effect in public goods (defense) contexts than in private/club goods (offense) contexts, and that the effectiveness of punishment as a recruitment/enforcement tool depends on the structure of the collective action problem and cues about labor sufficiency. However, it does not provide any direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, group payoff, or realized cooperation in public goods games or similar environments. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment will be more or less effective as a cooperation-enforcing mechanism, especially in relation to the publicness of the good and the perceived need for labor, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The study is best used for mechanism and moderator hypotheses, not for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Li, Y., Fermin, A. S. R., Kanai, R., Takagishi, H., Matsumoto, Y., Kiyonari, T., & Sakagami, M. (2017). Behavioural Differences and Neural Substrates of Altruistic and Spiteful Punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that individuals who engage in costly punishment in the TPPG (altruistic punishers) also behave more pro-socially in a variety of non-strategic games, while those who only reject unfair offers in the UG (spiteful punishers) behave more selfishly. The distinction is supported by neural data: spiteful punishers have larger left caudate nucleus and nucleus accumbens volumes. The paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, nor does it manipulate the presence or absence of punishment in a public goods or social dilemma context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or social dilemma games. It does, however, suggest that the motivations behind punishment (altruistic vs. spiteful) are heterogeneous and that not all punishment is associated with pro-social or efficiency-enhancing behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment may not always increase efficiency, depending on the underlying motives of punishers, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Thielmann, I., & Hilbig, B. E. (2018). Daring dishonesty: On the role of sanctions for (un)ethical behavior. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that increasing the probability of being checked for dishonesty and increasing the monetary penalty for being caught both substantially reduce the rate of cheating in individual tasks. In Experiment 1, tripling the probability of being checked (from 16.7% to 50%) nearly halved the estimated cheating rate. In Experiment 2, increasing both the probability of being checked and the penalty for being caught further reduced cheating, with the lowest dishonesty observed when both risk and severity were high. The results support utility-based theories of dishonesty, showing that external costs (sanctions) deter unethical behavior. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the design is not a public goods game or a direct analog.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the risk and severity of monetary sanctions reduces individual cheating in private reporting tasks. While the findings are highly relevant for understanding the deterrent effect of punishment on norm violations, they do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar group cooperation settings. The results may be indirectly informative for predicting that punishment mechanisms with higher detection probability and higher penalty severity are more likely to reduce norm violations, but the transfer to efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited by the lack of group interaction, contribution, or payoff aggregation in the study.

- source: Brüne, M., Scheele, D., Heinisch, C., Tas, C., Wischniewski, J., & Güntürkün, O. (2012). Empathy Moderates the Effect of Repetitive Transcranial Magnetic Stimulation of the Right Dorsolateral Prefrontal Cortex on Costly Punishment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that inhibiting the right DLPFC via rTMS increases the amount of costly punishment assigned by third-party observers in a Dictator Game with punishment option. This effect is significantly moderated by individual differences in empathy: the increase in punishment is stronger for individuals with lower empathy scores. No significant effect is found for left DLPFC stimulation. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not use a public goods game or a direct variant, but rather a third-party punishment Dictator Game. The findings are relevant for understanding the neural and psychological mechanisms underlying costly punishment, but do not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms that may underlie costly punishment, showing that punishment can be increased by disrupting cognitive control (right DLPFC) and that this effect is moderated by empathy. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or group payoff, this paper is only weakly relevant, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it use a PGG or close variant. It may be useful for understanding individual differences in punishment behavior, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Hilbig, B. E., Thielmann, I., Klein, S. A., & Henninger, F. (2016). The two faces of cooperation: On the unique role of HEXACO Agreeableness for forgiveness versus retaliation. *Journal of Research in Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in the Uncostly Retaliation Game, participants are much more likely to retaliate (punish) after being treated unfairly, and that this retaliation is predicted by lower Agreeableness (HEXACO AG), but not by Honesty-Humility (HH). Retaliation after fair treatment (antisocial punishment) is rare and is negatively predicted by Honesty-Humility. The game structure ensures that punishment is uncostly to the punisher, isolating the psychological motive for retaliation. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported. The results are robust across two experiments and are not explained by social value orientation or inequality aversion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar multi-player, repeated games. Its main contribution is to show that the psychological trait of Agreeableness predicts individual differences in uncostly punishment (retaliation) after unfair treatment in a two-player, one-shot allocation game. The game design is adjacent to public goods games in that it involves punishment for norm violations, but it lacks the group, repeated, and efficiency elements central to the prediction task. The findings may inform models of individual punishment behavior, but not efficiency outcomes.

- source: Deutchman, P., Bracic, M., Raihani, N., & McAuliffe, K. (2021). Punishment is strongly motivated by revenge and weakly motivated by inequity aversion. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment is primarily motivated by revenge (reciprocating losses), but disadvantageous inequity also motivates punishment, even in the absence of loss. The effect of inequity aversion is weaker than the effect of loss. The design manipulates whether the partner can steal from or augment the responder's payoff, and the resulting payoff differences. Punishment is most frequent when the partner steals, especially if this creates disadvantageous inequity. There is also some punishment when the partner does not augment, if this results in disadvantageous inequity. The study does not measure or report efficiency or group payoff, so it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence about the psychological motivations for punishment in two-player games, but does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. However, it does suggest that punishment is more likely to be used when losses or disadvantageous inequity are present, and that the cost and effectiveness of punishment can moderate its use. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not provide data on group efficiency or welfare with or without punishment.

- source: Kurz, T., Thomas, W. E., & Fonseca, M. A. (2014). A fine is a more effective financial deterrent when framed retributively and extracted publicly. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The field experiment demonstrates that the effectiveness of financial punishment in deterring undesired behavior (lateness) depends strongly on both the framing (retributive vs. compensatory) and the public/private nature of the sanction. Retributive and public fines significantly increase punctuality compared to compensatory or private fines, which are no more effective than no fine. A follow-up scenario study shows that compensatory fines reduce the perceived immorality of transgression, while retributive fines do not. The results support a theory that the moral framing and public visibility of punishment are key moderators of its behavioral impact.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the framing and public administration of punishment can substantially moderate its behavioral effectiveness, even when the material cost is held constant. While the context is not a public goods game, the findings suggest that in collective action settings, punishment will be more effective at promoting cooperation if it is framed as retributive (moral) and administered publicly. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so direct prediction of efficiency effects is not possible. The results are most useful for informing the design of punishment mechanisms (e.g., public vs. private, moral vs. compensatory framing) rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Price, M. E. (2005). Punitive sentiment among the Shuar and in industrialized societies: cross-cultural similarities. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that among the Shuar, as in industrialized societies, higher contributors to collective action (mingas) express stronger punitive sentiment toward free riders, and that only intentional absences (free riding) are considered deserving of punishment. The main dependent variable is the amount of fine each member thinks should be imposed for unexcused absences. There is a strong positive correlation between contribution (attendance) and punitive sentiment. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it provides cross-cultural evidence that punitive sentiment toward free riders is a robust feature of collective action settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, support the generalizability of punitive sentiment toward free riders across cultures and suggests that the psychological mechanisms underlying punishment are similar in small-scale and industrialized societies. For prediction tasks, this paper is useful for understanding the universality of punitive motivations, but not for quantifying efficiency effects or moderating variables. It does not provide parameter values or outcome data for the key game design dimensions relevant to efficiency prediction.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Berger, J. (2016). The Sanctioning Dilemma: A Quasi-Experiment on Social Norm Enforcement in the Train. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that peer-sanctioning (negative sanctioning of a norm violator) occurs in about 50% of interventions. Contrary to the classic diffusion of responsibility hypothesis, the likelihood of sanctioning increases with the number of passengers present. Passengers who are closer to the norm breaker and those whose activities require silence are more likely to sanction. The likelihood of sanctioning decreases as the train approaches its destination. No significant difference in sanctioning rates is found between silent-area and non-silent-area train cars. The study does not measure or report any payoff, efficiency, or group welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their variants. While it is conceptually adjacent—framing norm enforcement as a second-order public good and using volunteer's dilemma theory—the outcomes are strictly behavioral (sanctioning occurrence and timing). There is no measurement of group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, nor any manipulation of punishment cost, effectiveness, or related game design dimensions. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments, though it may provide some indirect, conceptual support for the importance of heterogeneity and group size in norm enforcement contexts.

- source: Rosati, A. G., DiNicola, L. M., & Buckholtz, J. W. (2018). Chimpanzee Cooperation Is Fast and Independent From Self-Control. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Chimpanzees show a fast response bias for prosocial actions (including punishment of theft), but the frequency of such actions is modest and varies across individuals. More prosocial individuals are faster to act in both helping and punishment contexts. There is no evidence that self-control (temporal or motoric) predicts prosocial or punishment behavior. The three measured domains of prosociality (donation, helping, punishment) are uncorrelated, suggesting distinct underlying mechanisms. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its design is adjacent to PGGs, using individual-level donation, helping, and punishment tasks with chimpanzees, but not repeated group games or explicit payoff structures. The main relevance is in showing that punishment behavior exists and is associated with faster response times in chimpanzees, but not with self-control. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper offers only indirect, weak support: it suggests that punishment can be a salient behavioral response, but does not inform whether it increases or decreases group efficiency.

- source: Berger, J., & Hevenstone, D. (2016). Norm enforcement in the city revisited: An international field experiment of altruistic punishment, norm maintenance, and broken windows. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that direct and indirect norm enforcement behaviors (including costly punishment) occur in natural, anonymous, one-shot interactions, but at lower rates than in laboratory experiments. Enforcement is more common in Switzerland than in New York City. The presence of disorder (litter) reduces the likelihood of both direct and indirect enforcement. Making it easier to maintain the norm (by being close to a trashcan) increases norm maintenance behaviors. Subtle sanctions and norm maintenance are more frequent than direct confrontation. Individual characteristics (in-group status, age) also predict enforcement. The study does not report any efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, so its findings are not directly transferable to predictions about group efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that costly punishment and other forms of norm enforcement are less frequent in real-world, anonymous, one-shot settings than in laboratory public goods games. It also shows that environmental context (disorder, opportunity structure) and location matter for the frequency of enforcement. However, because the study does not measure group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value for prediction is as indirect, contextual evidence about the real-world prevalence and moderators of costly punishment, suggesting that laboratory findings may overestimate the frequency and possibly the efficiency impact of punishment in naturalistic settings.

- source: Mathew, S. (2017). How the Second-Order Free Rider Problem Is Solved in a Small-Scale Society. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that Turkana adults express strong disapproval toward second-order free riders (those who do not punish norm violators), as well as toward those who punish excessively, unjustly, or without proper group consensus. Punishment is seen as legitimate only when collectively decided and administered by a preordained group. These meta-norms help regulate peer punishment and may solve the second-order free rider problem in this society. However, the evidence is based on responses to hypothetical scenarios, not on observed behavior or economic outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that strong social norms and meta-norms can support the legitimacy and regulation of peer punishment in a real-world society, potentially solving the second-order free rider problem. However, because the study does not measure efficiency, group payoff, or actual economic outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of meta-norms and collective legitimacy for the functioning of peer punishment systems.

- source: Dimant, E., & Gesche, T. (2023). Nudging enforcers: how norm perceptions and motives for lying shape sanctions. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment increases with the size of the lie and is harsher when the lie increases inequity or is for personal gain, compared to when it restores equity. Norm-nudges (empirical or normative information about others' behavior or approval) increase punishment, especially when preexisting norms are ambiguous. The effect of norm-nudges is strongest in scenarios where the social norm is less clear. These findings are robust across a behavioral experiment, a vignette experiment, and a norm elicitation study. However, the study does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on how punishment behavior is shaped by norm-nudges and the context of norm violations, but it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it shows that punishment is sensitive to context and can be increased by norm-nudges, especially when norms are ambiguous. However, it does not provide evidence on whether or how punishment affects group efficiency or welfare, nor does it use a public goods game structure. Thus, it should be used as indirect evidence about the determinants of punishment behavior, not about efficiency effects.

- source: Martin, J. W., Jordan, J. J., Rand, D. G., & Cushman, F. (2019). When do we punish people who don't?. *Cognition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that people are more likely to punish (or endorse punishing) third-party observers who fail to punish norm violators than second-party victims who fail to punish. This pattern is robust across economic games and vignettes, and holds for both severe and non-criminal norm violations. The relationship between the third party and the victim modulates HOP: authorities are punished most for failing to punish, followed by friends, then strangers. The study does not examine or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and thus does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. Its outcomes are exclusively about the social psychology of higher-order punishment—when people punish non-punishers—not about the consequences of punishment for cooperation, contribution, or efficiency. The economic game used is not a public goods game or a close variant, and no group-level payoff or efficiency measures are reported. Thus, this paper should not be used to inform quantitative predictions of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Eriksson, K., Andersson, P. A., & Strimling, P. (2016). Moderators of the disapproval of peer punishment. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that peer punishment of norm violators is typically disapproved of by observers, especially when the punishment is severe or appears aggressive. This disapproval extends to viewing the punisher as a problem for the group and the act as inappropriate. Disapproval is moderated by the observer's own trait aggression (less disapproval among more aggressive raters), the severity of punishment (more severe = more disapproval), and whether the punishment is collective or individual (collective = slightly less disapproval). Economic sanctions are disapproved of less than physical ones, and unselfish economic sanctions (where the punisher does not profit) are disapproved of less than selfish ones. Verbal sanctions are also disapproved of, even when effective. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any related outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding social approval and the perceived legitimacy of peer punishment, but not for predicting how punishment affects group efficiency or earnings. The design is adjacent to PGGs (common resource, norm violation, sanctioning), but the outcomes are purely attitudinal and perceptual. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task of treatment efficiency, though it may inform hypotheses about the social acceptability of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Eriksson, K., Strimling, P., Andersson, P. A., Aveyard, M., Brauer, M., Gritskov, V., Kiyonari, T., Kuhlman, D. M., Maitner, A. T., Manesi, Z., Molho, C., Peperkoorn, L. S., Rizwan, M., Stivers, A. W., Tian, Q. R., Van Lange, P. A. M., Vartanova, I., Wu, J. H., & Yamagishi, T. (2017). Cultural Universals and Cultural Differences in Meta-Norms about Peer Punishment. *Management and Organization Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that across eight countries, punishment of norm violators in a resource-sharing scenario is judged as more appropriate when enacted by the group rather than by an individual. There is substantial cross-cultural variation in the appraisal of punishers versus non-punishers: in more individualistic countries (USA, Netherlands, Sweden, Japan), punishers are rated less favorably than non-punishers, while in more collectivistic countries (Pakistan, UAE, Russia, China), this difference is absent or reversed. These patterns hold for both retributive and restorative forms of punishment. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any related payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are limited to social appraisals and meta-norms about punishment, not behavioral or payoff outcomes. However, it may inform predictions about the acceptability or likelihood of punishment being used in different cultural contexts, which could indirectly affect the implementation or effectiveness of punishment in real-world or experimental settings. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing only contextual or indirect evidence, not direct evidence about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment.

- source: Gordon, D. S., & Lea, S. E. G. (2016). Who Punishes? The Status of the Punishers Affects the Perceived Success of, and Indirect Benefits From, Moralistic'' Punishment. *Evolutionary Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The studies find that observers expect only high-status (HS) individuals to engage in and succeed at moralistic punishment of norm violations. HS punishers are seen as more likely to be successful, more dominant, and more likely to be chosen for leadership roles. Low-status (LS) individuals are not expected to punish, and even if they do, they do not gain the same reputational or leadership benefits. The perceived benefits of punishment (e.g., increased likability, dominance, or leadership nomination) are conditional on both the status of the punisher and the success of the intervention. The work does not address actual behavioral outcomes in economic games, nor does it report on efficiency or payoff effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding social expectations and reputational dynamics around punishment, especially the role of status, but do not inform quantitative predictions about group efficiency or payoff outcomes when punishment is enabled. The results may be contextually useful for interpreting when and why punishment is expected or effective in real-world or experimental settings, particularly regarding heterogeneity in punishment costs or effectiveness due to status, but they do not provide data on efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Gordon, D. S., Madden, J. R., & Lea, S. E. G. (2014). Both Loved and Feared: Third Party Punishers Are Viewed as Formidable and Likeable, but These Reputational Benefits May Only Be Open to Dominant Individuals. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that third party punishment is perceived as both a dominant and likable act, distinguishing it from other aggressive behaviors. However, only dominant individuals are seen as able to punish effectively and at low cost, with reduced risk of retaliation and greater ability to use non-violent (social) punishment. Subordinate individuals are perceived as less likely to succeed and at higher risk if they attempt punishment. The reputational benefits of punishment (increased likability and dominance) are thus mainly accessible to dominant individuals. These findings are based on observer judgments in hypothetical scenarios, not on actual behavior in economic games or real payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that heterogeneity in the cost and effectiveness of punishment (due to dominance/status) may be important moderators of the impact of punishment on cooperation and norm enforcement. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that the effectiveness and social acceptability of punishment may depend on the perceived status of the punisher, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental data on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding mechanisms and moderators of punishment, but not for direct prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Pedersen, E. J., McAuliffe, W. H. B., Shah, Y., Tanaka, H., Ohtsubo, Y., & McCullough, M. E. (2020). When and Why Do Third Parties Punish Outside of the Lab? A Cross-Cultural Recall Study. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment in real-life conflicts is infrequent, especially when the third party does not value the welfare of the victim (i.e., low WTR). Intervention (helping the victim without punishing the transgressor) is more common than punishment. The main predictor of both punishment and intervention is the third party's WTR for the victim, with higher WTRs associated with greater likelihood of action. WTR for the transgressor predicts less anger but does not significantly predict intervention or punishment. The results are consistent across U.S. students, U.S. MTurkers, and Japanese students, though Japanese students intervene and punish less often. The study concludes that real-world third-party punishment is much less common than laboratory experiments suggest, likely due to the lack of anonymity, the availability of alternative responses, and the importance of social relationships.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it is highly informative about the external validity of laboratory punishment rates: it suggests that real-world third-party punishment is rare, especially among strangers, and that laboratory designs may overstate the prevalence and impact of punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the view that punishment's effect on cooperation or efficiency in real-world or naturalistic settings may be much smaller than in lab-based PGGs, particularly when social distance is high and alternative responses are available. The findings highlight the importance of relationship context and available response options as moderators of punishment behavior.

- source: Zinchenko, O. (2019). Brain responses to social punishment: a meta-analysis. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that social punishment tasks consistently activate the bilateral claustrum, right inferior frontal gyrus, and left superior frontal gyrus. These regions are associated with salience detection, executive function, and emotional empathy. The meta-analysis includes a range of paradigms (Ultimatum Game, Dictator Game, third-party punishment, social rejection), but does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or related economic outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the neural basis of social punishment but do not provide evidence on the effects of punishment on cooperation, efficiency, or payoff in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their variants. While it confirms that social punishment is a robust neural phenomenon across several economic and social paradigms, it does not report or synthesize any data on group earnings, efficiency, or welfare. Therefore, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about the impact of punishment on efficiency in public goods game-like environments.

- source: Adams, G. S., & Mullen, E. (2013). Increased Voting for Candidates Who Compensate Victims Rather than Punish Offenders. *Social Justice Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that people prefer to vote for candidates who compensate victims (or both compensate and punish) over those who only punish perpetrators or do not respond to injustice. This preference is robust across different types of transgressions (genocide, bike theft, domestic violence) and is mediated by perceptions of warmth. Perceived effectiveness of the response is measured subjectively, not as a monetary or group payoff. The prevalence of the transgression and political orientation do not moderate these effects. The studies do not involve economic games, group cooperation, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. The outcomes are about third-party voting preferences and trait perceptions, not about how punishment affects cooperation, contributions, or efficiency. While the paper discusses punishment and compensation as responses to norm violations, it does not manipulate or measure the game design dimensions relevant to public goods games, nor does it report any payoff-based outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Masclet, D., & Pénard, T. (2012). Do reputation feedback systems really improve trust among anonymous traders? An experimental study. *Applied Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that adding a reputation feedback system (where players can assign costly positive or negative ratings to their partner after each round) to a repeated trust game significantly increases both trust (amount sent) and trustworthiness (amount returned), resulting in higher average payoffs for both roles. The simultaneous rating system, which prevents players from knowing their partner's rating before making their own, produces the highest efficiency and welfare, as it minimizes strategic and retaliatory rating behavior. Sequential systems, where players can condition their rating on their partner's action, are less effective, and a system that prevents one side from punishing (as in the eBay-inspired treatment) does not improve and may even reduce efficiency. The results are robust across multiple sessions and are supported by both behavioral and payoff data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a costly punishment/reward (reputation feedback) system in a repeated trust game increases efficiency (average payoff) compared to a control without such a system. The effect is most pronounced when the feedback system is designed to prevent strategic and retaliatory use (i.e., simultaneous, secret ratings). The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment/reward system with moderate cost and effectiveness, especially when implemented in a way that minimizes strategic retaliation, should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control. The results are directly relevant for trust-game-like environments and are likely to transfer to PGG-like settings with similar feedback mechanisms, though the mapping is not exact. The paper also provides evidence on the moderating role of feedback system design (timing, information structure) on the efficiency effect.

- source: Leibbrandt, A., & Sääksvuori, L. (2012). Communication in intergroup conflicts. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated intergroup contest game (two groups of four, 20 rounds), the opportunity for within-group peer punishment (REST+PUN) does not reduce group conflict expenditures compared to restricted communication without punishment (REST). Both treatments result in much higher conflict expenditures (i.e., lower efficiency) than the no-communication baseline. Open communication between groups, in contrast, dramatically reduces conflict expenditures and increases efficiency. Punishment is rarely used when communication is available, and its presence does not significantly affect group payoffs or efficiency. The main mechanism for efficiency gains is open communication and turn-taking, not punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in intergroup contest environments with intra-group punishment and communication, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency relative to communication alone. If the control (no-punishment) game has restricted communication, adding punishment does not increase efficiency. If open communication is possible, efficiency is much higher regardless of punishment. Thus, for prediction, the presence of peer punishment in this design should not be expected to increase efficiency, and may even be associated with high waste. The findings are most relevant for contest-like games with group-level competition and intra-group punishment, rather than standard linear PGGs.

- source: Normann, H. T., Rösch, J., & Schultz, L. M. (2015). Do buyer groups facilitate collusion?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: In repeated Cournot triopoly experiments, buyer groups that can exclude members (closed buyer groups) enable targeted punishment of high-output (deviating) firms, which facilitates collusion and reduces industry output toward the joint-profit maximizing (collusive) level. This reduces efficiency and welfare compared to baseline or open buyer groups (which cannot exclude). Communication, especially when used for explicit quantitative agreements, further increases collusion and reduces output. Open buyer groups without exclusion do not significantly differ from baseline in terms of output or efficiency. The exclusion mechanism is used mainly against high-output firms, consistent with punishment of deviations. Overall, mechanisms that enable punishment (exclusion) or communication (especially explicit agreements) reduce efficiency by facilitating collusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling targeted punishment (via exclusion from a buyer group) in a repeated Cournot game reduces efficiency by facilitating collusion, as measured by lower industry output and welfare. The effect is more pronounced when communication is allowed, especially with explicit quantitative agreements. In the absence of exclusion or communication, buyer groups do not significantly affect efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (exclusion), communication, and the structure of the game (repeated, triopoly, continuous output) are key moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. The findings are directly relevant for settings where punishment is implemented as exclusion or similar targeted sanctions, and less so for standard PGGs with costly punishment tokens.

- source: Lumeau, M., Masclet, D., & Penard, T. (2015). Reputation and social (dis)approval in feedback mechanisms: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both private and public feedback mechanisms increase trust (amount sent) and trustworthiness (return rate) in a repeated trust game compared to a baseline with no feedback. Public feedback (where ratings are observable to future partners) is more effective than private feedback (where ratings are only seen by the recipient) in increasing these behaviors. The difference is attributed to both a higher frequency of ratings and a stronger behavioral response to public ratings. When a cost is introduced for assigning ratings, the effects persist but are weaker, especially for private feedback. The study concludes that reputation mechanisms (public feedback) are essential for sustaining high levels of cooperation and implied efficiency in environments with imperfect information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a feedback mechanism (a form of non-monetary sanction or reward) increases cooperation and implied efficiency in trust-game-like environments. Publicly observable feedback (reputation) is more effective than private feedback (social (dis)approval only). The results are robust to the introduction of a small cost for assigning feedback. For prediction tasks, the presence and observability of feedback mechanisms should be expected to increase efficiency, with public/reputational mechanisms having a larger effect than private ones. However, the results are from a trust game, not a PGG, so direct quantitative transfer to PGGs should be cautious, but the qualitative direction is likely similar.

- source: Gervais, M. M. (2017). RICH Economic Games for Networked Relationships and Communities: Development and Preliminary Validation in Yasawa, Fiji. *Field Methods*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a Fijian village, participants are willing to spend significant portions of their endowment to reduce the earnings of others in a costly reduction game (CRG), with 66% of deciders reducing at least one target. The mean amount spent on reductions was 45% of the available stake. Target earnings varied widely, and some participants ended with negative aggregate earnings (before post-hoc adjustment). The motives for reduction included both punitive and leveling (egalitarian) reasons. Compared to anonymous-target punishment games in neighboring villages, the rate of punitive behavior is higher in the CRG, likely due to the integration of recipient identities. However, the study does not directly measure the effect of punishment on group efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, nor does it use a standard public goods game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a context where punishment is possible and recipient identities are known, individuals are willing to pay substantial costs to reduce others' payoffs, often for leveling or punitive reasons. The cost-to-impact ratio is clearly specified (0.5 FJ$ cost to reduce 2 FJ$ from a target). However, because the game is not a standard public goods game and there is no control condition without punishment, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. The high willingness to punish and the resulting wide variation in earnings suggest that punishment can lead to significant reductions in aggregate payoffs, especially when used for leveling rather than norm enforcement. The study is most informative for settings where punishment is possible, costly, and can be used for reasons beyond norm enforcement, particularly in small, close-knit communities.

- source: López-Pérez, R., & Vorsatz, M. (2010). On approval and disapproval: Theory and experiments. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three treatments: (1) control (standard PD, no feedback), (2) expectations (subjects state what they expect their co-player will think of their choice), and (3) feedback (after the PD, subjects can pay to send an approval/disapproval message to their co-player). The cooperation rate is highest in the feedback treatment (38.3%), intermediate in the expectations treatment (29.0%), and lowest in the control (22.4%), but only the difference between feedback and control is statistically significant. The study finds that the possibility of non-material sanctions (approval/disapproval) increases cooperation, but does not analyze or report efficiency or group payoff. The effect is interpreted as arising from increased awareness of others' opinions and aversion to receiving negative feedback. The feedback mechanism is costly (10 ECU per message), but the messages have no material effect on payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the opportunity for non-material, costly approval/disapproval feedback can increase cooperation in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff. The findings are adjacent to public goods games with punishment, as the game is a two-player PD and the 'punishment' is non-material and costly, with no direct effect on payoffs. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that enabling even non-material, costly feedback can increase cooperation rates, but the effect on efficiency is not measured. The design dimensions most relevant here are the presence of a feedback/punishment stage, the cost of feedback, and the fact that feedback is non-material. The results suggest that both the psychological salience of being evaluated and the aversion to negative feedback are important for increasing cooperation, but the transferability to efficiency in PGGs is limited by the absence of group payoff data.

- source: Jung, Y. B., Hall, J., Hong, R. Y., Goh, T., Ong, N., & Tan, N. (2014). Payback: Effects of relationship and cultural norms on reciprocity. *Asian Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that reciprocity norms (reward and punishment) are shaped by relationship and culture. Collectivists are more generous to friends after positive treatment and less likely to punish after negative treatment (even to strangers), sometimes offering an 'olive branch' (positive or neutral response to negative treatment). Individualists are more likely to punish norm violators (destroy money at a cost to themselves) regardless of relationship. The main dependent variable is the amount given or destroyed, not group efficiency or total payoff. The study does not use a public goods game or a repeated multi-player setting, but rather a dyadic hybrid trust-dictator game with a single round (though participants believed there would be five rounds).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the behavioral drivers of punishment and reward in dyadic trust/dictator game settings, especially the influence of culture and relationship on willingness to punish at a cost. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or multi-player repeated setting. The findings may inform expectations about the likelihood and intensity of costly punishment or reward in similar dyadic games, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency changes from enabling punishment in public goods games. The evidence is most relevant for understanding the psychological and cultural moderators of punishment behavior, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Truskanov, N., Emery, Y., & Bshary, R. (2020). Juvenile cleaner fish can socially learn the consequences of cheating. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
