  findings: Juvenile cleaner fish, after observing adults interacting with model clients (plates) that respond negatively to cheating (either by fleeing or punishing), subsequently behave more cooperatively (feed more against their preference) and prefer partners that are tolerant to cheating. Social learning about the consequences of cheating shapes cooperative strategies, but juveniles do not simply copy arbitrary preferences of adults. The study provides evidence that social learning about sanctions can promote cooperation in a non-human animal, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff as in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that observation of punishment or negative partner responses can increase cooperative behavior in a dyadic animal model. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that the presence of observable sanctions can bias individuals toward more cooperative choices, which in analogous human or multi-player games could translate to higher efficiency if similar mechanisms operate. However, the lack of direct payoff or efficiency measurement, and the dyadic (not group) structure, limit direct transfer to the prediction of efficiency in public goods games with punishment. The main value is mechanistic: social learning about sanctions can promote cooperation.

- source: Gingins, S., Werminghausen, J., Johnstone, R. A., Grutter, A. S., & Bshary, R. (2013). Power and temptation cause shifts between exploitation and cooperation in a cleaner wrasse mutualism. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_tech
  findings: Cleaner wrasse strategically adjust their foraging behavior in response to the threat of early termination (sanction) and temptation to cheat. When the threat of early termination is high and the temptation to cheat is low, cleaners behave more cooperatively (eat more less-preferred items, which is analogous to providing better service). When the temptation to cheat is high and the threat is low, cleaners exploit the situation (eat more preferred items). The non-cleaner control species does not adjust its behavior in response to these manipulations. The study provides evidence that simple partner control mechanisms (sanctions) can maintain cooperation in asymmetric interactions, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that the presence of a sanction mechanism (threat of early termination) can shift behavior from exploitation to cooperation, especially when the temptation to cheat is low. However, the outcomes are behavioral and not directly about efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that, in analogous public goods games, enabling a sanction mechanism may increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency would depend on the cost structure and whether the sanction is costly or not. The study is most informative for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which sanctions can promote cooperation, but less directly informative for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Lacomba, J. A., Lagos, F., Reuben, E., & van Winden, F. (2014). On the escalation and de-escalation of conflict. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in one-shot or randomly matched (Strangers) conflict games, conflict expenditures are high and escalate over time, leading to low efficiency (mean earnings well below the endowment). When losers can destroy resources (akin to punishment), conflict expenditures decrease, and in the 'Resistance' game (where destruction can be conditioned on the winner's appropriation), mean earnings are significantly higher than in the baseline. However, destruction itself is wasteful and only increases efficiency if it deters appropriation and reduces conflict. In repeated interaction (Partners), a substantial minority of groups achieve long-lasting peace (zero conflict, high efficiency), but most do not. Peace is achieved through costly signaling (not fighting, low appropriation), not through destruction. Overall, the effect of punishment-like destruction on efficiency is mixed: it can increase efficiency by reducing conflict, but can also reduce it if destruction is frequent. The design dimensions (matching protocol, post-conflict options) strongly moderate these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, the ability of losers to destroy resources) on efficiency in conflict games is highly context-dependent. In one-shot or random-matching settings, allowing punishment (destruction) can reduce conflict expenditures and, if destruction is conditional on appropriation, can increase efficiency. However, if destruction is frequent, efficiency may not improve. In repeated games, the possibility of peace (high efficiency) depends more on costly signaling and restraint than on punishment. For prediction, the presence of a punishment mechanism (destruction) is likely to reduce conflict and increase efficiency only if it credibly deters appropriation and is not overused. The matching protocol (repeated vs. random) and the structure of post-conflict options are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is low in these games; enabling punishment can increase efficiency in some variants (notably Resistance/RE), but not always.

- source: Barker, J. L., & Barclay, P. (2016). Local competition increases people's willingness to harm others. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that when competition is local (i.e., participants compete for prizes within small groups), people are more willing to pay a cost to harm others, resulting in lower group and individual payoffs (efficiency). In contrast, under global competition (competing against all participants), less money is spent on harming, and payoffs are higher. This effect holds whether endowments are earned or given. The findings are robust across three studies, including one where participants could harm both ingroup and outgroup members: under local competition, harming is targeted at ingroup members (the direct competitors), further reducing within-group efficiency. The results are interpreted as a 'tragedy of the commons' driven by local competition, where wasteful harming reduces overall welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in economic games where players can pay to reduce others' payoffs (money-burning), the structure of competition (local vs global) is a critical moderator of efficiency. When competition is local, efficiency is much lower due to increased costly harming, even when the cost-effectiveness of harming is high (burning $1 to reduce $4). The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of local competition (small group contests) should be expected to strongly reduce efficiency when punishment/harming is possible, compared to global competition. The results are robust to whether endowments are earned or given, and to whether harming is targeted at ingroup or outgroup members. However, the game is not a standard public goods game or punishment game: the main action is costly harming, not punishment for norm violation or contribution decisions. Still, the efficiency implications are directly relevant for settings where costly sanctions are possible.

- source: Camera, G., & Casari, M. (2014). The Coordination Value of Monetary Exchange: Experimental Evidence. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated, anonymous helping game with four players, introducing a fiat money-like mechanism (tickets) does not increase the maximum attainable efficiency but changes how cooperation is coordinated. In the baseline (no tickets), realized efficiency is 48.2% of the maximum. With tickets, overall efficiency is 46.8%, not significantly different from baseline. However, when ticket transfer is feasible, cooperation and efficiency rise to 61.4%; when not feasible, they drop to 12.5%. The presence of tickets leads to the emergence of a monetary exchange strategy, increases the predictability of cooperative behavior, and redistributes surplus from frequent defectors to frequent cooperators. The main effect of the monetary mechanism is to facilitate coordination and reduce strategic uncertainty, not to increase aggregate efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing a monetary-like mechanism (tickets) in a repeated, anonymous, decentralized helping game does not increase overall efficiency compared to a baseline with only decentralized punishment, but it does change the distribution and predictability of cooperation. The effect of the monetary mechanism is highly contingent on whether the mechanism is feasible in a given match. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence of a monetary or token-based exchange mechanism may not increase group efficiency if the baseline already allows for decentralized punishment and the efficiency frontier is not expanded. However, such mechanisms can facilitate coordination, increase the stability of cooperation, and redistribute surplus toward cooperators. The results are most relevant for environments where punishment is decentralized and impersonal, and where direct reciprocity and reputation are absent.

- source: Bolton, G., Greiner, B., & Ockenfels, A. (2018). Dispute Resolution or Escalation? The Strategic Gaming of Feedback Withdrawal Options in Online Markets. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In laboratory markets with two-sided moral hazard (both buyer and seller can defect), the introduction of a mutual feedback withdrawal option leads to more strategic negative feedback, less honest feedback, and lower efficiency (measured as realized gains from trade divided by maximum possible gains). The feedback system becomes less informative, and trust/trustworthiness are reduced. In one-sided markets (only seller can defect), the feedback withdrawal option does not significantly affect efficiency or the informativeness of feedback. The negative effect of FBW on efficiency is thus conditional on the presence of two-sided moral hazard and mutual withdrawal. The paper also finds that some buyers engage in 'altruistic punishment' by refusing to withdraw negative feedback even at a cost to themselves, partially mitigating the detrimental effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to public goods games with two-sided moral hazard and mutual punishment/retaliation options, introducing a mutual feedback withdrawal (punishment-like) mechanism reduces efficiency relative to a control without such a mechanism. The effect is not observed in one-sided environments. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of mutual, bilateral punishment/retaliation options in two-sided games is likely to reduce efficiency, even if the control game is already inefficient. The effect is mediated by strategic retaliation and the breakdown of reputation as an incentive for cooperation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with similar two-sided, bilateral punishment/retaliation structures.

- source: Fehr, D., & Sutter, M. (2019). Gossip and the efficiency of interactions. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that enabling third-party gossip in a repeated trust game increases both trustor investments and trustee return ratios, resulting in higher efficiency (total group earnings) compared to a baseline with no third party and to a treatment with only observation (no communication). Efficiency in the Gossip treatment is 13% higher than baseline and 5% higher than Observation. The effect is robust to the cost of gossip and the amount of information available to gossipers, but is reduced when gossip is noisy (messages may be misdelivered). The presence of a third party who can observe and potentially gossip is more effective at increasing efficiency than simply providing first-hand information to trustors. The results suggest that the threat or possibility of gossip acts as a cost-effective social sanction, increasing cooperation and efficiency without direct material punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in trust-game-like environments, enabling a third-party gossip mechanism (even when costly and unverifiable) increases efficiency relative to both a baseline and an observation-only condition. The efficiency gain is substantial and robust to variations in the cost and informativeness of gossip, but is reduced if gossip is unreliable. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, the findings are likely relevant for adjacent environments where indirect social sanctions (gossip, reputation) are possible. For prediction, the presence of a gossip/observation mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency, with the largest gains when gossip is reliable and can be selectively transmitted. The effect is not due to direct punishment, but to reputational concerns and information transmission.

- source: Nunney, S. J., van der Schalk, J., & Manstead, A. S. R. (2022). Emotion and intergroup cooperation: How verbal expressions of guilt, shame, and pride influence behavior in a social dilemma. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across two lab experiments using an intergroup centipede game, the authors find that when a group expresses shame (or guilt) after behaving uncooperatively, the opposing group is more likely to cooperate in subsequent rounds, resulting in higher group payoffs (tickets won) and more prosocial behavior. In contrast, when a group expresses pride after uncooperative behavior, the opposing group is less likely to cooperate, resulting in lower payoffs and more costly punishment. The effect of emotion expression is causal (Study 2) and operates above and beyond the effect of prior behavior. The study does not use a standard public goods game or implement a formal punishment stage as in PGGs, but does include a costly punishment measure in Study 2.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that communication—specifically, the expression of self-conscious emotions—can significantly moderate the effect of prior uncooperative behavior on subsequent group efficiency in repeated, sequential social dilemmas. While not a standard PGG, the centipede game shares key features (sequential cooperation, group payoffs, potential for exploitation). The findings suggest that, in environments where communication is possible, expressions of shame or guilt can substitute for or augment formal punishment mechanisms in restoring cooperation and efficiency, while pride expressions can undermine them. However, the study does not manipulate punishment cost, technology, or other standard PGG design dimensions, so its direct predictive value for punishment-enabled PGG efficiency is limited. It does, however, highlight the importance of communication and emotional signaling as moderators of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Fonseca, M. A., & Peters, K. (2021). Is it costly to deceive? People are adept at detecting gossipers' lies but may not reward honesty. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Dishonest gossip reduces efficiency in trust-based interactions: participants who act on dishonest gossip (especially positive lies about untrustworthy agents or negative lies about trustworthy agents) earn lower payoffs than those who act on honest gossip. Participants can detect some dishonest gossip, especially after interacting with the target, and are more likely to trust gossipers they believe to be honest. However, the tendency to socially sanction dishonest gossipers (by reducing trust) is inconsistent and influenced by the valence of the gossip: gossipers who share negative information, even if honest, are trusted less. Thus, while social sanctions for dishonesty exist, they are imperfect and do not always align with actual honesty, limiting their effectiveness in promoting efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the presence of dishonest communication in trust-based games reduces efficiency, as measured by participant payoffs. While not a public goods game, the trust game with gossip is adjacent and the mechanism—misleading information undermining cooperation—transfers to PGG-like settings. The findings suggest that, in environments where social sanctions for dishonesty are weak or inconsistently applied, the efficiency gains from reputation mechanisms (like gossip) are limited by the prevalence and detectability of dishonesty. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effectiveness of punishment or sanction mechanisms in improving efficiency depends not only on their presence but also on their alignment with actual dishonest behavior and the social context (e.g., whether negative but honest information is socially penalized). The paper does not manipulate classic PGG punishment dimensions (cost, tech, etc.), but it does show that the ability to sanction dishonesty is necessary but not sufficient for efficiency gains.

- source: Oberholzer-Gee, F., Waldfogel, J., & White, M. W. (2010). FRIEND OR FOE? COOPERATION AND LEARNING IN HIGH-STAKES GAMES. *Review of Economics and Statistics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study documents that, over two seasons of the game show, players shift from unconditional to conditional cooperation based on observable opponent characteristics (e.g., gender, race, age). In the first season, cooperation rates are unrelated to opponent attributes; in the second season, players are more likely to defect (choose 'foe') against opponents whose attributes predict less cooperation. This learning process leads to a substantial decline in overall efficiency: the average share of stakes converted to winnings drops from 39% in season 1 to 30% in season 2. The decline is especially pronounced for players with attributes associated with low cooperation (e.g., men, black players, younger players), whose average efficiency falls sharply. In contrast, players with more cooperative-associated attributes (e.g., women, white players) maintain their efficiency. The findings suggest that conditional cooperation, when based on imperfect signals, can reduce group efficiency in one-shot, high-stakes settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in one-shot, simultaneous-move games structurally similar to the prisoner's dilemma, the emergence of conditional cooperation based on observable attributes can reduce overall efficiency, especially when players use imperfect signals to predict others' behavior. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling mechanisms that allow players to condition on opponent characteristics (e.g., through history, reputation, or observable traits) may decrease efficiency relative to baseline, particularly if the environment is not anonymous and players can stereotype. The findings are most relevant for games with binary (all-or-nothing) choices, no explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and high stakes. The results caution that learning and conditional strategies do not always improve efficiency and may exacerbate payoff inequality.

- source: Bernard, M., Fanning, J., & Yuksel, S. (2018). Finding cooperators: Sorting through repeated interaction. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that in the Baseline (replacement allowed), cooperation (measured by responder return) increases over time, surpassing the Fixed Partner treatment (no replacement) in later rounds, despite similar initial levels. The Contract Restrictions treatment (no replacement, no punishment) starts with lower cooperation but converges to the Fixed Partner level in later rounds. The ability to punish by reducing transfers does not significantly increase cooperation compared to the inability to punish. Instead, the ability to sort (replace uncooperative partners) is the key driver of higher long-run cooperation. Surplus sharing becomes more equal in long-term relationships, but overall efficiency is not maximized in any treatment. The findings suggest that sorting, not punishment, is the main mechanism for sustaining cooperation in this environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated gift-exchange games (adjacent to PGGs), the ability to punish (reduce transfers) does not significantly increase efficiency or cooperation compared to when punishment is not possible. Instead, the ability to sort (replace partners) is more important for sustaining cooperation and improving surplus. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment alone (without sorting/replacement) may not increase efficiency, and that the structure of partner matching is a critical moderator. The findings are most relevant for environments where partner selection or replacement is possible, and less so for standard PGGs with fixed groups and explicit costly punishment. The paper's results should be used to caution against overestimating the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games without sorting opportunities.

- source: Nakashima, N. A., Halali, E., & Halevy, N. (2017). Third parties promote cooperative norms in repeated interactions. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Third party intervention in repeated adversarial interactions (modeled as a Peacemaker Game) increases cooperation rates among adversaries. Early intervention is more effective at establishing persistent cooperative norms than late intervention. Third parties are more likely to intervene when adversaries have a history of cooperation, and adversaries are more likely to cooperate following third party intervention. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation and intervention rates), not efficiency or group payoff. The study provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which combine reward and punishment can transform competitive repeated interactions into more cooperative ones, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that enabling third-party interventions (which combine reward and punishment) can increase cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like settings. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation and intervention rates, not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as suggestive rather than directly predictive for efficiency outcomes. The game structure is adjacent to public goods games, and the intervention mechanism is not a standard peer punishment or reward system. The results suggest that early, consistent intervention is more effective at establishing cooperation than late intervention, and that the presence of a third party who can both reward and punish can transform group behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that enabling such mechanisms is likely to increase cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency is not directly reported.

- source: Halevy, N., & Halali, E. (2015). Selfish third parties act as peacemakers by transforming conflicts and promoting cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main findings are: (1) The possibility and higher probability of third-party intervention increase cooperation rates among disputants in both interpersonal and intergroup contexts. (2) Third parties' willingness to intervene is highly sensitive to the risk and potential rewards of intervention; reducing risk or increasing potential gain increases intervention rates. (3) Despite the potential for increased collective gains, third parties often intervene less frequently than would be optimal, leading to missed opportunities for higher group payoffs. (4) The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as primary outcomes, but discusses them in the context of missed opportunities and the structure of the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the introduction of a punishment/reward mechanism (via third-party intervention) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma-like settings, even when the underlying game is not a standard public goods game. However, because the primary outcomes are behavioral (cooperation/intervention rates) and not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that the design of punishment/reward mechanisms (including the risk and incentive structure for enforcers) is critical for their effectiveness. The evidence is most relevant for adjacent settings where third-party intervention is possible, but less directly transferable to standard PGGs with endogenous peer punishment.

- source: Cason, T. N., & Mui, V. L. (2014). Coordinating Resistance Through Communication and Repeated Interaction. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that repetition alone only modestly reduces leader DAC (transgression) rates, as leaders can punish beneficiaries who resist, deterring future resistance. Communication between responders, even when non-binding and private, substantially increases the rate of coordinated resistance and reduces leader DAC. The combination of repetition and communication is most effective at reducing DAC. The study also shows that hiding responder identities from the leader (so the leader cannot target challenging beneficiaries) increases resistance. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total surplus, or group payoff as a main outcome, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The results are most relevant for understanding how communication and repeated interaction affect coordination in social dilemmas with endogenous roles and targeted punishment, rather than standard public goods games with peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the importance of communication and repeated interaction for coordinated resistance in social dilemmas with endogenous roles. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that communication between potential punishers (or resisters) can be as important as, or more important than, repetition for deterring exploitative behavior. The results highlight that the ability to target punishers (e.g., identifying who punished) can undermine the effectiveness of resistance. For prediction tasks in public goods games, the main lesson is that communication channels and anonymity of punishment/reward can strongly moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, efficiency. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes, but it does suggest that communication is a powerful moderator.

- source: Garrett, J. B., Livingston, J. A., Tayler, W. B., Cade, N. L., & McVay, S. E. (2019). Controls and Cooperation in Interactive and Non-Interactive Settings. *Contemporary Accounting Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a control (audit with bonus) in a repeated prisoner's dilemma increases subsequent cooperative behavior (measured as investment in a trust game) for both participants who directly experienced the control and those who only observed it, but the effect is significantly larger for those who directly benefited from cooperation. The effect is mediated by observed cooperation in the initial game. The results suggest that controls can foster trust and reciprocity, and that these effects are stronger in interactive settings. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the main outcome is behavioral (investment/cooperation), not payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that control mechanisms (akin to monitoring or incentive-based controls) can increase cooperative behavior in subsequent social dilemmas, especially in interactive settings. However, because the main outcome is investment in a trust game (a behavioral measure) and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not involve explicit punishment or sanctions, but rather a positive incentive (bonus for high contribution). The results suggest that mechanisms increasing observed cooperation can have positive spillovers on future cooperation, but do not directly inform the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Burum, B., Nowak, M. A., & Hoffman, M. (2020). An evolutionary explanation for ineffective altruism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that people are generally insensitive to the efficacy (impact) of their charitable giving, in contrast to their sensitivity when making decisions for themselves or family. Social rewards (reputation, praise, monetary reward from third parties) are given for the act of giving, not for giving effectively or giving more. This insensitivity is not due to cognitive or emotional limitations, but rather to the evolutionary logic that social rewards are easier to coordinate around observable, well-defined actions (like giving) than around less observable or harder-to-define features (like efficacy or amount). The studies do not use public goods games or punishment, but the reward mechanism is conceptually adjacent to sanctioning. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers indirect evidence that social incentives (rewards) are most effective when they are based on observable, well-defined actions, and that people are not motivated by efficiency or impact when giving is socially rewarded. The findings suggest that, in environments where social rewards or punishments are based on easily observed behaviors (like contributing at all), these incentives may not improve efficiency if they do not target effective or high-impact contributions. The lack of sensitivity to efficacy in social rewards may limit the effectiveness of punishment or reward mechanisms in increasing group efficiency, unless the mechanisms are designed to be sensitive to impact. The paper is most relevant for understanding the limitations of social incentives in promoting efficient cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Wu, J. H., Balliet, D., Kou, Y., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2019). Gossip in the Dictator and Ultimatum Games: Its Immediate and Downstream Consequences for Cooperation. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the possibility of gossip (reputation transmission) increases generosity in both dictator and ultimatum games. However, strategic motivation (fear of rejection in the ultimatum game) does not significantly increase generosity compared to the dictator game. In subsequent trust games, those who previously played the ultimatum game are less likely to reciprocate trust, especially when gossip is present. The effects are measured in terms of individual behavioral outcomes (generosity, trust, reciprocity), not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that reputation systems (gossip) can promote cooperation, but strategic motivations may undermine future cooperation, particularly when reputation is at stake.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers adjacent evidence that reputation mechanisms (gossip) can increase cooperative behavior in dyadic allocation games, and that strategic motivations (as in the ultimatum game) may not robustly increase cooperation and can even reduce reciprocity in subsequent interactions. Since the design lacks group-level interaction, explicit punishment, and efficiency outcomes, its direct predictive value for the target task is limited. It may inform the prediction task by analogy: reputation-based incentives can substitute for punishment in promoting cooperation, but strategic extrinsic incentives may have negative downstream effects on intrinsic cooperation.

- source: Samu, F., & Takács, K. (2021). Evaluating mechanisms that could support credible reputations and cooperation: cross-checking and social bonding. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment found that allowing for cross-checking of gossip or opportunities for social bonding did not significantly improve cooperation rates compared to a control. High competition for reputation increased cooperation, but overall cooperation declined over time in all conditions. Gossip was generally honest but became more negative about rivals under high competition. Reputation scores and gossip valence influenced conditional cooperation, but the mechanisms tested did not create a reliable path to sustained high cooperation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of communication, reputation, and competition on cooperation in repeated dyadic games, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not include explicit costly punishment or group-level payoff analysis. Findings suggest that mechanisms to improve the credibility of social information (cross-checking, social bonding) may not be sufficient to sustain cooperation, and that competition for reputation can increase cooperation rates. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper offers only contextual or indirect guidance.

- source: Bednarik, P., Fehl, K., & Semmann, D. (2014). Costs for switching partners reduce network dynamics but not cooperative behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing costs for forming new links in dynamic networks reduces the rate at which participants break links (i.e., switch partners). However, cooperation rates in the repeated Prisoner's Dilemma remain high across all dynamic treatments, regardless of the cost level, and are significantly higher than in static networks where no partner switching is allowed. The ability to break links—even if rarely used—appears to be enough to maintain high cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of these dynamics on efficiency cannot be directly determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with dynamic partner networks, the introduction of costs for switching partners reduces network dynamism but does not reduce cooperation rates. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment or partner-switching mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings suggest that the mere threat of partner switching can sustain cooperation, but the efficiency implications (e.g., total group payoff relative to maximum possible) remain unaddressed.

- source: Harth, N. S., & Regner, T. (2017). The spiral of distrust: (Non-)cooperation in a repeated trust game is predicted by anger and individual differences in negative reciprocity orientation. *International Journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that senders who receive feedback about low back transfers from trustees experience increased anger, which in turn leads to reduced cooperation (lower amounts sent) in subsequent rounds. Individuals with a high negative reciprocity orientation are more prone to anger and are less willing to cooperate, even in the absence of anger-eliciting feedback. The interaction between anger and negative reciprocity orientation further reduces cooperation. The absence of feedback results in lower anger and relatively higher cooperation, especially among those low in negative reciprocity orientation. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it studies a repeated trust game without any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (amount sent/cooperation) and emotional (anger), not payoff-based. However, it provides indirect evidence that negative emotions and individual differences in reciprocity orientation can reduce cooperation in repeated interactions, especially when feedback about others' behavior is available. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only weakly informative and should not be weighted heavily unless the prediction context is specifically about trust games or the psychological mechanisms of cooperation.

- source: Andersson, O., & Wengström, E. (2012). Credible communication and cooperation: Experimental evidence from multi-stage Games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that allowing only pre-play communication leads to higher cooperation rates in the first stage (prisoner's dilemma) than allowing both pre-play and intra-play communication. Sending or receiving messages that signal cooperative intentions has a strong positive effect on cooperation when only pre-play communication is possible, but this effect is significantly weakened when intra-play communication is allowed. The results support the theoretical prediction that renegotiation opportunities (via intra-play communication) undermine the credibility of punishment threats and thus reduce the effectiveness of communication in sustaining cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its findings are most relevant for predicting behavioral (not payoff) effects of communication structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effect of communication structure (timing and frequency) on cooperation in multi-stage games with punishment/reward strategies, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that communication structure can moderate the effectiveness of punishment/reward threats in sustaining cooperation, which may indirectly affect efficiency in similar environments. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a prisoner's dilemma plus coordination game, and punishment is implemented via equilibrium threats rather than explicit costly punishment. The main actionable insight is that more communication opportunities (especially intra-play) can reduce the effectiveness of pre-play communication in sustaining cooperation, likely due to renegotiation.

- source: Zhen, S. S., & Yu, R. J. (2016). Tend to Compare and Tend to Be Fair: The Relationship between Social Comparison Sensitivity and Justice Sensitivity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds a positive correlation between social comparison sensitivity and justice sensitivity, both in behavioral tasks (UG and SCG) and in self-report inventories. In the UG, participants are more likely to reject disadvantageous offers, and this tendency is associated with their behavior in the SCG. The results suggest that social comparison motives underlie fairness-related decisions, particularly the rejection of unfair offers. However, the study does not include a public goods game, does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a separate stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses a modified ultimatum game (dyadic, proposer-responder) and a social comparison game, focusing on individual differences in fairness and comparison sensitivity. There is no group interaction, no punishment stage as in PGGs, and no efficiency or group payoff data. The findings may be relevant for understanding motives behind rejection (which can be interpreted as a form of costly punishment), but the lack of group-level payoff outcomes and absence of a PGG or punishment-enabled/control design means this paper offers only indirect, limited support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Mancini, A., Betti, V., Panasiti, M. S., Pavone, E. F., & Aglioti, S. M. (2011). Suffering Makes You Egoist: Acute Pain Increases Acceptance Rates and Reduces Fairness during a Bilateral Ultimatum Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that acute pain leads to higher acceptance rates of unfair offers and lower fairness in offers made, suggesting that pain induces more self-interested behavior in both roles of the Ultimatum Game. The effect is specific to pain (not just heat) and is most pronounced for moderately unfair offers. The intensity of pain predicts acceptance rates for these offers. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses the Ultimatum Game, which is adjacent but not equivalent to PGGs, and focuses on the effect of pain (a state manipulation) rather than institutional punishment. The outcomes are behavioral (acceptance, offer size) rather than payoff-based. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games is limited.

- source: Townsend, C., Aktipis, A., Balliet, D., & Cronk, L. (2020). Generosity among the Ik of Uganda. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that Ik participants in unframed Dictator Games give on average 23.3% of their endowment, which is within the expected range for similar societies. Framing the game to invoke both need and supernatural punishment significantly increases giving (to 43.2%), while either frame alone does not have a significant effect. The results challenge the claim that the Ik are uniquely selfish and show that cultural norms and beliefs can increase generosity. However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not include punishment as a costly sanctioning option, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is a Dictator Game, not a PGG or its variant, and the 'punishment' manipulation is a framing invoking supernatural punishment, not a real, costly, player-administered sanction. The outcome is individual giving, not group efficiency. The findings may be weakly informative for understanding how cultural beliefs about punishment can increase generosity, but they do not provide evidence about the efficiency effects of actual punishment mechanisms in PGG-like environments.

- source: Jones, M. T. (2014). Strategic complexity and cooperation: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Increasing the complexity of strategies required to sustain cooperation in an indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma reduces the frequency of cooperation and increases the prevalence of simple selfish strategies. This effect is observed both when the cooperate action and when the defect action are made state-dependent, increasing the information processing required for cooperation or punishment, respectively. The study also finds that higher cognitive ability is associated with greater cooperation in the baseline, but this advantage is reduced when complexity is increased. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral measures such as cooperation rates and strategy inference.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that increasing the cognitive or strategic complexity of sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas reduces cooperation rates, and by implication, would likely reduce efficiency or group payoff if measured. However, the study is not a public goods game and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on endogenous punishment via repeated game strategies (e.g., Grim Trigger). The findings are most relevant for predicting how complexity or cognitive burden might moderate the effect of punishment or cooperation-supporting mechanisms in repeated games, but do not provide direct evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games. Use this paper as informative indirect evidence about the role of complexity and cognitive ability in sustaining cooperation, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Bigoni, M., Potters, J., & Spagnolo, G. (2019). Frequency of interaction, communication and collusion: an experiment. *Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that, without communication, cooperation (collusion) rates are low and not significantly affected by the frequency of interaction (flexibility). When pre-play communication is allowed, cooperation rates increase dramatically across all treatments, and the non-monotonic effect of flexibility predicted by theory (higher cooperation at intermediate flexibility) becomes more apparent, but is still secondary to the effect of communication. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and there is no explicit punishment mechanism; instead, punishment is implemented via Nash reversion (endogenous). The findings suggest that communication is a much stronger facilitator of cooperation than flexibility in this setting with imperfect monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that communication (chat) has a very strong positive effect on cooperation rates in a repeated game with imperfect monitoring, even in the absence of explicit punishment. However, because the game is a Cournot duopoly (not a public goods game), there is no explicit punishment or reward mechanism, and efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The main implication is that enabling communication can greatly increase cooperation, but the effect of flexibility (frequency of interaction) is non-monotonic and only emerges when communication is present. The results do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but suggest that communication is a key moderator.

- source: Brevers, D., Noël, X., Ermer, E., Dabiri, D., Verbanck, P., & Kornreich, C. (2013). Unfairness sensitivity and social decision-making in individuals with alcohol dependence: A preliminary study. *Drug and Alcohol Dependence*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Alcohol-dependent individuals are more likely to reject unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game compared to healthy controls, suggesting greater emotional reactivity to unfairness and reduced ability to make self-advantageous decisions in social contexts involving frustration. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and the design does not include a punishment phase or a repeated public goods structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The Ultimatum Game is adjacent to the target environment, as rejection of unfair offers can be interpreted as a form of costly punishment, but the context is one-shot, dyadic, and does not involve group cooperation or repeated interactions. The findings may inform understanding of individual differences in punishment sensitivity, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or the effect of punishment mechanisms in public goods games.

- source: Frey, V., & de Rijt, A. V. (2016). Arbitrary Inequality in Reputation Systems. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when trustors can observe the outcomes of others' exchanges (information sharing), they are more likely to repeatedly select the same trustee, leading to 'reputation cascades' and increasing inequality in how often trustees are chosen. This effect is strongest when there is a risk of trust abuse (the 'trust problem' condition). The paper shows that these cascades are arbitrary, as trustees with similar trustworthiness can end up with very different exchange volumes. While the paper discusses payoffs in the game structure, it does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The main focus is on behavioral dynamics of trust and reputation, not on efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of information sharing and reputation systems on behavioral outcomes in trust-based exchange games. It does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods or punishment games, as it does not report these outcomes. However, it suggests that increased information sharing can increase trust and exchange, but also leads to arbitrary inequality in participation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as the game structure and outcomes differ from standard PGGs and do not include punishment or efficiency as primary outcomes.

- source: Mellers, B. A., Haselhuhn, M. P., Tetlock, P. E., Silva, J. C., & Isen, A. M. (2010). Predicting Behavior in Economic Games by Looking Through the Eyes of the Players. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main findings are: (1) Players' self-defined want and ought motives predict their offers in ultimatum and dictator games; (2) in the ultimatum game, more players act according to what they believe is fair (ought), while in the dictator game, want and ought are equally influential; (3) positive mood induction increases the likelihood of making equal offers in the dictator game; (4) when given an exit option, players experiencing greater conflict between want and ought are more likely to exit. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment in public goods or repeated games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment institutions, marginal per-capita return, or group efficiency. Its findings on rejections in the ultimatum game and exit behavior in the dictator game are only loosely related to the prediction task. The main value is in highlighting the psychological mechanisms (want/ought selves) that may underlie cooperation and sanctioning, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions.

- source: Eilam, O., & Suleiman, R. (2004). Cooperative, pure, and selfish trusting: Their distinctive effects on the reaction of trust recipients. *European Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that trust recipients allocate significantly less to trust givers who express selfish expectations than to those who express cooperative or no expectations. There is no significant difference between the cooperative and pure trust conditions. All allocations to the trust giver are above the minimum (preservation point) and below the equality point. The results are interpreted as evidence that explicit selfish expectations activate self-interest and reactance, leading to less cooperative responses, while cooperative or unstated expectations elicit more equal allocations. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it implement punishment as a game feature.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot allocation game with no repeated interaction, no group structure, and no explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism. While the discussion references social sanctioning and norm enforcement, these are psychological constructs rather than implemented game features. The findings may be weakly informative for understanding how communicated expectations affect allocation behavior, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Wang, X. J., Chen, Z. S., Krumhuber, E. G., & Chen, H. (2021). Money and flexible generosity. *British Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three studies, the authors find that using money as the currency in DG/UG games reduces generosity compared to candy or food, but only when there are no sanctions (DG) or reputation concerns (private condition). When punishment is possible (UG) or when allocations are public (reputation at stake), the negative effect of money on generosity is reduced or eliminated. Imbuing money with prosocial meaning (charity) also attenuates the effect. The main outcome is generosity rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the presence of sanctions or reputation mechanisms can offset the self-serving effects of money on prosocial behavior, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of punishment or reputation mechanisms can mitigate the negative effect of monetary incentives on prosocial behavior, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game structure. The results are most relevant for predicting how punishment or reputation might moderate the effect of monetary incentives on generosity or cooperation, but less directly informative for predicting efficiency changes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that punishment/reputation mechanisms may help maintain prosocial behavior even in monetary contexts, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: Azevedo, R. T., Panasiti, M. S., Maglio, R., & Aglioti, S. M. (2018). Perceived warmth and competence of others shape voluntary deceptive behaviour in a morally relevant setting. *British Journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the perceived warmth and competence of the target modulate the likelihood of lying for self-gain: participants lie less to high-warmth individuals and more to high-competence individuals. The risk of disclosure (reputation risk) reduces lying, particularly toward low-warmth/low-competence targets. These effects are interpreted as reflecting malleable personal moral standards and social evaluation processes. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the game is not a public goods game or direct variant. The outcomes are behavioral (lying frequency) rather than payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how social context, reputation risk, and target characteristics can modulate dishonest behavior in a zero-sum, two-player setting. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The manipulation of reputation risk is conceptually adjacent to punishment/sanction mechanisms, but there is no explicit punishment, and the outcome is not efficiency. Thus, the paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it may inform understanding of how social evaluation and disclosure risk affect norm-violating behavior.

- source: Chen, M. K., & Hauser, M. (2005). Modeling reciprocation and cooperation in primates: evidence for a punishing strategy. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that tamarins' cooperative behavior in repeated food-exchange games is best described by 'punish-2' and 'punish-3' strategies, which are more punishing than standard tit-for-tat. These strategies require two or more consecutive cooperative acts to restart cooperation after a breakdown, leading to stable but sometimes brittle cooperation. The analysis uses maximum-likelihood fitting of a suite of behavioral models, showing that these punishing strategies account for over 90% of the posterior model weight. The paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, and all results are based on behavioral patterns (pulling rates and model fits).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. However, it does provide indirect evidence that more punishing strategies (requiring multiple acts of cooperation to restore cooperation) can stabilize cooperation in repeated dyadic interactions. The findings may inform mechanistic expectations about how punishment-like strategies affect cooperation dynamics, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs. The game is not a standard PGG, and the outcomes are not payoff-based.

- source: Cruz, T. D. D., Beersma, B., Dijkstra, M. T. M., & Bechtoldt, M. N. (2019). The Bright and Dark Side of Gossip for Cooperation in Groups. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The studies find that (1) people are more likely to gossip to protect group members from norm violators, but also gossip to vent emotions; (2) being the target of true negative gossip increases short-term intended work effort, but being the target of gossip (true or false) reduces long-term cooperative intentions toward the gossipers; (3) false gossip does not increase short-term cooperation. The results highlight both a 'bright side' (short-term compliance) and a 'dark side' (long-term alienation) of gossip as a social sanction. However, no actual payoff, group earnings, or efficiency measures are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It does, however, suggest that informal social sanctions like gossip can increase short-term compliance (analogous to increased contribution) but may undermine long-term cooperation (analogous to group cohesion or repeated cooperation). The findings are relevant for understanding the psychological and motivational effects of informal sanctions, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, punishment cost) are not systematically manipulated or reported in a way that supports prediction for the target task.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2020). The role of third parties in norm enforcement in customary courts among the Enga of Papua New Guinea. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study documents that in Enga customary courts, norm violations are addressed primarily through restorative justice: compensation to victims, reintegration of wrongdoers, and broad third-party participation in settlements. Punitive sanctions (punishment) are rare and not institutionally emphasized. Third parties contribute to compensation both during and outside court hearings, motivated by kinship, reputation, and community harmony. Repeated offenders and free riders receive less support over time. The system is designed to maintain cooperation and social cohesion, not to maximize punishment or deterrence. The paper argues that such restorative systems are more effective for sustaining cooperation in small-scale societies than third-party punishment, but does not provide quantitative efficiency or payoff data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides contextual and ethnographic evidence that, in small-scale societies like the Enga, restorative justice and third-party compensation are preferred over punitive sanctions for norm enforcement. While it does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that punishment is not the primary or most effective means of sustaining cooperation in such environments. For prediction tasks involving public goods games or efficiency outcomes, this paper is mainly informative for understanding the limits of punishment's effectiveness and the importance of alternative, restorative mechanisms. It does not provide direct evidence for how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games or similar experimental settings.

- source: Simpson, B., Harrell, A., & Willer, R. (2013). Hidden Paths from Morality to Cooperation: Moral Judgments Promote Trust and Trustworthiness. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three experiments, the authors show that (1) making moral judgments of others' immoral behavior increases subsequent trustworthiness in a trust game (measured by amount returned), (2) this effect is mediated by increased self-perceived moral identity, and (3) observers trust and prefer to interact with strong moral judges, perceiving them as more moral and trustworthy. The behavioral outcomes are individual-level trust and trustworthiness, not group-level efficiency or payoff. The design does not include public goods games, explicit punishment, or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are adjacent: it shows that making moral judgments (not material punishment) can increase trustworthiness in trust games, and that observers trust moral judges more. However, there is no manipulation of punishment, no public goods game, and no group payoff or efficiency outcome. The results may be relevant for understanding how non-material social judgments can affect prosocial behavior, but they do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Ge, E. H., Chen, Y., Wu, J. J., & Mace, R. (2019). Large-scale cooperation driven by reputation, not fear of divine punishment. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that cooperation (measured as donations) is higher in small, stable communities and among those with more public religious practice, but not among those with stronger belief in divine punishment. When reputational concerns are removed (private dice game), neither community size nor religiosity predicts honesty. The results suggest that reputation, not fear of supernatural punishment, drives large-scale cooperation in these settings. No evidence is provided on the effects of punishment or sanctions, nor on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental design does not include punishment, sanctions, or group payoff/efficiency measures. Its findings are only indirectly relevant: they suggest that reputational mechanisms (public observability, community stability) can increase prosocial behavior, but do not address the impact of punishment or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task, except as very weak contextual support for the importance of reputation versus punishment.

- source: Adam, T. C. (2010). Competition encourages cooperation: client fish receive higher-quality service when cleaner fish compete. *Animal Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Clients (ornate butterflyfish) with access to multiple cleaner fish stations receive higher-quality service from cleaners: they are cleaned for longer per event, are less likely to be ignored, and have fewer interactions terminated by the cleaner. However, the frequency of cheating by cleaners does not decrease with increased partner choice. Aggressive punishment (chasing) by clients in response to cheating is common and does not differ significantly between clients with and without partner choice. The study supports the role of partner choice (competition among cleaners) in promoting cooperative service, but suggests that punishment is used as a fixed strategy rather than being contingent on partner choice. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task: it shows that increased partner choice (analogous to increased competition or exit options in PGG-like games) leads to higher-quality cooperative behavior (service) from the provider, but does not reduce cheating frequency. Punishment (aggression) is used regardless of partner choice. However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not manipulate punishment costs or technology, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Thus, while the behavioral mechanisms (partner choice, punishment) are relevant, the findings cannot be directly mapped to predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs. The main value is in supporting the importance of partner choice and the robustness of punishment as a behavioral response.

- source: Artavia-Mora, L., Bedi, A. S., & Rieger, M. (2017). Intuitive help and punishment in the field. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that time delay (deliberation) reduces the likelihood of helping strangers and norm violators in the field, with helping rates dropping by 18-27 percentage points depending on the scenario. However, time delay does not significantly affect the rate of direct punishment of norm violators. The study also finds that time delay increases self-reported fear of retaliation for punishing. The design is a one-shot, non-strategic, individual-level field experiment, not a public goods game or its direct variant, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar strategic environments. Its design lacks group interaction, repeated rounds, contribution choices, or payoff structures analogous to PGGs. The main outcomes are binary helping or punishment actions in response to norm violations, not group payoffs or efficiency. While the findings may be contextually informative about the psychological underpinnings of prosocial and punitive behavior, they do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. The study is best used as contextual or mechanistic background, not as direct input for efficiency prediction models.

- source: Kaltwasser, L., Hildebrandt, A., Wilhelm, O., & Sommer, W. (2016). Behavioral and neuronal determinants of negative reciprocity in the ultimatum game. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both prosociality (as measured by social value orientation) and assertiveness predict the likelihood of rejecting unfair offers in the ultimatum game, supporting both the strong reciprocity and status defense models of negative reciprocity. Neural data (feedback negativity) show that individuals with higher negative reciprocity have stronger neural responses to proposer fairness. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not manipulate punishment as a treatment variable. The findings are limited to the behavioral and neural correlates of negative reciprocity in the UG, not to efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or similar games.
